i [SOURCE: Page_3]

Abstract (in English) Historically, genetic selection for greater milk production in dairy cows has worked well when [SOURCE: Page_4]

environmental conditions are favorable to gene expression, typically unlimited access to a high [SOURCE: Page_4]

quality feed and water, neutral ambient temperature, and low pathogen load. The environment [SOURCE: Page_4]

in dairy production, especially in grazing systems, is, however, increasingly variable due to [SOURCE: Page_4]

climate change and societal demand for a more sustainable agriculture. Different breeding and [SOURCE: Page_4]

feeding strategies might thus be chosen by farmers according to their local conditions. Selecting [SOURCE: Page_4]

animals that adapt well to future herd environments is important and it requires an ability to [SOURCE: Page_4]

predict how animals respond to a change in the environment in which they are placed, i.e. how [SOURCE: Page_4]

they partition obtained nutrients between life functions when resources are limiting. This implies [SOURCE: Page_4]

describing their capability to cope, or their robustness. In this context, the extent to which [SOURCE: Page_4]

nutrients are partitioned to the vital functions (health and fertility) is clearly important. In this [SOURCE: Page_4]

thesis, an existing lifetime nutrient partitioning model was further developed to simulate lifetime [SOURCE: Page_4]

productive and reproductive performance and thereby allow prediction of lifetime efficiency of [SOURCE: Page_4]

individual cows across feeding systems. The model is individual-based and it has incorporated [SOURCE: Page_4]

the possible performance consequences of cows on their probability of reproducing. Generally, [SOURCE: Page_4]

the whole model consistently simulated productive and reproductive performance of different [SOURCE: Page_4]

genotypes of cow. We have also provided information on the range and distribution of genetic- [SOURCE: Page_4]

scaling parameters needed for the model. It was shown that cows selected for increased milk [SOURCE: Page_4]

production had an inferior fertility and shorter longevity compared to cows that produced less [SOURCE: Page_4]

milk. At the animal level, despite having a shorter lifespan and a lower reproductive [SOURCE: Page_4]

performance selected cows were still more efficient than non-selected cows in terms of energy [SOURCE: Page_4]

captured in milk over energy intake throughout the lifespan. This conclusion might be extended [SOURCE: Page_4]

to lifetime nitrogen efficiency since we found a positive correlation between energy and nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_4]

efficiency. At farm level, economic indicators such as costs and returns of heifer replacement, [SOURCE: Page_4]

veterinary treatment, artificial insemination, and environmental impact should be included to [SOURCE: Page_4]

fully assess the lifetime profitability of cows in difference scenarios. The societal concern on [SOURCE: Page_4]

animal welfare is also an essential issue. Simulations showed that cows fed low concentrate had [SOURCE: Page_4]

worse productive and reproductive performance, and lifetime efficiency than high fed ones. This [SOURCE: Page_4]

thesis has shown that modeling approach can be a useful tool for testing future selection and [SOURCE: Page_4]

management strategies, and it has developed some key elements needed to do this. [SOURCE: Page_4]

Keywords: dairy cow, nutrient partitioning, lifetime efficiency, modelling, reproduction [SOURCE: Page_4]

ii [SOURCE: Page_4]

Abstract (en français) Historiquement, la sélection génétique chez la vache sur la production laitière a été efficace, car [SOURCE: Page_5]

l’expression du potentiel génétique n’était pas limitée par les conditions d’élevage du fait d’un [SOURCE: Page_5]

accès non limité à une alimentation de qualité, de conditions thermiques favorables, et d’une [SOURCE: Page_5]

maitrise croissante des pathologies. Les conditions environnementales concernant la production [SOURCE: Page_5]

laitière sont désormais plus variables du fait du changement climatique et de la demande [SOURCE: Page_5]

sociétale pour un développement durable de la production agricole. Les éleveurs peuvent donc [SOURCE: Page_5]

sélectionner différentes races et différentes stratégies alimentaires adaptées à leurs conditions [SOURCE: Page_5]

locales. Sélectionner des animaux adaptés à leur environnement nécessite de prédire leur réponse [SOURCE: Page_5]

aux changements de cet environnement, par exemple, comment la limitation des ressources [SOURCE: Page_5]

alimentaires peut affecter la partition des nutriments entre les différentes fonctions vitales. Ceci [SOURCE: Page_5]

implique de décrire et prédire leur capacité d’adaptation à ces situations, i.e. leur robustesse. [SOURCE: Page_5]

Dans ce contexte, la caractérisation de la partition des nutriments entre les fonctions de [SOURCE: Page_5]

production et les fonctions vitales (santé, fertilité…) est cruciale. Dans cette thèse, nous [SOURCE: Page_5]

développons un modèle préexistant de partition des nutriments durant la carrière de la vache [SOURCE: Page_5]

laitière pour modéliser ses fonctions de production et ses performances de reproduction afin de [SOURCE: Page_5]

prédire leur efficacité individuelle dans différents systèmes d’alimentation. Le modèle est [SOURCE: Page_5]

individu-centré, et il inclut la représentation des effets des performances de production sur la [SOURCE: Page_5]

probabilité de reproduction des vaches. Globalement, le modèle simule les performances de [SOURCE: Page_5]

production et de reproduction de vaches de différents génotypes de façon satisfaisante. Il permet [SOURCE: Page_5]

également de renseigner des informations sur l’amplitude et la distribution de paramètres [SOURCE: Page_5]

génétiques dérivés du modèle. Nos résultats montrent que les vaches sélectionnées pour la [SOURCE: Page_5]

production laitière sont moins fertiles et ont une longévité plus faible que celles non [SOURCE: Page_5]

sélectionnées. Néanmoins, ces vaches sélectionnées sont plus performantes durant l’ensemble de [SOURCE: Page_5]

leur carrière pour leur efficacité énergétique (énergie fixée dans le lait / énergie ingérée) que les [SOURCE: Page_5]

vaches faiblement productives. Cette conclusion peut être extrapolée à l’efficacité azotée du fait [SOURCE: Page_5]

de la corrélation positive entre efficacité énergétique et azotée que nous avons montrée. Au [SOURCE: Page_5]

niveau de l’exploitation, des indicateurs économiques comme le retour sur investissement des [SOURCE: Page_5]

génisses de remplacement, les coûts vétérinaires ou d’insémination, et l’impact environnemental [SOURCE: Page_5]

devront être inclus pour obtenir une évaluation complète de la rentabilité économique des vaches [SOURCE: Page_5]

dans ces différents scénarios. La demande sociétale concernant le bien-être animal doit aussi être [SOURCE: Page_5]

intégrée. Nos résultats montrent que les vaches restreintes en concentré ont de moins bonnes [SOURCE: Page_5]

iii [SOURCE: Page_5]

performances de production et de reproduction, et une moindre efficacité que celles alimentées [SOURCE: Page_6]

de façon plus libérale. Cette thèse montre que la modélisation peut constituer un outil pour tester [SOURCE: Page_6]

différentes stratégies de sélection et différents schéma de management du troupeau, et fournit [SOURCE: Page_6]

certains indicateurs pour ces objectifs. [SOURCE: Page_6]

Mots-clés: vache laitière, partition des nutriments, efficacité, carrière, modélisation, reproduction. [SOURCE: Page_6]

### 1.1.1. The challenges of an efficient dairy production

An increasing world human population, projected to reach 9.6 billion by 2050 (United [SOURCE: Page_14]

Nations Development Programme, 2009), and a greater demand on agricultural land for non- [SOURCE: Page_14]

agricultural activities imply that the dairy industry will face the challenges of meeting human [SOURCE: Page_14]

consumption, competing for grain and feed resources with other livestock industries, and biofuel [SOURCE: Page_14]

production (Berry, 2009; Gonzalez-Recio et al., 2014). At the same time, the increase of consumer [SOURCE: Page_14]

and public concern on the environmental impact of milk production has established pressures on [SOURCE: Page_14]

milk producers to mitigate greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions from their farms. The dairy sector, [SOURCE: Page_14]

specifically, contributes around 4% CO$_2$-equivalent to the global anthropogenic GHG emissions [SOURCE: Page_14]

(FAO, 2010), including mainly methane (CH$_4$), nitrous oxide (N2O) and carbon dioxide (CO$_2$). In [SOURCE: Page_14]

this context, improved feed efficiency (FE) of dairy cows, which is commonly defined as the ratio [SOURCE: Page_14]

between milk output and feed intake, is of clear importance for the dairy industry to meet the [SOURCE: Page_14]

challenges of future consumption requirements and sustainable farming objectives. Furthermore, [SOURCE: Page_14]

given that feed is by far the largest expense in the total variable costs of milk production, e.g. [SOURCE: Page_14]

more than 50% as estimated by Shalloo et al. (2004), any improvement of FE would have a [SOURCE: Page_14]

substantial influence on the profitability of dairy farms (Britt et al., 2003). Cabrera (2009), using a [SOURCE: Page_14]

milk price of 0.26$/kg and a total mixed ration (TMR) cost of 0.22$/kg of dry matter intake, [SOURCE: Page_14]

indicated that income increased approximately 5$/cow/day when feed efficiency (kg milk/ kg dry [SOURCE: Page_14]

matter intake) increased from 1.1 to 1.9. The recognition of the importance of FE in dairy industry [SOURCE: Page_14]

has led to a global effort to improve this trait (for example: Berry, 2009; Hayes et al., 2013; [SOURCE: Page_14]

Gonzalez-Recio et al., 2014; Pryce et al., 2014). [SOURCE: Page_14]

### 1.1.2. Increasing efficiency through selection on milk yield

So far, the improved FE in dairy cows has mainly been achieved indirectly through increased [SOURCE: Page_15]

milk production (Hietala et al., 2014), by improving genetics, nutrition, and management (Wall et [SOURCE: Page_15]

al., 2010; Connor et al., 2011). The strong genetic correlation between milk yield and FE (r = 0.79, [SOURCE: Page_15]

Oldenbroek, 1989) indicates that the selection for increased milk yield alone should also improve [SOURCE: Page_15]

feed efficiency without the requirement of measuring individual animal feed intake. With a [SOURCE: Page_15]

greater yield per cow, fewer cows are needed to obtain the same level of milk output, and [SOURCE: Page_15]

consequently reducing total feed requirement, land use, manure production, and GHG [SOURCE: Page_15]

emissions. Modern high-producing dairy cows can produce approximately 40 to 50 kg of milk [SOURCE: Page_15]

per day in early lactation, and the production of as high as 60kg/d is not uncommon (VandeHaar [SOURCE: Page_15]

and St-Pierre, 2006). Such an enormous increase in milk yield has been attributed to [SOURCE: Page_15]

approximately 50% of improved genetics and 50% of improved feeding and management (Pryce [SOURCE: Page_15]

and Veerkamp, 2001; Pryce et al., 2004). Certainly, genetics has played an important role in the [SOURCE: Page_15]

increase in milk production during the last decades, but proper nutrition and management are [SOURCE: Page_15]

compulsory to enable high-producing cows to attain their genetic merits. [SOURCE: Page_15]

Figure 1.1. Level of production and energy flow. As a cow eats more feed and produces more milk, a [SOURCE: Page_15]

smaller proportion of energy intake is used for maintenance [Source: VandeHaar and St-Pierre (2006)]. [SOURCE: Page_15]

2 [SOURCE: Page_15]

Chapter 1 - Introduction [SOURCE: Page_16]

The biological process underlying this improved production efficiency is known as the [SOURCE: Page_16]

“dilution of maintenance” effect (Figure 1.1) occurring with increased energy intake and milk [SOURCE: Page_16]

production, i.e. when a cow eats more feed to support milk production a smaller proportion of [SOURCE: Page_16]

that feed is used for maintenance, and it is thus more efficient (Bauman et al., 1985; VandeHaar [SOURCE: Page_16]

and St-Pierre, 2006). An intuitive example was given by VandeHaar and St-Pierre (2006): a cow of [SOURCE: Page_16]

625 kg has a maintenance requirement of approximately 10 Mcal of net energy for lactation (NE L) [SOURCE: Page_16]

per day which is assumed to be constant; if she consumes twice as much feed intake compared to [SOURCE: Page_16]

maintenance (20 Mcal of NEL/d), then 10 Mcal or 50% is captured in milk and 50% is for [SOURCE: Page_16]

maintenance; if she consumes three times the maintenance intake, then 67% of feed intake is [SOURCE: Page_16]

captured in milk while only 23% is for maintenance. [SOURCE: Page_16]

### 1.1.3. Limitations of selection focused on milk yield and towards selection for lifetime

efficiency [SOURCE: Page_16]

The benefits of increased FE in terms of milk production have, however, not been [SOURCE: Page_16]

straightforward. The single-trait selection for greater milk production has been shown to be [SOURCE: Page_16]

associated with the significant declines in health, fertility, and consequently productive longevity [SOURCE: Page_16]

of dairy cows (Royal et al., 2000; Pryce et al., 2004; Dillon et al., 2006). The reported consequences [SOURCE: Page_16]

generally include delayed resumption of postpartum estrus cycling, increased incidence of [SOURCE: Page_16]

abnormal cyclicity, less expression of estrus, and poor conception rate (Butler, 2000; Kerbrat and [SOURCE: Page_16]

Disenhaus, 2004; Dobson et al., 2008) (see Table 1.1 for examples); increased prevalence of [SOURCE: Page_16]

metabolic diseases such as ketosis, metritis or fatty liver (Gillund et al., 2001; Giuliodori et al., [SOURCE: Page_16]

2013), and mastitis (Lassen et al., 2003). These resulting problems of selection for higher milk [SOURCE: Page_16]

production do not only impair the benefits of improved feed efficiency through, for example, [SOURCE: Page_16]

increases in veterinary and reproductive costs, and environmental losses, they also impact on [SOURCE: Page_16]

productive longevity through increased involuntary culling for reproductive failure and health [SOURCE: Page_16]

problems. The lower longevity would reduce lifetime efficiency because the shorter the [SOURCE: Page_16]

productive life, the longer is the rearing (non-productive) phase as a proportion of the lifespan, [SOURCE: Page_16]

and thus the lower the lifetime efficiency. Animal welfare is also affected (Oltenacu and Algers, [SOURCE: Page_16]

2005). A considerable body of research has, therefore, been conducted to elucidate the [SOURCE: Page_16]

mechanisms involved, and the different factors of the genotype-production-nutrition complex [SOURCE: Page_16]

that affect reproductive performance of animals [see reviews of Pryce et al. (2004), Lucy (2007) or [SOURCE: Page_16]

Friggens et al. (2010)]. [SOURCE: Page_16]

3 [SOURCE: Page_16]

Chapter 1 - Introduction [SOURCE: Page_17]

A general explanation for the declines in fertility and health is based on the arguments of [SOURCE: Page_17]

nutrient prioritization: postpartum high-producing cows prioritize to channel their obtained [SOURCE: Page_17]

nutrients towards milk production, and then body condition, and only after these immediate [SOURCE: Page_17]

needs are met the reproductive processes are resumed (Leroy et al., 2008b). This prioritization [SOURCE: Page_17]

creates a nutrient highway from the digestive tract and body reserves to the udder (Lucy, 2001). [SOURCE: Page_17]

There is a decrease followed by a replenishment of body fatness in early and late lactation. In this [SOURCE: Page_17]

context, if feed efficiency is considered over the lifespan of the animal then, the extent to which [SOURCE: Page_17]

nutrients are partitioned to the vital functions such as health and fertility is clearly important. [SOURCE: Page_17]

Leroy et al. (2008a), however, pointed out that the energy needed to develop, mature, and ovulate [SOURCE: Page_17]

a follicle, to form a corpus luteum, and to maintain early pregnancy is negligible in comparison to [SOURCE: Page_17]

energy required for milk production and maintenance. This suggests that there might be other [SOURCE: Page_17]

indirect indicators or physiological mechanisms reflecting the effects of such a nutrient [SOURCE: Page_17]

partitioning process on animal health and fertility. These indicators have been discussed in the [SOURCE: Page_17]

literature including milk yield, body fatness, and body fat mobilization (Lopez et al., 2004; Roche [SOURCE: Page_17]

et al., 2009; Yániz et al., 2008; Cutullic et al., 2012; Friggens et al., 2010). Table 1.1. The decline in fertility of Spanish dairy cattle in the period 1991 – 2007* [SOURCE: Page_17]

Time period                              1991 – 1995     1996 – 2000     2003 - 2007 [SOURCE: Page_17]

Inseminated cows (%)                          87.0           81.2            82.1 [SOURCE: Page_17]

Pregnancy at first AI (%)                     39.1           34.8            32.3 [SOURCE: Page_17]

Ovarian hypofunction (%)                      3.8            10.2            12.0 [SOURCE: Page_17]

Annual milk yield per cow (kg)                8300           9660            11221 [SOURCE: Page_17]

*   Data of Yániz et al. (2008). [SOURCE: Page_17]

The physiological mechanisms of the effects of milk yield, and body fat mobilization on [SOURCE: Page_17]

fertility have been investigated (Veerkamp et al., 2000; Butler, 2003; Garnsworthy et al., 2008; [SOURCE: Page_17]

Senatore et al., 2010), whereas the physiological linkage between body fatness and fertility is still [SOURCE: Page_17]

unclear although the evidence has been reported (Wright et al., 1992). However, the individual [SOURCE: Page_17]

effects of milk yield, body fatness, and body reserve mobilization are not easy to be differentiated [SOURCE: Page_17]

because of their inter-correlation; only a few authors have attempted to investigate this issue [SOURCE: Page_17]

(Wright et al., 1992; Cutullic et al., 2011). The metabolic disorders in postpartum dairy cows [SOURCE: Page_17]

relating to the excessive elevated non-esterified fatty acid, β-hydroxybutyrate, and urea that lead [SOURCE: Page_17]

to excessive steatosis and ketogenesis have been associated with reduced animal health and [SOURCE: Page_17]

4 [SOURCE: Page_17]

Chapter 1 - Introduction [SOURCE: Page_18]

reproductive performance (De Wit et al., 2001; Gillund et al., 2001; LeBlanc, 2010). In summary, [SOURCE: Page_18]

the negative consequences of focusing excessively on an increasing milk production, and thereby [SOURCE: Page_18]

the consequences on long-term efficiency, emphasizes the need to be able to predict the process [SOURCE: Page_18]

in which dairy cows partition their obtained nutrients in priority between physiological [SOURCE: Page_18]

functions. This would allow a better optimization of the linking between animal health and [SOURCE: Page_18]

fertility and the complex milk yield - body fatness - body fat mobilization. The essential biological [SOURCE: Page_18]

elements needed in modelling nutrient partitioning in dairy cows have been recently discussed [SOURCE: Page_18]

within the perspective of including information on animal genotypes (Friggens et al., 2013). The [SOURCE: Page_18]

interaction between genotype and environment (G × E) modulates expression of the process of [SOURCE: Page_18]

nutrient partitioning and animal performance, and thus it has also to be considered, especially in [SOURCE: Page_18]

the context of an increasingly variable environment of the future (Bryant et al., 2005; Friggens and [SOURCE: Page_18]

Newbold, 2007). [SOURCE: Page_18]

Genotype by environment interactions occur when the size of the animal response to a [SOURCE: Page_18]

change in environment, for example, a decrease in feed availability is different for different [SOURCE: Page_18]

genotypes (Simm, 1998). These interactions might lead to different consequences on animal [SOURCE: Page_18]

health and reproduction, and thus they may affect lifetime efficiency. In dairy production, many [SOURCE: Page_18]

studies have detected G × E interactions and investigated their subsequent influences on [SOURCE: Page_18]

production, health, and fertility of the animals (Veerkamp et al., 1994; Pryce et al., 1999; Calus, [SOURCE: Page_18]

2006; Horan et al., 2005; Delaby et al., 2009; Cutullic et al., 2011). The experiments of Delaby et al. [SOURCE: Page_18]

(2009), Cutullic et al. (2009), and Cutullic et al. (2011) have, for example, evaluated the influences [SOURCE: Page_18]

of two feeding strategies on productive and reproductive performance of two breeds: Holstein [SOURCE: Page_18]

selected for milk production, and Normande selected for dual-purpose of milk-meat. The results [SOURCE: Page_18]

have shown that the low dietary energy feeding strategy (i.e. ~10 MJ metabolizable energy/ kg of [SOURCE: Page_18]

dry matter intake) led to a low milk yield but a high loss of body condition, whereas the high [SOURCE: Page_18]

dietary energy feeding system (i.e. ~10.8 MJ metabolizable energy/ kg of dry matter intake) led to [SOURCE: Page_18]

a high milk yield with a low loss of body condition. The extent of decreasing milk yield from the [SOURCE: Page_18]

high to the low energy feeding in Holstein cows was larger than Normande cows. High milk [SOURCE: Page_18]

yield and high rate of loss of body condition have been well known to be contributors to the [SOURCE: Page_18]

declined animal health and fertility (Pryce et al., 2001; Berry et al., 2003). In terms of breed, [SOURCE: Page_18]

Normande cows had a significantly better re-calving rate than Holstein cows (52 versus 35%). [SOURCE: Page_18]

The high energy feeding tended to have a better re-calving rate compared to the low energy [SOURCE: Page_18]

feeding in both Normande and Holstein breeds (58 versus 46%, and 38 versus 32%, respectively). [SOURCE: Page_18]

In the context of future climate change where environmental conditions might be altered, feed [SOURCE: Page_18]

5 [SOURCE: Page_18]

Chapter 1 - Introduction [SOURCE: Page_19]

resources might be under pressure, different breeding and feeding strategies might be chosen by [SOURCE: Page_19]

the farmers, thus representing a need to predict the adaptive responses of the animals in these [SOURCE: Page_19]

situations. An obviously important question might, for instance, be: Is the intensively selected [SOURCE: Page_19]

production animal well suited with a free-range low input system? (Friggens et al., 2013). This is [SOURCE: Page_19]

particularly important for dairy cows in grazing systems where their environment might vary [SOURCE: Page_19]

more widely than that of other livestock species. [SOURCE: Page_19]

During this thesis, different aspects of FE were tackled based on the perspective of [SOURCE: Page_19]

Thus, the next section provides some general information on FE, nutrient partitioning regulation, [SOURCE: Page_19]

efficiency. [SOURCE: Page_19]

### 1.2. Common measures of feed efficiency in dairy cattle and their implications

In dairy production system, gross efficiency (GE), and residual feed intake (RFI) are the two [SOURCE: Page_19]

common measures of FE (Brody, 1945; Koch et al., 1963; Berry, 2009). The most appropriate [SOURCE: Page_19]

definition is, however, still unclear (Berry and Crowley, 2013). Defining FE for lactating animals [SOURCE: Page_19]

is more complicated than for growing ones because they undergo lactation cycles featuring by a [SOURCE: Page_19]

rapid catabolism of body reserves (mainly adipose tissue) immediately after parturition, followed [SOURCE: Page_19]

by an anabolism of these reserves in late lactation, although in thin animals body reserves can be [SOURCE: Page_19]

reconstituted in early lactation (Garnsworthy and Topps, 1982). Any measurements of FE must, [SOURCE: Page_19]

therefore, take into account such a contribution of body reserve mobilization. Another issue is [SOURCE: Page_19]

that, FE has normally been evaluated in terms of energy (or mass), with nitrogen being accounted [SOURCE: Page_19]

for via only its energy content (Zamani et al., 2008). However, as attention on environmental [SOURCE: Page_19]

impact of emissions of NH$_3$ and N2O has increased, not only energy efficiency but also nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_19]

efficiency should be studied. Key questions would then be that whether energy and nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_19]

efficiency are affected by the same factors, and what is the relationship between them. Though [SOURCE: Page_19]

the environmental impact of phosphorous excretion, and maybe other minerals, has been [SOURCE: Page_19]

recognized (Wu et al., 2000; Knowlton and Herbein, 2002) it is out of the scope of the current [SOURCE: Page_19]

thesis. Further studies that incorporate phosphorous efficiency are, therefore, highly [SOURCE: Page_19]

recommended. [SOURCE: Page_19]

### 1.2.1. Gross efficiency

Gross efficiency is defined as the ratio of output to input, i.e. milk produced/dry matter [SOURCE: Page_20]

intake (kg/kg), energy in milk/energy intake (Mcal/Mcal) or nitrogen in milk/nitrogen intake (g/g) [SOURCE: Page_20]

(Brody, 1945). This is the most common measure of milk production efficiency in literature [SOURCE: Page_20]

because it is easy to measure, explain, and compare across populations. GE has a moderate [SOURCE: Page_20]

heritability of 0.14 – 0.37 (Van Arendonk et al., 1991; Vallimont et al., 2011), and has been [SOURCE: Page_20]

included in dairy cattle breeding programs of Ireland, Australia, and New Zealand where the [SOURCE: Page_20]

breeding goal is on increased milk-solid production (Coleman et al., 2010). [SOURCE: Page_20]

There are, however, several problems limiting the usage of GE as a selection criterion that [SOURCE: Page_20]

have been discussed in a numerous studies (Wang et al., 1992; Veerkamp and Emmans, 1995; [SOURCE: Page_20]

Berry and Crowley, 2013). Firstly, since it is expressed as a ratio it suffers from an increase of the [SOURCE: Page_20]

error variance as a proportion of total variance in the statistical analysis (Lison, 1958); and [SOURCE: Page_20]

moreover selection for the trait can be unpredictable if selection pressure is disproportionate [SOURCE: Page_20]

between the two components traits, for example, milk yield and feed intake (Gunsett, 1984). [SOURCE: Page_20]

Secondly, gross feed efficiency has a high genetic correlation with milk yield [r = 0.79; [SOURCE: Page_20]

(Oldenbroek, 1989)], implying that selection for milk production alone would also improve FE. [SOURCE: Page_20]

Such a genetic selection for milk yield has been associated with an increased body reserve [SOURCE: Page_20]

mobilization in early lactation (Berry et al., 2003). Thirdly, GE does not differentiate the partial [SOURCE: Page_20]

efficiencies for maintenance, lactation, pregnancy, and body tissue gain or loss (Veerkamp and [SOURCE: Page_20]

Emmans, 1995). [SOURCE: Page_20]

This undesirable relationship between single trait selection and other important production [SOURCE: Page_20]

characteristics has resulted in a development of other indices (e.g. longevity) that balance [SOURCE: Page_20]

between production and functional traits such as health and fertility (VanRaden, 2004; Shook, [SOURCE: Page_20]

2006; Norman et al., 2010). Miglior et al. (2005) reviewed a number of selection indices used in [SOURCE: Page_20]

dairy cattle breeding program for 15 countries in the world and showed that in one decade most [SOURCE: Page_20]

of them have reduced the emphasis on milk production to approximately 50% in order to achieve [SOURCE: Page_20]

other breeding goals such as longevity, udder health, or calving ease. Nutritional and [SOURCE: Page_20]

management strategies have also been explored to reduce the consequences of such an [SOURCE: Page_20]

unfavorable relationship [see reviews of Lucy (2001) and Friggens et al. (2010)]. [SOURCE: Page_20]

### 1.2.2. Residual feed intake

Residual feed intake, sometime referred to net feed efficiency, is an alternative measure of [SOURCE: Page_21]

feed efficiency which was first proposed by Koch et al. (1963), and now is increasingly popular in [SOURCE: Page_21]

the dairy industry and research approaches. RFI can be defined as the difference between actual [SOURCE: Page_21]

feed intake and feed intake predicted from animal performance. The prediction of intake from [SOURCE: Page_21]

performance can be done in two ways: nutrient requirements, commonly energy, are computed [SOURCE: Page_21]

using feed table, for example, that of INRA-AFZ (Sauvant et al., 2004) or NRC (2001); or more [SOURCE: Page_21]

commonly by comparison among contemporaries within the same population by regressing [SOURCE: Page_21]

energy intake on energy sinks such as metabolic body weight, body weight change, and energy [SOURCE: Page_21]

corrected milk yield (Coleman et al., 2010). Additional energy requirements for physical activity [SOURCE: Page_21]

and pregnancy might also be included (Pryce et al., 2014). Dairy cows with negative values of RFI [SOURCE: Page_21]

consume less dietary energy intake than expected from their performance relative to their [SOURCE: Page_21]

contemporaries, and are therefore more efficient, whereas inefficient cows eat more feed than [SOURCE: Page_21]

expected, i.e. positive RFI. [SOURCE: Page_21]

Because RFI is, by definition, phenotypically independent of the energy sinks included in the [SOURCE: Page_21]

estimate of energy intake, it allows comparison between individuals differing in production level [SOURCE: Page_21]

(Herd and Arthur, 2009). The difference in residual feed intake might be due to the true [SOURCE: Page_21]

difference in efficiency among animals, random noise, or bias in the regression coefficients for the [SOURCE: Page_21]

respective regressors (Berry, 2009). Five major physiological processes that are likely contributing [SOURCE: Page_21]

to the variation in RFI have been discussed by Herd and Arthur (2009) consisting of intake of [SOURCE: Page_21]

feed, digestion of feed, metabolism (anabolism and catabolism associated with and including [SOURCE: Page_21]

variation in body composition and milk composition), physical activity, and thermoregulation. [SOURCE: Page_21]

The disadvantage of residual feed intake is that it is more difficult to compute and can be [SOURCE: Page_21]

more difficult to explain than GE. Richardson and Herd (2004) conducted a study on Angus [SOURCE: Page_21]

steers following divergent selection for RFI, estimated that only 73% variation in RFI would be [SOURCE: Page_21]

explained by variations in heat production from metabolic processed, body composition, and [SOURCE: Page_21]

physical activity, 27% of variation in RFI remained unexplained. It is also important to explore [SOURCE: Page_21]

whether this improved net feed efficiency will exhibit the same undesirable effects on health and [SOURCE: Page_21]

fertility of animals as have been discussed for high-producing cows or high gross efficiency, or [SOURCE: Page_21]

eventually other undesirable consequences. [SOURCE: Page_21]

### 1.2.3. Biological components underlying variation in feed efficiency

An understanding of biological processes that create variation in feed efficiency is necessary [SOURCE: Page_22]

for improving feed efficiency. Given the multiple definitions of feed efficiency, the biological [SOURCE: Page_22]

sources of variation also depend upon how it is defined. In terms of gross efficiency, four [SOURCE: Page_22]

biological processes have been drawn as the major sources for its variation including digestion [SOURCE: Page_22]

and nutrient absorption, maintenance requirement, utilization of metabolizable energy for [SOURCE: Page_22]

production, and nutrient partitioning (Bauman et al., 1985). Thus, the improvement in GE can be [SOURCE: Page_22]

obtained as a result of changes in such processes. Especially, nutrient partitioning is attributed to [SOURCE: Page_22]

the major source for the historic gain in gross efficiency and is the physiological basis for the [SOURCE: Page_22]

differences in genetically superior cows (Reddy and Krishna, 2009). [SOURCE: Page_22]

Digestion and nutrient absorption [SOURCE: Page_22]

The chemical composition and physical characteristic of feedstuffs can have a major effect [SOURCE: Page_22]

the digestibility (Bauman et al., 1985). It has also been well known that when the level of feed [SOURCE: Page_22]

intake increases relative to the requirement of maintenance, the digestion of feed (as measured by [SOURCE: Page_22]

total tract disappearance) tends to decrease (Nousiainen et al., 2009; Huhtanen et al., 2009). [SOURCE: Page_22]

Conversely, most studies with lactating dairy cows suggest little variation between animals in [SOURCE: Page_22]

apparent digestibility under standardized conditions and a similar level of intake (see review of [SOURCE: Page_22]

Veerkamp and Emmans, 1995). Thus management of feeding can considerably influence the [SOURCE: Page_22]

digestive efficiency but genetic improvement seems to be of minor importance. [SOURCE: Page_22]

Maintenance requirement [SOURCE: Page_22]

A variation in maintenance requirement might exist between cows differing in body size and [SOURCE: Page_22]

ages (Mowrey and Hershberger, 1982). It might also be affected by diet, management, and [SOURCE: Page_22]

environment. Fibre content of diet, grazing condition or heat stress can be considered as [SOURCE: Page_22]

examples for (Agnew and Yan, 2000; Collier et al., 2005). Few studies have, however, investigated [SOURCE: Page_22]

the variation in maintenance requirement of dairy cows under standardized conditions. [SOURCE: Page_22]

Moreover, maintenance requirement is not influenced by genetic merit for milk production in [SOURCE: Page_22]

Holstein Friesians (Holmes et al., 1983; Dong et al., 2013). [SOURCE: Page_22]

Utlization of metabolizable energy for milk production [SOURCE: Page_22]

There are differences in the partial efficiency of utilization of metabolizable energy for [SOURCE: Page_22]

different physiological functions (maintenance, growth, lactation, fattening, and pregnancy), and [SOURCE: Page_22]

between type of diet (Agnew and Yan, 2000). Such a variation is associated to the pathway of [SOURCE: Page_22]

9 [SOURCE: Page_22]

Chapter 1 - Introduction [SOURCE: Page_23]

absorbed nutrients and the specific products being formed. For example, when protected fat is [SOURCE: Page_23]

fed to dairy cows the secretion of milk fat and the partial efficiency of utilization of metabolizable [SOURCE: Page_23]

energy for milk production are increased which implies a very low cost of energy related to the [SOURCE: Page_23]

conversion of absorbed fatty acids to milk fat compared to the energy cost of de novo synthesis of [SOURCE: Page_23]

milk fatty acids (Smith, 1990). However, under typical conditions of nutrient supply and milk [SOURCE: Page_23]

composition little variation in partial efficiency for milk synthesis can be expected (Tolkamp and [SOURCE: Page_23]

Kyriazakis, 2009). [SOURCE: Page_23]

Nutrient partitioning [SOURCE: Page_23]

Nutrient partitioning has been attributed as the major source of the historic gains in feed [SOURCE: Page_23]

efficiency of dairy cows (VandeHaar and St-Pierre, 2006; Reddy and Krishna, 2009). High genetic [SOURCE: Page_23]

merit cows partition a greater proportion of nutrient intake to the mammary gland for milk [SOURCE: Page_23]

synthesis and this is associated with an increase in the voluntary intake, whereas low genetic [SOURCE: Page_23]

merit cows have a lower voluntary intake but if they do consumer more intake they would use it [SOURCE: Page_23]

for excessive body reserves deposition rather than milk synthesis. Thus, genetic selection for high [SOURCE: Page_23]

milk production does not only lead to cows that direct more absorbed nutrients for milk [SOURCE: Page_23]

synthesis but also eat more to support the greater milk yield (Yan et al., 2006; Xue et al., 2011). [SOURCE: Page_23]

The feeding management such as changing diet composition can also affect nutrient partitioning [SOURCE: Page_23]

(Oba and Allen, 2000). The principle of increased feed efficiency by changing nutrient [SOURCE: Page_23]

partitioning direction is based on the “dilution of maintenance” effect as described previously. [SOURCE: Page_23]

The side-effect of such an increased partition of nutrient intake to synthesizing milk on lifetime [SOURCE: Page_23]

efficiency has been aforementioned. [SOURCE: Page_23]

In the case of residual feed intake, its way of calculation has removed the animal difference [SOURCE: Page_23]

in nutrient partitioning by adjusting feed efficiency for differences in, for example, milk yield, [SOURCE: Page_23]

milk composition, body weight, or body composition. Thus variation in nutrient partitioning is [SOURCE: Page_23]

not a component of RFI but it would include animal differences in digestive efficiency and [SOURCE: Page_23]

nutrient absorption, maintenance requirement and partial efficiency of utilization of nutrient for [SOURCE: Page_23]

milk synthesis (see review of Herd and Arthur, 2009). [SOURCE: Page_23]

### 1.3. Genetic improvement and animal efficiency interrelationship: from the

perspective of nutrient partitioning [SOURCE: Page_23]

In this section, we describe a representation of the physiological regulations of nutrient [SOURCE: Page_23]

partitioning in dairy cows, which is likely to incorporate the influence of farm management [SOURCE: Page_23]

10 [SOURCE: Page_23]

Chapter 1 - Introduction [SOURCE: Page_24]

decisions (feeding, reproduction, culling and replacement but also the choice of animal genotype) [SOURCE: Page_24]

on animal performance. We argue that such representation is necessary to predict lifetime [SOURCE: Page_24]

efficiency especially when external pressures on farm management increase. [SOURCE: Page_24]

Between genotypes variation in physiological regulation of nutrient partitioning plays an [SOURCE: Page_24]

important role for successful lactation, and represents the physiological basis for the [SOURCE: Page_24]

improvements in productive efficiency, and also for the deteriorations of health and fertility that [SOURCE: Page_24]

emerge in high milk producing cows (Garnsworthy et al., 2008). Moreover, when animals are [SOURCE: Page_24]

transferred to the environments that differ from where they originated they might perform [SOURCE: Page_24]

differently; and this is termed as genotype by environment interaction previously. Such an [SOURCE: Page_24]

interaction would result in different consequences on animal performance, health, and [SOURCE: Page_24]

reproductive performance, and eventually animal efficiency. [SOURCE: Page_24]

### 1.3.1. Physiological regulations of nutrient partitioning

The term “nutrient partitioning”, in broad sense, refers to the processes by which available [SOURCE: Page_24]

nutrients are partitioned in varying proportions to different physiological functions; and in dairy [SOURCE: Page_24]

nutrition it refers particularly to the partition of obtained nutrients between milk outputs and [SOURCE: Page_24]

body reserves (Friggens and Newbold, 2007). Two types of physiological regulation of nutrient [SOURCE: Page_24]

partitioning have now been well recognized in the literature including homeostasis and [SOURCE: Page_24]

homeorhesis (Bauman and Currie, 1980). [SOURCE: Page_24]

Homeostatic control (Figure 1.2b; right-box) refers to the maintenance of physiological [SOURCE: Page_24]

equilibrium or constant conditions in the internal environment (Bauman and Currie, 1980). There [SOURCE: Page_24]

are many clear examples of homeostasis such as regulation to maintain body temperature or the [SOURCE: Page_24]

consumption of food and partition of nutrients during absorptive and post-absorptive periods. [SOURCE: Page_24]

The intake of a meal results in the changes in concentrations of blood metabolites, and an [SOURCE: Page_24]

increased ratio of insulin to glucagon. These changes lead to a greater uptake of glucose by the [SOURCE: Page_24]

liver (for glycogen synthesis), and by adipose tissue (for lipid synthesis). During the post- [SOURCE: Page_24]

absorptive period when the uptake of nutrient has been diminished the insulin-glucagon ratio [SOURCE: Page_24]

decreases sufficiently to result in mobilizations of glycogen and lipid stores. Obviously, there is [SOURCE: Page_24]

an acute regulation, i.e. homeostasis, of plasma glucose concentration by the reciprocal actions of [SOURCE: Page_24]

insulin and glucagon to ensure the proper balance in glucose supply and utilization by body [SOURCE: Page_24]

tissues and organs during absorptive and post-absorptive periods. Homeostatic control is [SOURCE: Page_24]

directed to the survival of an individual when facing with the environmental disturbances, e.g. [SOURCE: Page_24]

extreme shortage of nutritional resources. [SOURCE: Page_24]

11 [SOURCE: Page_24]

Chapter 1 - Introduction [SOURCE: Page_25]

Figure 1.2. A schematic representation of the two types of nutrient partitioning: homeostasis and [SOURCE: Page_25]

homeorhesis [Source: Friggens et al. (2013)]. [SOURCE: Page_25]

It has, however, been well documented that nutrient partition changes with physiological [SOURCE: Page_25]

stage of the animals (Kirkland and Gordon, 2001). Lipolysis and lipogenesis are, for example, up- [SOURCE: Page_25]

and down-regulated in different stages of a reproductive cycle (Chilliard et al., 2000), and there [SOURCE: Page_25]

are also endocrine changes (Bauman, 2000). The net result of these changes is that nutrients are [SOURCE: Page_25]

allocated to different organs, life functions, and end-products (Friggens and Newbold, 2007). [SOURCE: Page_25]

Clearly, this type of partition occurs not as a function of changing nutritional environment, but [SOURCE: Page_25]

rather with physiological stage, which is commonly defined as homeorhesis (Bauman and Currie, [SOURCE: Page_25]

1980) or teleophoresis (Chilliard et al., 2000). [SOURCE: Page_25]

Homeorhetic control is normally defined as the orchestrated or coordinated changes in [SOURCE: Page_25]

metabolism of body tissues necessary to support a physiological stage (Bauman and Currie, [SOURCE: Page_25]

1980). The implication behind this concept is that the animals has genetic drives of the [SOURCE: Page_25]

orchestrated changes in priority for partitioning available nutrients between metabolic functions [SOURCE: Page_25]

and are directed towards the perpetuation of the species. This type of drive is illustrated by the [SOURCE: Page_25]

bold arrow in Figure 1.2 (left-box); in other words, this control is part of the genetic make-up of [SOURCE: Page_25]

the animals. An example of homeorhetic control is the regulation of mobilizing and regaining [SOURCE: Page_25]

body reserves in early and late lactation, respectively (Friggens, 2003). [SOURCE: Page_25]

Such a notion of homeorhetic regulation highlights the importance of the connection [SOURCE: Page_25]

between genetic selection and nutrition, and thus capability to predict the changing partitioning [SOURCE: Page_25]

priorities of an animal as it ages, through physiological stages, and in response to different [SOURCE: Page_25]

12 [SOURCE: Page_25]

Chapter 1 - Introduction [SOURCE: Page_26]

nutritional environments because they play important roles in variation of lifetime efficiency of [SOURCE: Page_26]

the animals. The central role of the animal in regulating nutrient partition has been discussed [SOURCE: Page_26]

(Friggens et al., 2013). An advanced step of modelling nutrient partitioning of dairy cows has [SOURCE: Page_26]

been made by Martin and Sauvant (2010). [SOURCE: Page_26]

### 1.3.2. Environmental limits and challenges emerging in the context of genetic

improvement [SOURCE: Page_26]

The societal demand for a more sustainable agriculture and the future climate change imply [SOURCE: Page_26]

an possible increase in the diversity of environments in which the animals will be raised [SOURCE: Page_26]

(Friggens et al., 2013). It has widely been reported that dairy cattle might perform differently [SOURCE: Page_26]

when moving to another environment that differs from where they originated (Veerkamp et al., [SOURCE: Page_26]

1994; Kolmodin et al., 2002; Coffey et al., 2004; Boettcher et al., 2003). This evidence for genotype [SOURCE: Page_26]

by environment interaction suggests that some genotypes have a greater ability to cope or to [SOURCE: Page_26]

adapt to changes in the environments. In this context, some animals might respond to a change in [SOURCE: Page_26]

environment in a much stronger way than others; these animals are environmentally sensitive [SOURCE: Page_26]

and are termed “specialist” genotypes. In contrast, the less environmentally sensitive animals are [SOURCE: Page_26]

called “generalist”. In other words, the generalist can change their genotypes to adapt to different [SOURCE: Page_26]

environments whereas the specialist can hardly do so (Ten Napel et al., 2009). If animals rank [SOURCE: Page_26]

differently in different environments there is a risk of selecting environmentally sensitive [SOURCE: Page_26]

individuals if the selection mainly takes place in the favorable conditions, which is the case of [SOURCE: Page_26]

dairy cows. It is, therefore, important to be able to predict the adaptability of the animals when [SOURCE: Page_26]

they are moving to other environments. [SOURCE: Page_26]

Further, the way in which the selected genotypes of cow partition nutrient intake between [SOURCE: Page_26]

life functions has also been changed; compared to Norwegian cows, Holstein Friesian cows [SOURCE: Page_26]

partition more energy to the mammary gland for milk synthesis and less to body reserves (Yan et [SOURCE: Page_26]

al., 2006). The comparison between a high and low feeding systems has shown that dual-purpose [SOURCE: Page_26]

breed of Normande seemed to be better in adapting to the low feeding system than high [SOURCE: Page_26]

producing Holstein cows through better fertility (Cutullic et al., 2011). Thus, options have to be [SOURCE: Page_26]

found to select new genotypes that can adapt well with these emerging environments while still [SOURCE: Page_26]

maintaining production efficiency. Given the drastic decline in fertility and health, future [SOURCE: Page_26]

emphasis should be placed on longevity and lifetime performance traits in selection decisions. [SOURCE: Page_26]

13 [SOURCE: Page_26]

Chapter 1 - Introduction [SOURCE: Page_27]

Figure 1.3. Two separates views of genetic selection as an inter-generational process of selecting elite [SOURCE: Page_27]

individuals among a population of candidates, and of animal nutrition, as an intra-generational [SOURCE: Page_27]

process of nutrient partitioning by individuals. The emergence of G × E interactions can be viewed as [SOURCE: Page_27]

a result of combining these two processes (Re-produced from Douhard, 2013). [SOURCE: Page_27]

On the one hand, one can attempt to improve the environment so that it is less constraining [SOURCE: Page_27]

to the animals, e.g. by feed supplementation. On the other hand, one can select animal that adapt [SOURCE: Page_27]

well with the variations in the environment. In dairy cows, the inclusion of functional traits such [SOURCE: Page_27]

as health and fertility into the selection indices has increasingly been performed (Miglior et al., [SOURCE: Page_27]

2005; Miglior and Sewalem, 2009). Though these ideas are well recognized by both animal [SOURCE: Page_27]

production scientists (e.g. Blanc et al., 2007) and geneticists (e.g. Huquet et al., 2012), they are [SOURCE: Page_27]

commonly considered separately (Douhard, 2013). As we have seen that the differences in the [SOURCE: Page_27]

response by individuals of varying genotypes to the same nutritional treatment (environment) [SOURCE: Page_27]

can be substantial it is essential that the nutritional and genetic approaches should be integrated [SOURCE: Page_27]

to realistically represent the systems (Figure 1.3). [SOURCE: Page_27]

### 1.4. Mathematical model for integrating genetics and nutrition in dairy cattle

Generally, simulation models not only provide a tool to integrate knowledge from biological [SOURCE: Page_27]

experiments but can also assist the process of decision making by predicting the outcomes of [SOURCE: Page_27]

animal performance under different management strategies. In the recent review, McNamara [SOURCE: Page_27]

14 [SOURCE: Page_27]

Chapter 1 - Introduction [SOURCE: Page_28]

(2011) has indicated that the mathematical model is a powerful tool for integrating complex [SOURCE: Page_28]

relationship between genetic factors, nutritional environment and time. In dairy cattle nutrition, [SOURCE: Page_28]

the pioneering works of Baldwin et al. (1987a; b) and Oldham and Emmans (1989) have shown [SOURCE: Page_28]

that mathematical models can be used to explore data, concepts and hypotheses related to [SOURCE: Page_28]

nutrient partitioning [see review of Friggens et al. (2013) for the recent modelling advances]. [SOURCE: Page_28]

Reproduction of dairy cattle has also been extensively modeled [see reviews of Blanc et al. (2001) [SOURCE: Page_28]

and Vetharaniam et al. (2010)]. The simulations of production and reproduction are, however, [SOURCE: Page_28]

commonly done separately. The models of Bryant et al. (2005, 2007), and Martin and Sauvant [SOURCE: Page_28]

(2010), for example, have acceptably predicted the performance of dairy cows in different [SOURCE: Page_28]

nutritional environments. However, these models do not integrate the possible interaction [SOURCE: Page_28]

between production and reproduction. At the other extreme, the models that predict [SOURCE: Page_28]

reproduction of the animals only focus on the reproductive processes (Oltenacu et al., 1980; [SOURCE: Page_28]

Plaizier et al., 1998; Blanc and Agabriel, 2008). Attempts to develop a model that allow linking [SOURCE: Page_28]

production and reproduction in different environments are therefore necessary. The outcomes of [SOURCE: Page_28]

this type of model can be used to suggest the most suitable and profitable management strategy [SOURCE: Page_28]

for a specific genotype of cow. [SOURCE: Page_28]

15 [SOURCE: Page_28]

Chapter 2 – Objective and Approach [SOURCE: Page_29]

### 2.1. Research objective

In this thesis, we propose that the management of the genotype by environment interaction [SOURCE: Page_29]

at the animal level such as breeding and feeding strategies requires a representation of the long- [SOURCE: Page_29]

term effects on both productive and reproductive performance which are the major determinants [SOURCE: Page_29]

of lifetime efficiency. The overall objective of the present Ph.D. thesis was accordingly to develop [SOURCE: Page_29]

a model for predicting the consequences of different management strategies on the lifetime [SOURCE: Page_29]

efficiency of individual dairy cows (Figure 2.1). [SOURCE: Page_29]

Figure 2.1. The schematic presentation of developing a model for predicting lifetime efficiency of [SOURCE: Page_29]

individual cows. [SOURCE: Page_29]

Because the model in the present study was supposed to be energy-based, but nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_29]

efficiency is increasingly considered in dairy industry, an important issue needed to be initially [SOURCE: Page_29]

explored was what that if an improvement in energy efficiency would also lead to an [SOURCE: Page_29]

improvement in nitrogen efficiency. Additionally it is well recognized that there is a significant [SOURCE: Page_29]

genetic variation between individual cows of the same population in their capability to perform [SOURCE: Page_29]

16 [SOURCE: Page_29]

Chapter 2 – Objective and Approach [SOURCE: Page_30]

in the same environment, an individual-based model should be able to take this into account. [SOURCE: Page_30]

Lastly, there is an interaction between production and reproduction and it is changing when [SOURCE: Page_30]

animals move to different environments, thus the realistic prediction of animal efficiency should [SOURCE: Page_30]

take this into account. These three issues were identified as the key questions towards the [SOURCE: Page_30]

development of a lifetime energy efficiency model of individual dairy cows. [SOURCE: Page_30]

 Question 1. How does an improvement in energy efficiency relate to nitrogen efficiency? [SOURCE: Page_30]

Though the efficiency of utilization of minerals such as calcium or phosphate are [SOURCE: Page_30]

increasingly considered, energy and nitrogen efficiency are the most common indicators of feed [SOURCE: Page_30]

efficiency in dairy production systems. Environmentally speaking, energy and nitrogen efficiency [SOURCE: Page_30]

relate to the emissions of CH$_4$, N2O and CO$_2$, which are the principle greenhouse gases. In [SOURCE: Page_30]

economic terms, nitrogen efficiency used to be paid less attention because feeding more protein [SOURCE: Page_30]

than required by a cow usually increases feed cost without financial return but the loss of milk [SOURCE: Page_30]

due to underfeeding protein is even more expensive (VandeHaar and St-Pierre, 2006), and thus [SOURCE: Page_30]

nitrogen efficiency had been evaluated simply via its energy content. However, with the [SOURCE: Page_30]

increasing environmental concerns relating to nitrogen excretions, nitrogen efficiency becomes [SOURCE: Page_30]

more important (Bannink et al., 2013). In the context of this thesis, a question then arises is that [SOURCE: Page_30]

whether we have to develop two separate models for predicting energy and nitrogen efficiency [SOURCE: Page_30]

individually, or is it possible to initially build one model that can largely account for both [SOURCE: Page_30]

predicting energy efficiency and considering nitrogen efficiency as an add-on. This would only [SOURCE: Page_30]

be possible if they are correlated, and if they are affected by common factors. To our best [SOURCE: Page_30]

knowledge such information has not been reported in the literature. The objective of the first [SOURCE: Page_30]

study was, therefore, to explore the relationship between the two major components of feed [SOURCE: Page_30]

efficiency, i.e. energy and nitrogen efficiency, and possible nutritional and animal factors that [SOURCE: Page_30]

affect these efficiencies, and their relationship. [SOURCE: Page_30]

 Question 2. How to represent genetic variability in production potentials of individual cows in a [SOURCE: Page_30]

lifetime nutrient partitioning model? [SOURCE: Page_30]

It has widely been reported that there is a significant variability in performance between [SOURCE: Page_30]

individual cows in the same population (e.g. Rose et al., 2004; Bastin et al., 2011). In animal [SOURCE: Page_30]

breeding this variability can be quantified by recording and comparing the means of a trait for [SOURCE: Page_30]

different genotypes in the same environment and represented as the estimated breeding values [SOURCE: Page_30]

(Veerkamp and Emmans, 1995). Simulation models in dairy cattle, however, often assume an [SOURCE: Page_30]

average population response and apply it to all animals in the same population without reference [SOURCE: Page_30]

17 [SOURCE: Page_30]

Chapter 2 – Objective and Approach [SOURCE: Page_31]

to genetic variation between animals (see review of Bryant et al., 2005). This is even more difficult [SOURCE: Page_31]

because animals differing in genetic potential might respond differently with different treatments [SOURCE: Page_31]

(G × E) (Pomar et al., 2003). One approach to integrate such a genetic variation into the model for [SOURCE: Page_31]

simulating different genotypes of cow is to use the estimated breeding values (Bryant et al., 2008). [SOURCE: Page_31]

Another approach uses numerical parameters to scale animals differing in genetic potentials [SOURCE: Page_31]

(Vetharaniam et al., 2003; Martin and Sauvant, 2010), i.e. each parameter represents a [SOURCE: Page_31]

performance trait and thus allows describe genetic differences in performance. In the model of [SOURCE: Page_31]

Martin and Sauvant (2010), for example, cows that differ in milk production capacity are [SOURCE: Page_31]

theoretically represented by different values of parameter of νy, i.e. νy of one represents a cow [SOURCE: Page_31]

that has peak milk yield of 24 kg/d in first lactation, and a high value of ν y implies a high milk [SOURCE: Page_31]

production potential and vice versa. The application of the latter approach to realistically [SOURCE: Page_31]

simulate different genotypes of cow in the population, however, requires knowledge of variation [SOURCE: Page_31]

of such parameters, their nature of distribution, and the possible correlations that may exist [SOURCE: Page_31]

between parameters. The second study was, therefore, aimed to use an existing model of lifetime [SOURCE: Page_31]

nutrient partitioning for estimate individual variability in genetic potentials for some production [SOURCE: Page_31]

traits of different genotypes of cow. [SOURCE: Page_31]

 Question 3. How to represent the effect of performance and nutritional environment on reproduction [SOURCE: Page_31]

and productive lifespan? [SOURCE: Page_31]

As indicated previously, management strategies such as breeding programs or feeding [SOURCE: Page_31]

regimes might have an effect on milk yield and body reserves of dairy cows that are commonly [SOURCE: Page_31]

suggested as the main determinants of the impaired health and fertility of cows (e.g. Cutullic et [SOURCE: Page_31]

al., 2009, 2011). This indicates a need to be able to predict not only performance but also long- [SOURCE: Page_31]

term consequences on health and reproduction of the animals under changing management [SOURCE: Page_31]

scenarios. In most simulation models in dairy cattle, the reproductive process is usually [SOURCE: Page_31]

represented by assuming a fixed reproductive performance such as fixed postpartum anestrus [SOURCE: Page_31]

period, or fixed calving interval which are increasingly not realistic for high producing cows. [SOURCE: Page_31]

Recently a few authors have incorporated the possible interaction between milk yield and body [SOURCE: Page_31]

reserves and reproductive performance (Inchaisri et al., 2010; Brun-Lafleur et al., 2013), the focus [SOURCE: Page_31]

of these models are within a lactation, and moreover they do not allow responses of animals in [SOURCE: Page_31]

terms of production under changing nutritional environment to be simulated. Thus, the objective [SOURCE: Page_31]

of this last study was to extend the model that had been used in the second study to simulate [SOURCE: Page_31]

productive and reproductive performance, and lifetime efficiency of different genotypes of cow [SOURCE: Page_31]

under different feeding regimes. [SOURCE: Page_31]

18 [SOURCE: Page_31]

Chapter 2 – Objective and Approach [SOURCE: Page_32]

### 2.2. General approach

This section describes in general terms the research methodology that was applied during [SOURCE: Page_32]

this thesis. Firstly, the meta-analysis approach was applied to explore the relationship between [SOURCE: Page_32]

energy and nitrogen efficiency, and factors affecting these efficiencies. Secondly, an external data [SOURCE: Page_32]

set was utilized to evaluate the suitability of a previously published model for deriving the [SOURCE: Page_32]

estimates of genetic variability in genetic potentials of individual cows. Finally, the interaction [SOURCE: Page_32]

between production and reproduction was incorporated in a model to explore the consequences [SOURCE: Page_32]

on animal’s lifetime efficiency. It was realized through incorporation of the possible influences of [SOURCE: Page_32]

milk yield, and body reserves on reproductive process. Specifically, we hypothesized that high [SOURCE: Page_32]

milk yield, low energy balance, and low body condition negatively affects reproductive [SOURCE: Page_32]

performance of cows. [SOURCE: Page_32]

### 2.2.1. Study 1: meta-analysis approach to explore factors affecting energy and nitrogen

efficiency, and their relationship [SOURCE: Page_32]

Building database [SOURCE: Page_32]

The initial step towards a meta-analysis was to create a database for later analyses by [SOURCE: Page_32]

conducting a literature search using online search engines including ScienceDirect, Google [SOURCE: Page_32]

Scholar, and Web of Science. Keywords were energy efficiency, nitrogen efficiency, dairy cows, [SOURCE: Page_32]

nutrient utilization and dairy cow performance, whereas the main criterion for inclusion of a [SOURCE: Page_32]

study in the meta-analysis was that it reported, or permitted calculation of, energy and nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_32]

efficiency. Consequently, a data set containing in general the information on number of studies, [SOURCE: Page_32]

number of observations, nutritional and animal factors possibly affecting energy and nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_32]

efficiency was obtained. The next step was to define how to measure energy and nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_32]

efficiency. [SOURCE: Page_32]

Energy and nitrogen efficiency derivations and their relationship [SOURCE: Page_32]

In this study, energy and nitrogen efficiency were defined as the ratios between energy in [SOURCE: Page_32]

milk over energy intake (Mcal/Mcal), and nitrogen in milk over nitrogen intake (g/g), [SOURCE: Page_32]

respectively. Dietary energy and nitrogen were computed at digestible level using chemical [SOURCE: Page_32]

composition information obtained from feeding tables of Institut National de la Recherche [SOURCE: Page_32]

Agronomique (INRA, 1988, 2010), and collaboration between INRA and Association Francaise de [SOURCE: Page_32]

Zootechnie (INRA-AFZ; Sauvant et al., 2004), and equations proposed by National Research [SOURCE: Page_32]

Council (NRC, 2001). The equation of Sauvant and Giger-Reverdin (2009) was applied to account [SOURCE: Page_32]

19 [SOURCE: Page_32]

Chapter 2 – Objective and Approach [SOURCE: Page_33]

for reduced digestibility due to increased dry matter intake. Energy and nitrogen in milk were [SOURCE: Page_33]

calculated using equations of NRC (2001). Details on these equations can be found in chapter III [SOURCE: Page_33]

(Materials and Methods). [SOURCE: Page_33]

The correlation between energy and nitrogen efficiency was investigated in two ways: inter- [SOURCE: Page_33]

and intra-study. The inter-study correlation was computed from the observed data of energy and [SOURCE: Page_33]

nitrogen efficiency, whereas the intra-study correlation was the correlation after adjustment for [SOURCE: Page_33]

the random effect of study. [SOURCE: Page_33]

Statistical analysis [SOURCE: Page_33]

To analyze the effects of animal and nutritional variables on energy and nitrogen efficiency, [SOURCE: Page_33]

multiple linear regression was applied using the nlme and lm packages of the statistical software [SOURCE: Page_33]

R. Because the data were obtained from many studies conducted over many years and in [SOURCE: Page_33]

different conditions, study effect was considered as random as suggested by St-Pierre (2001). [SOURCE: Page_33]

Moreover, as energy and nitrogen efficiency were calculated as the ratios of the outputs of [SOURCE: Page_33]

these respective nutrients in milk over inputs in the diet, milk yield and dry matter intake [SOURCE: Page_33]

appeared in both ratios. We, therefore, would expect a strong correlation between them [SOURCE: Page_33]

irrespective of any animal or diet composition factors. To overcome this problem, we decided to [SOURCE: Page_33]

look at also the ratio between nitrogen and energy efficiency, which means that this “artifact” [SOURCE: Page_33]

would be removed. [SOURCE: Page_33]

### 2.2.2. Study 2: simulating genetic variability in lifetime performance of individual cows

In this study, we evaluated a previously published model of Martin and Sauvant (2010), [SOURCE: Page_33]

referred to here as GARUNS, for its capability of simulating genetic variability in nutrient [SOURCE: Page_33]

partitioning and performance through the lifespan of individual cows. This model was proposed [SOURCE: Page_33]

to be used later for simulating different genotypes of cow differing in genetic merit, e.g. high [SOURCE: Page_33]

versus low milk production. [SOURCE: Page_33]

We used an external dataset containing records on performance, i.e. milk yield, milk [SOURCE: Page_33]

composition, body weight, and dry matter intake, of individual cows of three breeds, i.e. Danish [SOURCE: Page_33]

Red, Danish Holstein, and Jersey (Nielsen et al., 2003). The model was fitted to data cow by cow [SOURCE: Page_33]

individually, using the Modelmaker software version 3.0 (Cherwell Scientific Ltd, 2000), to [SOURCE: Page_33]

investigate the capability of the GARUNS model in capturing the individual variability in genetic [SOURCE: Page_33]

production potentials of different genotypes of cow. Specifically, the cows in this data set were [SOURCE: Page_33]

20 [SOURCE: Page_33]

Chapter 2 – Objective and Approach [SOURCE: Page_34]

kept under an environment that was assumed to be non-constraint, and thus make it appropriate [SOURCE: Page_34]

to be used in our study. [SOURCE: Page_34]

Figure 4.1. Schematic diagram of the GARUNS model [adapted with permission of Martin and [SOURCE: Page_34]

Sauvant (2010)], showing how energy requirements for different life functions (growth, body reserves, [SOURCE: Page_34]

and milk) are derived from genetic scaling parameters combined with lifetime profiles of relative [SOURCE: Page_34]

priorities for the different life functions. The priorities for foetal growth, ageing (in regulating sub- [SOURCE: Page_34]

model), and energy needed for foetal growth (in operating sub-model) are not presented as they are [SOURCE: Page_34]

assumed to be common trajectories for every individual. Total energy needed is the sum of the [SOURCE: Page_34]

different requirements, from which DM intake can be calculated assuming an appropriate diet energy [SOURCE: Page_34]

density (eD). [SOURCE: Page_34]

In this context, we hypothesized that if the GARUNS model fits well the individual data, the [SOURCE: Page_34]

prediction accuracy should be within an acceptable range, and the residuals from individual fits [SOURCE: Page_34]

should be equally distributed around zero without any specific time-trends through lactation. [SOURCE: Page_34]

The prediction accuracy was evaluated using root mean square error. Since the fittings were done [SOURCE: Page_34]

for three breeds through animal’s life within the experiment, a statistical model that includes the [SOURCE: Page_34]

effects of these two factors was used to investigate the distribution of the fitting residuals [SOURCE: Page_34]

(Observed – Fitted values). If the GARUNS general trajectory of life function priority applies [SOURCE: Page_34]

universally then the distribution of the residuals should be normal and one should not obtain the [SOURCE: Page_34]

21 [SOURCE: Page_34]

Chapter 2 – Objective and Approach [SOURCE: Page_35]

significant effects of breed and day from calving variables. The general form of such a statistical [SOURCE: Page_35]

model was: [SOURCE: Page_35]

Yijk = µ + αiDFCi + βjBreedj + λijDFC×Breedij + δiDFC2i + γk + εijk [SOURCE: Page_35]

where Yijk is the fitting residual of BW, MY, milk fat (MCF), milk protein (MCP), milk lactose [SOURCE: Page_35]

(MCL), and DMI, µ is the overall intercept across animals, αi, βj, and λij, and δi are regression [SOURCE: Page_35]

coefficients of Y on the corresponding predictors of day from calving (DFC), breed, interaction [SOURCE: Page_35]

between DFC and breed, and squared DFC, respectively, γk is the random effect of kth animal, εijk [SOURCE: Page_35]

is the residual error which was assumed to be normally distributed with mean of 0 and variance [SOURCE: Page_35]

of σ2e, i is the number of days from calving, j is the number of breeds, and k is number of animals. [SOURCE: Page_35]

Moreover, in order to use the model for simulating genotypes of cow it is essential to obtain [SOURCE: Page_35]

knowledge on the individually derived genetic scaling parameters such as correlations between [SOURCE: Page_35]

them, and (animal) factors affecting these parameters. We explored such information using the [SOURCE: Page_35]

correlation matrix, and principal component analysis. [SOURCE: Page_35]
