### 2.2.3. Study 3: predicting lifetime efficiency of different genotypes of cow under different

feeding strategies [SOURCE: Page_35]

This study extended the GARUNS model to allow realistic prediction of the lifetime [SOURCE: Page_35]

efficiency of various genotypes of cow, specifically high versus low milk producing, under [SOURCE: Page_35]

different feeding strategies, specifically high versus low energy feeding. The general schematic [SOURCE: Page_35]

representation of this extended model is shown in Figure 2.3. [SOURCE: Page_35]

By changing the genetic scaling parameters it is possible to produce different genotypes of [SOURCE: Page_35]

cow differing in, for example, milk production potential, whereas the change in nutritional [SOURCE: Page_35]

environment can be done through changing dietary energy density. The outputs of the GARUNS [SOURCE: Page_35]

model including milk production, body weight, and energy balance were subsequently used to [SOURCE: Page_35]

drive the reproduction sub-model (REPRO). Conventional reproduction models normally include [SOURCE: Page_35]

different successive steps of uterine evolution, resumption of the cycle, estrus expression, [SOURCE: Page_35]

conception, and embryo survival. However, the main interest of our present study was on [SOURCE: Page_35]

lifetime efficiency, for which a key component is the productive lifespan of the animals. Cows [SOURCE: Page_35]

that fail to establish pregnancy after a period of time are culled which leads to a shortening of the [SOURCE: Page_35]

productive lifespan, this information is currently missing in the GARUNS model. Thus, the [SOURCE: Page_35]

reproductive information needed is only that creates variation in the lifespan, this can be simply [SOURCE: Page_35]

seen as a result of whether the cow successfully gets pregnant, and calves or not. Consequently, [SOURCE: Page_35]

22 [SOURCE: Page_35]

Chapter 2 – Objective and Approach [SOURCE: Page_36]

instead of modelling all these reproductive steps we assumed a likelihood of conception of a [SOURCE: Page_36]

viable calf (LCONC) as the final product of reproduction process, which is a result of other steps [SOURCE: Page_36]

involved towards having a newborn calf, and being affected by milk yield, body condition, and [SOURCE: Page_36]

energy balance. We hypothesized that a cow with high milk yield, low body condition, and low [SOURCE: Page_36]

energy balance would have a low likelihood of conception. This situation might be exacerbated [SOURCE: Page_36]

when cows face with environmental constraints, such as shortage of energy. [SOURCE: Page_36]

Figure 2.3. General presentation of model for predicting lifetime efficiency of individual cows [SOURCE: Page_36]

The function for the likelihood of conception is presented below: [SOURCE: Page_36]

LCONC = f (LCONCMY, LCONCBCS, LCONCEB) [SOURCE: Page_36]

where LCONCMY, LCONCBCS, and LCONCEB are the likelihood of conceptions determined [SOURCE: Page_36]

by milk yield, body condition, and energy balance. We assumed a maximal value of 0.6 ± 0.06 for [SOURCE: Page_36]

LCONC which reflects the average conception rate of virgin heifers. LCONCMY, LCONCBCS, and [SOURCE: Page_36]

LCONCEB were described using the logistic function as follows: [SOURCE: Page_36]

LCONCMY = 2 × (1 / 1 + exp (RatMY × MY)) [SOURCE: Page_36]

LCONCBCS = 2 × (1 / 1 + exp (-RatBCS × (BCS - 3)) [SOURCE: Page_36]

LCONCEB = 2 × (1 / 1 + exp (-RatEB × EB)) [SOURCE: Page_36]

where RatMY, RatBCS, and RatEB are parameters; MY is milk yield (kg/d), BCS is body [SOURCE: Page_36]

condition score ranging from 0 to 5, and EB is energy balance (MJ/d). [SOURCE: Page_36]

The model’s outputs such as interval from calving to conception, interval from first [SOURCE: Page_36]

insemination to conception, or conception rate were compared to data from the literature to [SOURCE: Page_36]

evaluate the quality of the model. [SOURCE: Page_36]

The lifetime efficiency of the animals was computed using the following equation: [SOURCE: Page_36]

23 [SOURCE: Page_36]

Chapter 2 – Objective and Approach [SOURCE: Page_37]

N EMilk LTE =              ×100 1 EIntake [SOURCE: Page_37]

where LTE is lifetime efficiency of energy utilization (%), N is day from birth to culling [SOURCE: Page_37]

(day), EMilk is total energy in milk (MJ), EIntake is total metabolizable energy intake (MJ). [SOURCE: Page_37]

24 [SOURCE: Page_37]

Chapter 3 – Meta-analysis [SOURCE: Page_38]

Chapter 3 [SOURCE: Page_38]

Factors Affecting Energy and Nitrogen Efficiency: a Meta-analysis [SOURCE: Page_38]

H. N. Phuong,†* N. C. Friggens,†* I. J. M. de Boer,‡ and Ph. Schmidely†* [SOURCE: Page_38]

† INRA, UMR791 Modélisation Systémique Appliquée aux Ruminants, F-75005 Paris, France [SOURCE: Page_38]

* AgroParisTech, UMR791 Modélisation Systémique Appliquée aux Ruminants, F-75005 Paris, [SOURCE: Page_38]

France [SOURCE: Page_38]

‡ Animal Production Systems group, Wageningen University, PO 338, 6700 AH Wageningen, The [SOURCE: Page_38]

Netherlands [SOURCE: Page_38]

Journal of Dairy Science 96 (2013): 7245-7259 [SOURCE: Page_38]

25 [SOURCE: Page_38]

Chapter 3 – Meta-analysis [SOURCE: Page_39]

Abstract [SOURCE: Page_39]

A meta-analysis was performed to explore the correlation between energy and nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_39]

efficiency of dairy cows, and to study nutritional and animal factors that influence these [SOURCE: Page_39]

efficiencies, as well as their relationship. Treatment mean values were extracted from 68 peer- [SOURCE: Page_39]

reviewed studies, including 306 feeding trials. The main criterion for inclusion of a study in the [SOURCE: Page_39]

meta-analysis was that it reported, or permitted calculation of, energy efficiency (Eeff; energy in [SOURCE: Page_39]

milk/ digestible energy intake) and nitrogen efficiency (Neff; nitrogen in milk/ digestible nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_39]

intake) at the digestible level (digestible energy or digestible protein). The effect of nutritional [SOURCE: Page_39]

and animal variables including neutral detergent fibre, acid detergent fibre (ADF), digestible [SOURCE: Page_39]

energy, digestible protein, proportion of concentrate (PCO), dry matter intake (DMI), milk yield [SOURCE: Page_39]

(MY), days in milk (DIM) and body weight (BW), on energy efficiency, nitrogen efficiency and [SOURCE: Page_39]

the ratio Neff:Eeff was analyzed using mixed models. The interstudy correlation between Eeff [SOURCE: Page_39]

and Neff was 0.62, whereas the intrastudy correlation was 0.30. The higher interstudy correlation [SOURCE: Page_39]

was partly due to milk yield and dry matter intake being present in both Eeff and Neff. We, [SOURCE: Page_39]

therefore, also explored the ratio Neff:Eeff. Energy efficiency was negatively associated with ADF [SOURCE: Page_39]

and PCO, whereas Neff was negatively associated with ADF and digestible energy. The Neff:Eeff [SOURCE: Page_39]

ratio was affected by ADF and PCO only. In conclusion, the results indicate a possibility to [SOURCE: Page_39]

maximize feed efficiency in terms of both energy and nitrogen at the same time. In other words, [SOURCE: Page_39]

an improvement in Eeff would also mean an improvement in Neff. The current study also shows [SOURCE: Page_39]

that these types of transverse data are not sufficient to study the effect of animal factors, such as [SOURCE: Page_39]

DIM, on feed efficiency. Longitudinal measurements per animal would probably be more [SOURCE: Page_39]

appropriate. [SOURCE: Page_39]

Key words: meta-analysis, energy efficiency, nitrogen efficiency, dairy cows [SOURCE: Page_39]

26 [SOURCE: Page_39]

Chapter 3 – Meta-analysis [SOURCE: Page_40]

Introduction [SOURCE: Page_40]

Feed efficiency (FE) in dairy production has received increasing attention because it [SOURCE: Page_40]

influences not only farm profitability but also losses to the environment. Because feed costs [SOURCE: Page_40]

account for more than 50% of total costs of dairy production (Shalloo et al., 2004), any [SOURCE: Page_40]

improvement in FE has a direct impact on the profitability of dairy farms (Britt et al., 2003). [SOURCE: Page_40]

Moreover, if nutrients consumed are not converted into milk, body reserves or a newborn calf, [SOURCE: Page_40]

they are excreted into the environment, resulting in emissions of, for example, ammonia, [SOURCE: Page_40]

methane or nitrous oxide (Thomassen et al., 2009). Improving FE can, therefore, be beneficial for [SOURCE: Page_40]

both farm profitability and the environmental impact of milk production (Wall et al., 2010; Yan et [SOURCE: Page_40]

al., 2010). [SOURCE: Page_40]

Feed efficiency is traditionally defined as the ratio of output to input [e.g., milk [SOURCE: Page_40]

produced/DMI (kg/kg), energy in milk/energy intake (Mcal/Mcal) or nitrogen in milk/nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_40]

intake (g/g); (Brody, 1945)]. An alternative definition of FE is residual feed intake (RFI; (kg/day or [SOURCE: Page_40]

Mcal/day), which estimates the difference between actual and predicted intake (Koch et al., 1963; [SOURCE: Page_40]

Prendiville et al., 2009). In genetic selection, RFI has been preferred to efficiency expressed as [SOURCE: Page_40]

ratios, as selection based on ratios carries disadvantages, such as increased error variance as a [SOURCE: Page_40]

proportion of total variance and a strong correlation with component traits, for example FE and [SOURCE: Page_40]

milk yield (Wang et al., 1992). Residual feed intake is, by definition, phenotypically independent [SOURCE: Page_40]

of component traits, but suffers from the problem of accumulation of errors in the measurements [SOURCE: Page_40]

used (Herd and Arthur, 2009). Richardson et al. (2004), for example, stated that only 73% of the [SOURCE: Page_40]

variation in RFI could be explained by biological mechanisms. Despite their obvious importance, [SOURCE: Page_40]

in practice, neither FE nor RFI are used extensively due to the difficulties in the field of [SOURCE: Page_40]

measuring their components, particularly intake, with a reasonable level of precision [SOURCE: Page_40]

(McNaughton and Pryce, 2007; Moore et al., 2009). A second issue is that FE has commonly been [SOURCE: Page_40]

evaluated in terms of energy (or mass), with nitrogen being accounted for only via its energy [SOURCE: Page_40]

content (Zamani et al., 2008). However, attention for environmental impact of emissions of NH$_3$ [SOURCE: Page_40]

or N2O, which are related to N excretion, has increased. Hence, nitrogen efficiency should also be [SOURCE: Page_40]

considered. In this context, a key question is whether nitrogen and energy efficiency are affected [SOURCE: Page_40]

by the same factors? [SOURCE: Page_40]

FE can be measured at metabolizable, digestible or gross levels of nutrient intake (Veerkamp [SOURCE: Page_40]

and Emmans, 1995; Tolkamp, 2010), which we would then call metabolic, digestible and gross [SOURCE: Page_40]

efficiency, respectively. These different measures of FE may lead to different biological or [SOURCE: Page_40]

27 [SOURCE: Page_40]

Chapter 3 – Meta-analysis [SOURCE: Page_41]

economic interpretations (Blake and Custodio, 1984). Variation in metabolic efficiency indicates [SOURCE: Page_41]

primarily differences in partitioning of nutrients between milk production and other life [SOURCE: Page_41]

functions. Environmentally speaking, it does not include losses in feces, gases and urine being [SOURCE: Page_41]

excreted. At the other extreme, gross efficiency includes the part of feed that is not available to [SOURCE: Page_41]

animals, and, as such, it is of limited value for quantifying the variability between animals in [SOURCE: Page_41]

efficiency. Efficiency of use of digestible energy and nitrogen does not consider fecal losses but [SOURCE: Page_41]

includes losses in gases, urine, and animal differences in partitioning of nutrients. Digestible [SOURCE: Page_41]

efficiency, therefore, makes it possible to not only evaluate the environmental impact of milk [SOURCE: Page_41]

production, but also to compare efficiency between animal genotypes in using feed to produce [SOURCE: Page_41]

milk. Fecal losses can be estimated easily using feed chemical characteristics or in vitro [SOURCE: Page_41]

degradation data (Nousiainen et al., 2009). [SOURCE: Page_41]

Feed efficiency for milk production depends not only on diet composition but also on animal [SOURCE: Page_41]

genotype and physiological state (Blake and Custodio, 1984). Genetic potential for milk [SOURCE: Page_41]

production and stage of lactation differences modify partition of absorbed nutrients into milk and [SOURCE: Page_41]

other life functions. Feed efficiency should, therefore, be studied from both the nutritional and [SOURCE: Page_41]

genetic points of view. A better understanding of how nutrients are partitioned has been [SOURCE: Page_41]

recognized as being central to maximizing FE and minimizing environmental impact (Friggens et [SOURCE: Page_41]

al., 2011). However, it has become clear later the available data did not allow the exploration of [SOURCE: Page_41]

effect of animal factors on FE. In addition, insight into how Eeff and Neff are correlated and how [SOURCE: Page_41]

this correlation changes in different conditions would provide a basis for maximizing or [SOURCE: Page_41]

predicting FE of animals in both energy and nitrogen terms. Using a meta-analysis approach, the [SOURCE: Page_41]

current study explored the correlation between Eeff and Neff, and examined common nutritional [SOURCE: Page_41]

factors affecting Eeff and Neff, and their relationships. [SOURCE: Page_41]

Materials and Methods [SOURCE: Page_41]

Literature search [SOURCE: Page_41]

A literature search using the Web of Science (http://thomsonreuters.com/web-of-science/), [SOURCE: Page_41]

ScienceDirect (http://www.sciencedirect.com/), and Google Scholar (http://scholar.google.com/) [SOURCE: Page_41]

search engines was conducted to create a database for this study. The following keywords were [SOURCE: Page_41]

used in different combinations: energy efficiency, nitrogen efficiency, dairy cows, nutrient [SOURCE: Page_41]

utilization and dairy cow performance. The inclusion criteria in the database were a feed [SOURCE: Page_41]

description in terms of ingredients (%), proportion of concentrate (%), dry matter intake (kg/d), [SOURCE: Page_41]

milk yield (kg/d), milk fat, protein and lactose (%), body weight (kg) and days in milk (d). The [SOURCE: Page_41]

28 [SOURCE: Page_41]

Chapter 3 – Meta-analysis [SOURCE: Page_42]

chemical composition of the diets in publications was either obtained from the reported [SOURCE: Page_42]

laboratory analysis or estimated from NRC (2001), with exceptions of digestible energy and [SOURCE: Page_42]

digestible protein, which will be mentioned later in this section. [SOURCE: Page_42]

Only full studies published in peer-reviewed journals were selected (abstracts, conference [SOURCE: Page_42]

papers, and review articles were not considered). Eventually, 68 studies (listed in the Appendix) [SOURCE: Page_42]

consisting of 306 treatment means that satisfied the above criteria were kept for further analysis. [SOURCE: Page_42]

Efficiency derivations [SOURCE: Page_42]

As indicated, FE was defined as the ratio of nutrient output in milk to nutrient input in feed, [SOURCE: Page_42]

with a focus on energy and nitrogen (subsequently termed energy efficiency and nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_42]

efficiency). Additionally, inputs were expressed as digestible energy or digestible protein. [SOURCE: Page_42]

Energy efficiency was calculated as the ratio between energy in milk (Mcal/d) and digestible [SOURCE: Page_42]

energy intake (Mcal/d): [SOURCE: Page_42]

MY (kg/day) x E milk (Mcal/kgMilk) Energy efficiency (%) =                                                            [1] DMI (kg/day) x DE diet (Mcal/kgDM) [SOURCE: Page_42]

where, MY is milk yield of an animal per day, DE diet is the digestible energy content of the [SOURCE: Page_42]

diet, and E milk is the energy content of milk, which is the sum of energy of all milk components [SOURCE: Page_42]

including fat, protein and lactose using the equations suggested by NRC (2001) as follows: [SOURCE: Page_42]

E milk = milk fat content (kg/kg of milk) x 9.29 [SOURCE: Page_42]

+ milk protein content (kg/kg of milk) x 5.47 [SOURCE: Page_42]

+ milk lactose content (kg/kg of milk) x 3.95 [SOURCE: Page_42]

where 5.47, 9.29, and 3.95 are the amounts of energy released from the combustion of one kg of [SOURCE: Page_42]

protein, fat and lactose, respectively (Mcal/kg; NRC, 2001). [SOURCE: Page_42]

Dry matter intake is calculated per animal per day. The DE diet was estimated using tables [SOURCE: Page_42]

from Institut National de la Recherche Agronomique (INRA; 1988, 2010) and collaboration [SOURCE: Page_42]

between INRA and Association Française de Zootechnie (INRA-AFZ; Sauvant et al., 2004). To [SOURCE: Page_42]

account for reduced digestibility due to increased DMI, a correction was applied using the [SOURCE: Page_42]

equation of Sauvant and Giger-Reverdin (2009). [SOURCE: Page_42]

DEadjusted = DEstandard x dEadjusted [SOURCE: Page_42]

dEadjusted = 66.8 + 20.1 PCO – 3.37 FI [SOURCE: Page_42]

where DEadjusted is the digestible energy after being adjusted for observed feed intake and [SOURCE: Page_42]

proportion of concentrate (Mcal/kg of DM), DEstandard is the digestible energy value obtained at [SOURCE: Page_42]

maintenance level (Mcal/kg of DM), dEadjusted is the adjusted digestion coefficient of energy (%), [SOURCE: Page_42]

29 [SOURCE: Page_42]

Chapter 3 – Meta-analysis [SOURCE: Page_43]

PCO is the proportion of concentrate (%), and FI (%) is the observed feed intake (kg of DM/d) [SOURCE: Page_43]

expressed relative to body weight (kg). [SOURCE: Page_43]

Nitrogen efficiency was calculated as the ratio between nitrogen in milk (g/d) and digestible [SOURCE: Page_43]

nitrogen intake (g/d): [SOURCE: Page_43]

MY (kg/d) x N milk (g/kgMilk) Nitrogen efficiency (%) =                                                         [2] DMI (kg/d) x DN diet (g/kgDM) [SOURCE: Page_43]

where MY and DMI have already been indicated. N milk is the nitrogen content in milk, [SOURCE: Page_43]

which was either obtained from data (if provided) or calculated by dividing milk crude protein [SOURCE: Page_43]

content by 6.38. Likewise, DN diet is the digestible nitrogen content in the diet, which was either [SOURCE: Page_43]

obtained from the study or calculated by dividing digestible protein content in the diet (g/kg of [SOURCE: Page_43]

DM), obtained from INRA (1988, 2010) and INRA-AFZ (Sauvant et al., 2004), by 6.25. The effect of [SOURCE: Page_43]

the proportion of concentrate and an increased feed intake on protein digestibility was assumed [SOURCE: Page_43]

to be non-significant. [SOURCE: Page_43]

It can be clearly seen that milk yield and DMI appear in both Equations 1 and 2. Thus we [SOURCE: Page_43]

would expect an artifactual correlation between energy efficiency and nitrogen efficiency, [SOURCE: Page_43]

irrespective of any factor related to the diet or animals, with the extent of the correlation [SOURCE: Page_43]

depending on how largely MY and DMI numerically contribute to the computations of energy [SOURCE: Page_43]

and nitrogen efficiency. This should be taken into account when considering factors that affect [SOURCE: Page_43]

energy and nitrogen efficiency, and also when studying the relationship between energy and [SOURCE: Page_43]

nitrogen efficiency. Therefore, we decided to look also at the ratio of nitrogen efficiency to energy [SOURCE: Page_43]

efficiency (Neff:Eeff). By analyzing this ratio, the “artifact” of milk yield and DMI is theoretically [SOURCE: Page_43]

removed, and other factors that have an influence on the correlation between energy and [SOURCE: Page_43]

nitrogen efficiency can be more clearly examined. This ratio is shown in Equation 3: [SOURCE: Page_43]

Nitrogen efficiency (%)   N milk (Mcal/kgMilk) x DE diet (Mcal/kgDM)                                                                  [3] Energy efficiency (%)     DN diet (Mcal/kgDM) x E milk (Mcal/kgMilk) [SOURCE: Page_43]

In Equation 3, N milk, E milk, DE diet and DN diet are milk nitrogen content, milk energy [SOURCE: Page_43]

content, digestible energy content of the diet, and digestible protein content of the diet, [SOURCE: Page_43]

respectively. [SOURCE: Page_43]

Inter- and intrastudy correlations between energy and nitrogen efficiency [SOURCE: Page_43]

The correlation between energy and nitrogen efficiency was examined in two ways: inter- [SOURCE: Page_43]

and intra-study. The inter-study correlation was simply computed from the observed data of [SOURCE: Page_43]

energy and nitrogen efficiency, whereas the intrastudy correlation was the correlation after [SOURCE: Page_43]

30 [SOURCE: Page_43]

Chapter 3 – Meta-analysis [SOURCE: Page_44]

adjustment for the random effect of study. The adjustment for study was made independently for [SOURCE: Page_44]

energy and nitrogen efficiency, using in each case a mixed model with only the study factor [SOURCE: Page_44]

included as a random effect. The residuals of these two models represent the intra-study [SOURCE: Page_44]

variability in energy and nitrogen efficiency. [SOURCE: Page_44]

Statistical analysis [SOURCE: Page_44]

Because the factors that influence feed efficiency may be dependent upon each other, this [SOURCE: Page_44]

probably results in confounding or bias for later estimations of regression coefficients and [SOURCE: Page_44]

explanations (Silvey, 1969). The relationships among independent variables [i.e., NDF, ADF, [SOURCE: Page_44]

digestible CP (DCP), DE, PCO, DMI, MY, BW and DIM] were examined by scatterplot and [SOURCE: Page_44]

correlation matrix (Figure 3.1). Additionally, the residuals versus fitted plots of the models for [SOURCE: Page_44]

predicting energy efficiency, nitrogen efficiency and the Neff:Eeff ratio from only one predictor [SOURCE: Page_44]

were performed to check for any potential quadratic effect. [SOURCE: Page_44]

The effect of nutritional or animal variables, including NDF, ADF, DCP, DE, PCO, DMI, MY, [SOURCE: Page_44]

BW and DIM, on energy efficiency, nitrogen efficiency and the Neff:Eeff ratio was analyzed using [SOURCE: Page_44]

multiple linear regression. Milk yield and DMI were not included in the models for predicting [SOURCE: Page_44]

energy and nitrogen efficiency, but only in the model of the Neff:Eeff ratio, to avoid artifactual [SOURCE: Page_44]

correlations, as previously mentioned. Similarly, DE and DCP were not included in the model for [SOURCE: Page_44]

predicting the Neff:Eeff or in the separate models for predicting energy and nitrogen efficiency if [SOURCE: Page_44]

they appeared in the denominators of their efficiency calculation. Because the data used in this [SOURCE: Page_44]

meta-analysis were gathered from multiple studies conducted over multiple years and in many [SOURCE: Page_44]

different conditions, the study effect was considered to be random (St-Pierre, 2001). The random [SOURCE: Page_44]

effects were, therefore, incorporated into the estimation procedure using a mixed effect model. [SOURCE: Page_44]

The general form of the mixed model was: [SOURCE: Page_44]

Yij = B$_0$ + si + BkXijk + eij [SOURCE: Page_44]

where Yij is the dependent variable (energy efficiency, nitrogen efficiency, or the Neff:Eeff [SOURCE: Page_44]

ratio), B$_0$ is overall intercept across studies, si is the random effect of ith study, Bk is a vector of [SOURCE: Page_44]

regression coefficients of Y on the corresponding vector of predictors (Xijk), eij is the residual error, [SOURCE: Page_44]

which was assumed to be normally distributed with mean 0 and variance σ2e, i = 1…69 studies, j = [SOURCE: Page_44]

1…306 observations, and k =1 to 9 predictors. First, the full models including all nutritional and [SOURCE: Page_44]

animal variables were run. Second, the least significant variables were removed stepwise [SOURCE: Page_44]

according to the degree of significance until only significant variables remained. To reduce the [SOURCE: Page_44]

correlations between intercept and slope, and to make the intercept more meaningful, the [SOURCE: Page_44]

31 [SOURCE: Page_44]

Chapter 3 – Meta-analysis [SOURCE: Page_45]

prediction variables were centred on their means. The goodness of fit of the models was [SOURCE: Page_45]

evaluated internally using Akaike’s information criterion(Ngwerume and Mao, 1992). For [SOURCE: Page_45]

comparison across models, the coefficient of determination (R$_2$) was computed using the [SOURCE: Page_45]

following equation from Faraway (2009): [SOURCE: Page_45]

(yi - ŷi )2 R$_2$ =1- (yi - y)2 where yi is the ith observed value of efficiency, ŷi is the ith predicted value of efficiency and [SOURCE: Page_45]

y is the mean of efficiency, i = 1…306 observations. [SOURCE: Page_45]

To verify that the study effect was adequately accommodated by fitting it as a random effect, [SOURCE: Page_45]

the final model with random effect was compared to a model where the study was included as a [SOURCE: Page_45]

fixed effect. This model produced similar results to those of the model with a random study, and, [SOURCE: Page_45]

therefore, will not be further discussed. Mixed and fixed effect models were performed using [SOURCE: Page_45]

respectively the nlme and lm packages of the statistical software R version 2.15.1 (R Development [SOURCE: Page_45]

Core Team, 2012). [SOURCE: Page_45]

Results [SOURCE: Page_45]

Data description [SOURCE: Page_45]

The main ingredients, chemical composition of the investigated diets in this meta-analysis, [SOURCE: Page_45]

and their summary statistics are shown in Table 3.1. Corn silage and alfalfa silage were the [SOURCE: Page_45]

predominant forages, followed by grass silage. The main source of energy from concentrate was [SOURCE: Page_45]

corn, including ground corn and high moisture shelled corn. Soybean (solvent extracted, soybean [SOURCE: Page_45]

expeller, extruded and heat treated) was the main source of protein supplement. Corn gluten [SOURCE: Page_45]

meal, fish meal, blood and meat meal were the sources of rumen undegradable protein. Sugar [SOURCE: Page_45]

cane and sugar beet molasses were used as the supplementation of sugars/energy in supporting [SOURCE: Page_45]

microbial protein synthesis. Animal fat and urea were used as supplements of energy and non- [SOURCE: Page_45]

protein nitrogen respectively. The data set showed a large variation in the chemical composition [SOURCE: Page_45]

of the diets. Digestible protein averaged 11.08%, but ranged from 7.14 to 14.69%. Large variations [SOURCE: Page_45]

were also observed in NDF and ADF content, which ranged from 17.80 to 54.50% and from 8.40 [SOURCE: Page_45]

to 31.80%, respectively. The average digestible energy content was 3.16 Mcal/kg of DM. [SOURCE: Page_45]

Chapter 3 – Meta-analysis [SOURCE: Page_46]

energy content of diet. 5INRA (1988, 2010) and Sauvant et al. (2004). [SOURCE: Page_46]

Table 3.2 illustrates the performance of animals from the studies included in the database. [SOURCE: Page_46]

The average DMI was 23.18 kg of DM/d, with a range from 13.40 to 30.20 kg of DM/d. The [SOURCE: Page_46]

highest milk production was 43.28 kg/d, whereas 16.33 kg/d was the lowest recorded milk [SOURCE: Page_46]

production from an experiment with cows in late lactation (256 DIM). The earliest DIM recorded [SOURCE: Page_46]

was 15 d and the latest DIM was 256 d. Thirty-eight studies, totaling 153 observations, had DIM < [SOURCE: Page_46]

Correlation between nutritional and animal performance variables [SOURCE: Page_47]

Figure 3.1 shows the interstudy correlations between the dietary and animal variables that [SOURCE: Page_47]

were included in the subsequent analyses. It can be seen that fibre fractions (NDF and ADF) were [SOURCE: Page_47]

negatively associated with the other variables, including DE, PCO, DMI, MY, and DIM. The [SOURCE: Page_47]

digestible protein content of the diets was correlated with DE, PCO, MY, and DIM. Increased [SOURCE: Page_47]

digestible energy content of diets depressed DMI, but had a weaker correlation with milk [SOURCE: Page_47]

production. Moreover, a high energy-density diet was related to a large proportion of concentrate [SOURCE: Page_47]

(r = 0.42). A strong positive correlation between DMI and MY (r = 0.64) was found in our analysis, [SOURCE: Page_47]

which is consistent with the literature (Roseler et al., 1997; Martin and Sauvant, 2002; Voelker et [SOURCE: Page_47]

al., 2002). Positive correlations between BW and DMI or MY imply that bigger animals tended to [SOURCE: Page_47]

eat more feed and had a higher milk production. [SOURCE: Page_47]

The correlation between energy efficiency and nitrogen efficiency is shown in Figure 3.2. As [SOURCE: Page_48]

efficiency, a positive interstudy correlation existed between energy and nitrogen efficiency in the [SOURCE: Page_48]

unadjusted data (r = 0.62). However, Figure 3.2a shows that within experiments, an increase in [SOURCE: Page_48]

energy efficiency was not always associated with an increase in nitrogen efficiency (e.g., top [SOURCE: Page_48]

right). When the data are presented as intrastudy responses (i.e. they were corrected for the [SOURCE: Page_48]

Factors affecting energy efficiency, nitrogen efficiency and the Neff:Eeff ratio [SOURCE: Page_49]

The final models (i.e., including only significant variables) for predicting energy efficiency, [SOURCE: Page_49]

nitrogen efficiency and the Neff:Eeff ratio are shown in Table 3.3. Figures 3.3, 3.4, and 3.5 [SOURCE: Page_49]

illustrate the plots between observed and predicted values of the models predicting energy [SOURCE: Page_49]

efficiency, nitrogen efficiency and the Neff:Eeff ratio, respectively. The residual analysis of the [SOURCE: Page_49]

above models (plots not shown) was also performed to evaluate the model assumptions for the [SOURCE: Page_49]

residuals (for example, equal variance or normality). All final models show a good prediction, [SOURCE: Page_49]

with no discernible trend or pattern in the residuals. [SOURCE: Page_49]

Energy efficiency [SOURCE: Page_49]

Acid detergent fibre had a negative impact on energy efficiency (P < 0.05): an increase of 10% [SOURCE: Page_49]

of dietary ADF content reduced energy efficiency by 1.60%. In contrast, PCO affected energy [SOURCE: Page_49]

efficiency in a quadratic way (Table 3.3). The negative regression coefficient indicates that energy [SOURCE: Page_49]

efficiency improved with a small increase in PCO, but decreased once PCO increased further. [SOURCE: Page_49]

Digestible protein, BW, and DIM had no effect on energy efficiency (P > 0.05). At the mean values [SOURCE: Page_49]

of ADF and PCO, energy efficiency was approximately 34%. In addition, because a strong [SOURCE: Page_49]

association existed between NDF and ADF (r = 0.77), a comparison between a model using ADF [SOURCE: Page_49]

36 [SOURCE: Page_49]

Chapter 3 – Meta-analysis [SOURCE: Page_50]

and PCO and a model using NDF and PCO for predicting energy efficiency showed that the [SOURCE: Page_50]

latter produced a higher Akaike information criterion. Hence, the model including ADF and PCO [SOURCE: Page_50]

best predicted energy efficiency. [SOURCE: Page_50]

Nitrogen efficiency [SOURCE: Page_51]

Table 3.3 also indicates that increasing dietary ADF and DE contents were negatively [SOURCE: Page_51]

associated with nitrogen efficiency. Generally, a 10% increase in ADF or 1 Mcal increase in DE led [SOURCE: Page_51]

to a 5.60 and a 6.67% reduction in nitrogen efficiency, respectively. Nitrogen efficiency was not [SOURCE: Page_51]

significantly related to PCO, BW, or DIM. [SOURCE: Page_51]

Nitrogen Efficiency:Energy efficiency ratio [SOURCE: Page_51]

As can be seen from Table 3.3, the ratio between energy and nitrogen efficiency (Neff:Eeff) [SOURCE: Page_51]

was significantly associated with ADF and PCO. The slope decreased with the increase in dietary [SOURCE: Page_51]

ADF concentration, whereas it increased if the proportion of concentrate was increased. The [SOURCE: Page_51]

Neff:Eeff ratio was not significantly influenced by DMI, BW, DIM, or MY. [SOURCE: Page_51]

Chapter 3 – Meta-analysis [SOURCE: Page_53]

Discussion [SOURCE: Page_53]

The diets included in our study comprised a large variety of feeds. Major ingredients, [SOURCE: Page_53]

however, were corn silage, alfalfa silage, grass silage, corn grain, and soybean meal. Corn silage [SOURCE: Page_53]

and grass silage are often fed to dairy cows in European countries, in combination with barley or [SOURCE: Page_53]

corn grain and soybean meal, whereas in America, grass silage is normally replaced by alfalfa [SOURCE: Page_53]

silage or alfalfa hay (Huhtanen and Hristov, 2009). The main breed of dairy cows in the present [SOURCE: Page_53]

database was Holstein-Friesian. Moreover, it should be noticed that approximately 70% of data in [SOURCE: Page_53]

this study came from North America. The results from this meta-analysis are, therefore, most [SOURCE: Page_53]

applicable to Holstein cows fed corn silage, combined with alfalfa silage/hay or grass silage, corn [SOURCE: Page_53]

grain and soybean meal-based diets. [SOURCE: Page_53]

Regarding the chemical composition of the diets, the data showed a large variation in [SOURCE: Page_53]

protein, energy, and fibre fractions. The average concentration of DCP was 11.08%. In the [SOURCE: Page_53]

literature, most experiments report crude protein (Colmenero and Broderick, 2006; Brito and [SOURCE: Page_53]

Broderick, 2007; Cabrita et al., 2007; Broderick et al., 2009) and metabolizable protein (Blouin et [SOURCE: Page_53]

al., 2002; Raggio et al., 2004; Wang et al., 2007), rather than digestible protein. In terms of crude [SOURCE: Page_53]

protein, the range in our study is in agreement with Huhtanen and Hristov (2009). In their meta- [SOURCE: Page_53]

analysis of 425 feeding trials, dietary CP concentrations of 17.80 and 16.50% were reported for [SOURCE: Page_53]

North America and Northern Europe, respectively. The average concentrations of NDF and ADF [SOURCE: Page_53]

were 30.89 and 18.70%, respectively. Similar results have been found in other meta-analyses [SOURCE: Page_53]

(Hristov et al., 2004; Ferraretto et al., 2013). The average value of DE was 3.16 Mcal/kg of DM, this [SOURCE: Page_53]

is consistent with the report of Hristov et al. (2005), on the basis of net energy for lactation (NEL), [SOURCE: Page_53]

in which lactating cows received an average 37.9 Mcal NEL/day. [SOURCE: Page_53]

The efficiencies of digestible energy and nitrogen usage of lactating dairy cows were found [SOURCE: Page_53]

to be on average 34.09 and 42.63%, respectively, indicating approximately 60% losses of energy [SOURCE: Page_53]

and nitrogen, as gases, urine or heat increment. VandeHaar and St-Pierre (2006) reported a value [SOURCE: Page_53]

of energy efficiency varying from 17 to 20.5%, for cows from first to third lactation, when [SOURCE: Page_53]

calculated on a gross energy basis. Nitrogen efficiencies, computed as protein in milk/ crude [SOURCE: Page_53]

protein intake, ranging from 26 to 30% have been commonly reported (Castillo et al., 2001; Brito [SOURCE: Page_53]

and Broderick, 2006; Broderick et al., 2009). Tamminga and Verstegen (1996) indicated that, [SOURCE: Page_53]

generally, 70% of nitrogen intake is excreted in feces and urine. The difference between digestive [SOURCE: Page_53]

and gross efficiencies of energy and nitrogen usage can be attributed to the fecal losses. The [SOURCE: Page_53]

inefficiency of digestible energy and nitrogen usage is normally related to the presence of [SOURCE: Page_53]

40 [SOURCE: Page_53]

Chapter 3 – Meta-analysis [SOURCE: Page_54]

indigestible fibre (West et al., 1997) and the imbalance in the carbohydrate:protein ratio in the [SOURCE: Page_54]

rumen (Castillo et al., 2001). This imbalance impairs the rate of microbial synthesis, resulting in a [SOURCE: Page_54]

low rate of fibre and protein digestion, and consequently a high rate of ammonia being excreted [SOURCE: Page_54]

into the environment (Kebreab et al., 2001). A diet with an adequate energy-to-protein ratio is, [SOURCE: Page_54]

therefore, of importance to maximize animal efficiency (Hoover and Stokes, 1991). In the present [SOURCE: Page_54]

study, experiments with low energy and nitrogen efficiency were generally related to either high [SOURCE: Page_54]

NDF (≈ 40% of DM; da Silva et al., 2007; Vander Pol et al., 2008) or high DCP (≈ 13.24% of DM; [SOURCE: Page_54]

Broderick, 1992; Wattiaux et al., 1994; Groff and Wu, 2005). Additionally, the habit of feeding [SOURCE: Page_54]

dairy cows high dietary protein to ensure maximal milk production could also be a cause of such [SOURCE: Page_54]

a low nitrogen efficiency (VandeHaar and St-Pierre, 2006). Indeed, the economic cost in the form [SOURCE: Page_54]

of milk lost due to underfeeding protein is far more than the cost of overfeeding protein as a [SOURCE: Page_54]

margin of safety. In case of an excess of dietary protein, the nitrogen usage efficiency decreases [SOURCE: Page_54]

sharply and a significant amount of N is lost through feces, gases, and urine (Tamminga, 1992). [SOURCE: Page_54]

Therefore, research efforts should concentrate on finding ways to produce high quantity and [SOURCE: Page_54]

quality of milk with as low a dietary protein as possible. [SOURCE: Page_54]

Nutritional factors affecting energy and nitrogen efficiency [SOURCE: Page_54]

Various nutritional factors have been cited in the literature that affect feed efficiency: for [SOURCE: Page_54]

example, forage:concentrate ratio (Aguerre et al., 2011), carbohydrate:protein ratio (Aldrich et al., [SOURCE: Page_54]

1993), protein concentration (Cabrita et al., 2007; Broderick et al., 2009), and supplement of rumen [SOURCE: Page_54]

undegradable protein (Broderick et al., 2008). Our meta-analysis shows that ADF and PCO were [SOURCE: Page_54]

significantly associated with energy efficiency, whereas nitrogen efficiency was significantly [SOURCE: Page_54]

associated with ADF and DE. [SOURCE: Page_54]

The negative effect of ADF on FE could be attributed to a decreasing the availability of [SOURCE: Page_54]

digested nutrients for animals fed diets containing high fibre (Allen, 2000; Tjardes et al., 2002). [SOURCE: Page_54]

Highly fibrous feeds tend to fill up the rumen quickly, but because they are slowly digested and [SOURCE: Page_54]

passed, they limit DMI, milk production, and hence FE (Yang and Beauchemin, 2006). Moreover, [SOURCE: Page_54]

highly fibrous feeds contain a limited amount of energy to support microbial growth/synthesis [SOURCE: Page_54]

(Jetana et al., 2000). A diet containing an appropriate amount of fibre, however, is of importance [SOURCE: Page_54]

to avoid rumen acidosis and to maximize microbial growth, rumen health, and subsequently [SOURCE: Page_54]

animal efficiency. These considerations would suggest a nonlinear relationship between ADF and [SOURCE: Page_54]

energy and nitrogen efficiency. However, the residual plots of the models, using ADF [SOURCE: Page_54]

individually for predicting energy and nitrogen efficiency (Figures not shown), do not support [SOURCE: Page_54]

41 [SOURCE: Page_54]

Chapter 3 – Meta-analysis [SOURCE: Page_55]

this idea, since no discernible pattern could be observed. It would be expected that, in a low-PCO [SOURCE: Page_55]

diet, an increasing PCO improves energy efficiency by increasing the available energy to support [SOURCE: Page_55]

microbial synthesis, but a too high-PCO diet might result in acidosis, which impairs animal [SOURCE: Page_55]

performance and energy efficiency. Feeding cows with high-PCO diets may also alter nutrient [SOURCE: Page_55]

partitioning (i.e., more to adipose tissues) and subsequently reduces energy efficiency, as [SOURCE: Page_55]

computed in the current study. However, Table 3.3 indicates a linearly negative correlation [SOURCE: Page_55]

between PCO and energy efficiency in the full model. Although ADF and PCO are to some extent [SOURCE: Page_55]

correlated (r = 0.2), we suspected that they were reflecting different aspects: ADF indexing the [SOURCE: Page_55]

digestible energy content of the diet, and PCO indexing the risk of acidosis. Therefore, to separate [SOURCE: Page_55]

the effect of ADF and PCO on energy efficiency, we decided to split the data based on ADF. The [SOURCE: Page_55]

idea was to isolate a part where the likelihood of rumen acidosis is very low (i.e., high ADF), and [SOURCE: Page_55]

a part with high risk of acidosis (i.e., low ADF). Such a splitting allowed us to look at the effect of [SOURCE: Page_55]

PCO on energy efficiency in these two situations, and it revealed that a significant negative effect [SOURCE: Page_55]

of PCO on energy efficiency was only present in the low-ADF (≤21%) subset of data (Figure 3.6). [SOURCE: Page_55]

This motivated us to incorporate a curvilinear effect of PCO on energy efficiency in the model, [SOURCE: Page_55]

which was done by including a quadratic term. Statistically, such a negative regression coefficient [SOURCE: Page_55]

of PCO-squared in Table 3.3 implies that energy efficiency was positively affected by low PCO, [SOURCE: Page_55]

but negatively affected by high PCO. Agle et al. (2010) observed a decrease in FE, computed as [SOURCE: Page_55]

NEL, in high- versus low-PCO diets: 58.3 and 62.9%, respectively. Feeding cows with high PCO [SOURCE: Page_55]

(e.g., 65-70% of DM), might result in a depression of fibre digestibility and fat yield (Moorby et [SOURCE: Page_55]

al., 2006). The current study shows that a high PCO in the diet negatively affected energy [SOURCE: Page_55]

efficiency, which could be the consequence of acidosis (VandeHaar and St-Pierre, 2006). [SOURCE: Page_55]

However, in a diet with low concentrate (i.e., high fibre) content, energy efficiency was [SOURCE: Page_55]

negatively and dominantly affected by ADF. Our study also indicates that feeding dairy cows [SOURCE: Page_55]

with high PCO diets resulted in no significant improvement of nitrogen efficiency. This results is [SOURCE: Page_55]

consistent with Agle et al. (2010), who reported an only marginally improved nitrogen efficiency [SOURCE: Page_55]

when PCO increased from 20 to 60%. [SOURCE: Page_55]

It is important to also be aware that feeding dairy cows a high-PCO diet might alter the [SOURCE: Page_55]

partition of nutrients. A high-digestible-carbohydrate diet is expected to result in an increased [SOURCE: Page_55]

production of propionate in the rumen (Sutton et al., 2003; Friggens et al., 2004). Propionate is [SOURCE: Page_55]

absorbed and metabolized by the liver to glucose and triggers an insulin response (Ingvartsen [SOURCE: Page_55]

and Andersen, 2000), which subsequently stimulates the lipogenesis and inhibits the release of [SOURCE: Page_55]

fatty acid from adipose tissue (lipolysis; Chilliard et al., 2000). Thus, feeding animals with a high- [SOURCE: Page_55]

42 [SOURCE: Page_55]

Chapter 3 – Meta-analysis [SOURCE: Page_56]

PCO diet may result in more nutrients being partitioned to body tissue rather than milk [SOURCE: Page_56]

production, and a decrease in efficiencies (calculated as milk/feed). [SOURCE: Page_56]

Relationship between energy and nitrogen efficiency [SOURCE: Page_56]

Acid detergent fibre and PCO were the only significant feed factors affecting the Neff:Eeff [SOURCE: Page_56]

ratio, which is consistent with the separate regressions for each efficiency independently. This [SOURCE: Page_56]

suggests that, from a nutritional point of view, a model to predict energy efficiency could be used [SOURCE: Page_56]

to predict nitrogen efficiency without the need to building two separate models (i.e., nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_56]

efficiency could be considered as an add-on in a model of energy-based efficiency). The routine [SOURCE: Page_56]

correlation between these two efficiencies [0.62 in the present study and 0.90 in Wheadon et al. [SOURCE: Page_56]

(2012)] is, in part, due to the presence of both MY and DMI in the calculation of both efficiencies. [SOURCE: Page_56]

However, it is also in part attributable to the phenomenon of dilution of maintenance, as both [SOURCE: Page_56]

energy and nitrogen requirements exist for maintenance. Our study further consolidates this by [SOURCE: Page_56]

showing that both efficiencies are affected by the same nutritional factors. [SOURCE: Page_56]

Animal factors affecting energy and nitrogen efficiency [SOURCE: Page_56]

As stated by Blake and Custodio (1984), FE depends not only on diets but also on the [SOURCE: Page_56]

genotypes, physiological states of the animals, and their possible interaction. There are clear [SOURCE: Page_56]

differences in nutrient partitioning and FE between genotypes of cows (e.g., high- versus low- [SOURCE: Page_56]

genetic merit), stages of lactation, and parities (Veerkamp, 1998; Dillon et al., 2003; Friggens et al., [SOURCE: Page_56]

2011). However, Table 3.3 shows that the current type of transverse database obtained from the [SOURCE: Page_56]

literature is not appropriate to fully incorporate the effect of animal factors on FE. Body weight [SOURCE: Page_56]

and DIM were the only factors possible to include (with the exception of MY and DMI, which [SOURCE: Page_56]

were purposely excluded). Indeed, most of the studies in the literature focus on the effect of [SOURCE: Page_56]

nutritional factors (e.g., dietary protein, fibre concentration, dietary energy concentration; Castillo [SOURCE: Page_56]

et al., 2001; Colmenero and Broderick, 2006; Yang and Beauchemin, 2006), with little focus on the [SOURCE: Page_56]

effects of breed, parity, body weight, or lactation period on energy and nitrogen efficiency (Britt [SOURCE: Page_56]

et al., 2003; Prendiville et al., 2009). Other types of data (e.g., longitudinal) would be more [SOURCE: Page_56]

appropriate to look at the effects of animal factors on FE. [SOURCE: Page_56]

In the present study, we found no significant effect of BW on energy or nitrogen efficiency. [SOURCE: Page_56]

The significant correlations between BW, DMI, and MY (Table 3.3) indicate that larger animals [SOURCE: Page_56]

ate more feed and produced more milk. However, they also had a higher maintenance [SOURCE: Page_56]

requirement, which could explain the insignificant effect of BW on FE. Veerkamp (1998) pointed [SOURCE: Page_56]

out that if two cows of equal BW are compared, the cow with 25% more MY will have a 10 to 15% [SOURCE: Page_56]

43 [SOURCE: Page_56]

Chapter 3 – Meta-analysis [SOURCE: Page_57]

greater FE, whereas if two cows of equal MY are compared, the cow with 25% less BW will have [SOURCE: Page_57]

a 10 to 12% higher FE. Thus, selection for smaller animals that have the same MY may represent a [SOURCE: Page_57]

strategy for improving FE and reducing the environmental impact. Contrary to previous reports [SOURCE: Page_57]

(Veerkamp, 1998; Britt et al., 2003), the current meta-analysis found no significant effect of DIM [SOURCE: Page_57]

on FE. This probably reflects the shortage of data in early or late lactation, although it is known [SOURCE: Page_57]

cows mobilize body reserves in early lactation to support milk production which affects the [SOURCE: Page_57]

results of FE. Cows that mobilize more body reserves in early lactation seem to be more efficient [SOURCE: Page_57]

because the usual definition of FE does not consider nutrients from such a mobilization (Connor [SOURCE: Page_57]

et al., 2011). However, high rates of mobilization have been associated with reduced longevity of [SOURCE: Page_57]

animals (Rauw et al., 1998) probably via increased problems of fertility (Pryce et al., 2004; Berry et [SOURCE: Page_57]

al., 2008), and health (Oltenacu and Algers, 2005), which would reduce the benefit of improved [SOURCE: Page_57]

FE. Thus, a better predictive capability and a biological understanding are needed of how a cow [SOURCE: Page_57]

partitions her nutritional resources between life functions. Indeed, if FE is considered over [SOURCE: Page_57]

lifespan of animals, then the extent to which nutrients partitioned to the vital functions such as [SOURCE: Page_57]

health and fertility, is important (Friggens et al., 2011). [SOURCE: Page_57]

Conclusions [SOURCE: Page_57]

This meta-analysis shows that ADF, PCO, and DE were the common nutritional factors [SOURCE: Page_57]

affecting feed efficiency. The ratio between energy and nitrogen efficiency, however, was affected [SOURCE: Page_57]

by ADF and PCO content of feed only. The strong correlation between energy and nitrogen [SOURCE: Page_57]

efficiency results indicates a possibility to maximize feed efficiency in terms of both energy and [SOURCE: Page_57]

nitrogen at the same time. In other words, an improvement in energy efficiency would also mean [SOURCE: Page_57]

an improvement in nitrogen efficiency. The current study also shows that these types of [SOURCE: Page_57]

transverse data are not sufficient to study the effect of animal factors on feed efficiency. [SOURCE: Page_57]

Longitudinal measurements per animal would be more appropriate. [SOURCE: Page_57]

Chapter 4 – Individual variability [SOURCE: Page_72]

Chapter 4 [SOURCE: Page_72]

Deriving estimates of individual variability in genetic potentials of [SOURCE: Page_72]

performance traits for three dairy breeds, using a model of [SOURCE: Page_72]

lifetime nutrient partitioning [SOURCE: Page_72]

H. N. Phuong,†‣ O. Martin†‣, I. J. M. de Boer‡, K.L. Ingvartsen•, Ph. Schmidely†‣, [SOURCE: Page_72]

France [SOURCE: Page_72]

59 [SOURCE: Page_72]

Chapter 4 – Individual variability [SOURCE: Page_73]

Abstract [SOURCE: Page_73]

This study explored the ability of an existing lifetime nutrient partitioning model for [SOURCE: Page_73]

simulating individual variability in genetic potentials of dairy cows. Data of 102 cows including [SOURCE: Page_73]

180 lactations of three breeds: Danish Red, Danish Holstein, and Jersey were utilized. Individual [SOURCE: Page_73]

cow performance records through sequential lactations were used to derive genetic scaling [SOURCE: Page_73]

parameters for each animal by calibrating the model to achieve best fit, cow by cow. The model [SOURCE: Page_73]

was able to fit individual curves of body weight, and milk fat, milk protein, and milk lactose [SOURCE: Page_73]

concentrations with a high degree of accuracy. Daily milk yield and dry matter intake were [SOURCE: Page_73]

satisfactorily predicted in early- and mid-lactation but under-predictions were found in late- [SOURCE: Page_73]

lactation. Breeds and parities did not significantly affect the prediction accuracy. The means of [SOURCE: Page_73]

genetic scaling parameters between Danish Red and Danish Holstein were similar but [SOURCE: Page_73]

significantly different from those of Jersey. The extent of correlations between the genetic scaling [SOURCE: Page_73]

parameters was consistent with that reported in the literature. In conclusion, this model is of [SOURCE: Page_73]

value as a tool to derive estimates of genetic potentials of milk yield, milk composition, body [SOURCE: Page_73]

reserve usage, and growth for different genotypes of cow. Moreover, it can be used to separate [SOURCE: Page_73]

genetic variability in performance between individual cows from environmental noise. The [SOURCE: Page_73]

model enables simulation of the effects of a genetic selection strategy on lifetime efficiency of [SOURCE: Page_73]

individual cows which has a main advantage of including the rearing costs, and thus, can be used [SOURCE: Page_73]

to explore the impact of future selection on animal performance and efficiency. [SOURCE: Page_73]

Key words: nutrient partitioning, dairy cow, genetic variability, dynamic model. [SOURCE: Page_73]

60 [SOURCE: Page_73]

Chapter 4 – Individual variability [SOURCE: Page_74]

Introduction [SOURCE: Page_74]

Milk production efficiency is affected by the way in which dairy cows partition obtained [SOURCE: Page_74]

nutrients between life functions. Generally, an efficient cow should allocate a greater proportion [SOURCE: Page_74]

of nutrients towards the mammary gland, for milk synthesis (Linn et al., 2009). This traditional [SOURCE: Page_74]

view of efficiency is based on the concept of dilution of maintenance, i.e. when a cow eats more [SOURCE: Page_74]

feed to support milk production a smaller proportion is used for maintenance, and it is thus more [SOURCE: Page_74]

efficient (Bauman et al., 1985). However, if efficiency is considered over the lifespan of an animal [SOURCE: Page_74]

the extent to which nutrients are partitioned to vital functions such as health and fertility is [SOURCE: Page_74]

important, since the longer the productive life the smaller is the rearing phase as a proportion, [SOURCE: Page_74]

and thus greater the lifetime efficiency. These vital functions are not only related to economic [SOURCE: Page_74]

(Collard et al., 2000) and environmental issues (Garnsworthy, 2004), but animal welfare (Oltenacu [SOURCE: Page_74]

and Algers, 2005). Thus, being able to predict the nutrient partitioning process of cows can help [SOURCE: Page_74]

improve animal efficiency and farm profitability, and reduce environmental impact. [SOURCE: Page_74]

Pioneering works of Baldwin et al. (1987a; b) and Oldham and Emmans (1989) show that [SOURCE: Page_74]

mathematical models can be used to explore concepts and hypotheses, regarding nutrient [SOURCE: Page_74]

partitioning in dairy cows, and have potential for predicting long-term efficiency (Dumas et al., [SOURCE: Page_74]

2008; Friggens et al., 2013). There are clear genetic differences in nutrient partitioning between [SOURCE: Page_74]

cows (Yan et al., 2006) and physiological states (Kirkland and Gordon, 2001). Thus, prediction of [SOURCE: Page_74]

nutrient partitioning should consider genetic variability between cows and differences in [SOURCE: Page_74]

expression of their genotypes through time. By capturing these ideas, the model of Martin and [SOURCE: Page_74]

Sauvant (2010a) has shown a great ability to simulate the average pattern of dynamic partitioning [SOURCE: Page_74]

of energy intake and performance through lifespan of cows of different yield levels, and thus [SOURCE: Page_74]

allows genetic variability to be incorporated in prediction of lifetime efficiency. This model [SOURCE: Page_74]

assumes a general trajectory of dynamic partitioning of a cow’s relative priority between life [SOURCE: Page_74]

functions, and genetic scaling parameters are then incorporated to scale individual performance, [SOURCE: Page_74]

and to simulate variability between genotypes in, for example, milk production. In other words, [SOURCE: Page_74]

it assumes that performance of animals differ only in terms of scaling but not in terms of the [SOURCE: Page_74]

dynamic shape. [SOURCE: Page_74]

At this moment, however, the appropriateness of the model to realistically simulate [SOURCE: Page_74]

individual variability in nutrient partitioning trajectories is untested. Though the model shows a [SOURCE: Page_74]

good fit to the mean values of various population data from literature (Schutz et al., 1990; [SOURCE: Page_74]

Hoffman, 1997) the extent to which the assumption of one general trajectory of dynamic [SOURCE: Page_74]

61 [SOURCE: Page_74]

Chapter 4 – Individual variability [SOURCE: Page_75]

partitioning of relative priority still holds when comparing different genotypes should be [SOURCE: Page_75]

validated. Does the trajectory of dynamic partitioning of relative priority differ between breeds [SOURCE: Page_75]

kept in the same environment? It is also important to evaluate the individual variation in genetic [SOURCE: Page_75]

scaling parameters and their possible correlations and factors affecting them. Such information is [SOURCE: Page_75]

necessary for setting up realistic population simulations and, in the wider context, for using the [SOURCE: Page_75]

model to simulate the effects on lifetime efficiency of possible selection and management [SOURCE: Page_75]

strategies. [SOURCE: Page_75]

The aim of this study was, therefore, to examine the model of Martin and Sauvant (2010a) for [SOURCE: Page_75]

its capability of simulating individual variability in genetic potentials of milk yield, milk [SOURCE: Page_75]

composition, growth, and body reserves usage of different genotypes of cow. [SOURCE: Page_75]

Materials and Methods [SOURCE: Page_75]

For clarity, it should be noted that this paper dealt with the situation where cows are [SOURCE: Page_75]

managed in a non-constraint environment (i.e. unlimited access to a high quality feed, water or [SOURCE: Page_75]

neutral ambient temperature), and thus, the performance profiles that a given cow would achieve [SOURCE: Page_75]

in such an environment is referred to as genetic potential, in accordance with literature [SOURCE: Page_75]

definitions of genetic potential (e.g. Oldham and Emmans, 1989). [SOURCE: Page_75]

Teleonomic model (GARUNS) [SOURCE: Page_75]

This section presents a brief introduction to the teleonomic model of Martin and Sauvant [SOURCE: Page_75]

(2010a). This teleonomic model, referred to here as GARUNS, consists of a regulating sub-model [SOURCE: Page_75]

providing a driving force to control the function of an operating sub-model. An adapted scheme [SOURCE: Page_75]

of GARUNS is presented in Figure 4.1. The regulating sub-model describes the dynamic [SOURCE: Page_75]

partitioning of a female mammal’s priority between life functions: growth (G), ageing (A), [SOURCE: Page_75]

regaining of body reserves (R), and energy supply of the unborn (U), newborn (N), and suckling [SOURCE: Page_75]

(S) calf over a lifespan. This dynamic pattern of priorities is assumed to be general for all [SOURCE: Page_75]

individual cows. The sum of these relative priorities is always equal to one. The operating sub- [SOURCE: Page_75]

model uses the relative priorities to partition energy intake between foetal growth, body weight [SOURCE: Page_75]

and body composition, milk yield, and milk composition over the lifespan and during repeated [SOURCE: Page_75]

reproductive cycles. Genetic scaling parameters are incorporated in the operating sub-model to [SOURCE: Page_75]

scale individual performance potentials of, for example, mature body size or milk production. It [SOURCE: Page_75]

should be noted that these genetic scaling parameters are not simply related to the widely [SOURCE: Page_75]

reported breeding values commonly used in the animal genetics literature. They act as [SOURCE: Page_75]

62 [SOURCE: Page_75]

Chapter 4 – Individual variability [SOURCE: Page_76]

multipliers on the different dynamic priorities and thus provide the means to create different [SOURCE: Page_76]

levels of genetic potential performance for different cows. In this context, the variation in genetic [SOURCE: Page_76]

scaling parameters describes the variation in genetic potential between animals. In the model, an [SOURCE: Page_76]

animal is described in terms of its genotype for growth, capacity to store and mobilize reserves, [SOURCE: Page_76]

and milk production using the following genetic scaling parameters: non-labile body mass at [SOURCE: Page_76]

maturity (WM), rate of regaining body reserves per day (b^0), labile body mass mobilization index [SOURCE: Page_76]

(νX), peak milk yield potential (νy), milk fat secretion (νF), milk protein secretion (νP), and milk [SOURCE: Page_76]

lactose secretion (νL). The unit of WM is kilogram, whereas the remaining parameters are [SOURCE: Page_76]

fractions, and therefore, have no unit. Thus, to describe each genotype a specific set of these [SOURCE: Page_76]

genetic scaling parameters is needed. External triggers of successful insemination (i.e. [SOURCE: Page_76]

conception) drive the changes between non-reproductive and reproductive states of the animal [SOURCE: Page_76]

cueing in the dynamic priority trajectories described in the regulating sub-model. As shown in [SOURCE: Page_76]

Figure 4.1, the combination between dynamic priority and genetic scaling parameters makes it [SOURCE: Page_76]

possible to quantify the total amount of energy required to fulfil all functions (ΣE). The resulting [SOURCE: Page_76]

dry matter intake (DMI) is quantified by multiplying ΣE with the predetermined dietary energy [SOURCE: Page_76]

density (eD). The coefficients of energy partition to different life functions (EG/ΣE, ER/ΣE, EMY/ΣE, [SOURCE: Page_76]

where EG, ER, EMY are energy required for growth, reserves storage, and milk production, [SOURCE: Page_76]

respectively) are the result of the interactions between dynamic partitioning priorities and genetic [SOURCE: Page_76]

scaling parameters. [SOURCE: Page_76]

Animal data [SOURCE: Page_76]

Data used in this study were obtained from an experiment conducted at Aarhus University, [SOURCE: Page_76]

Denmark from January 1996 to October 2001. The experiment has been described in detail in [SOURCE: Page_76]

Nielsen et al. (2003). Briefly, three dairy cow breeds, i.e. Danish Red, Danish Holstein and Jersey, [SOURCE: Page_76]

were used. In total, there were 102 cows, including 180 lactations suitable for the present study. [SOURCE: Page_76]

Cows were kept in a neutral ambient temperature and were fed the same total mixed ration ad [SOURCE: Page_76]

libitum throughout lactation with an energy density that was assumed to allow the cows to fully [SOURCE: Page_76]

express their genetic potentials (i.e. non-constraining). The ingredients and chemical composition [SOURCE: Page_76]

of the diet were described in Nielsen et al. (2003). The average dry matter content of the diet [SOURCE: Page_76]

(DM) was 52% and the metabolizable energy density of the diet (eD) was 11.1 MJ/kg of DM. Cows [SOURCE: Page_76]

were milked twice daily, whereas milk yield and milk composition were recorded at each [SOURCE: Page_76]

milking. All animals were weighed on day 2, 3, and 8 after calving and subsequently, once a [SOURCE: Page_76]

week until 3 months after calving. From 3 months after calving towards the dry period they were [SOURCE: Page_76]

63 [SOURCE: Page_76]

Chapter 4 – Individual variability [SOURCE: Page_77]

weighed every two weeks and once a week during the dry period. The health status of cows was [SOURCE: Page_77]

also recorded (day of treatment, pre- and post-period of treatment, and healthy periods). The [SOURCE: Page_77]

cows were housed throughout the year in single tie stalls. [SOURCE: Page_77]

Figure 4.1. Schematic diagram of the GARUNS model [adapted with permission of Martin and [SOURCE: Page_77]

Sauvant (2010)], showing how energy requirements for different life functions (growth, body reserves, [SOURCE: Page_77]

and milk) are derived from genetic scaling parameters combined with lifetime profiles of relative [SOURCE: Page_77]

priorities for the different life functions. The priorities for foetal growth, ageing (in regulating sub- [SOURCE: Page_77]

model), and energy needed for foetal growth (in operating sub-model) are not presented as they are [SOURCE: Page_77]

assumed to be common trajectories for every individual. Total energy needed is the sum of the [SOURCE: Page_77]

different requirements, from which DM intake can be calculated assuming an appropriate diet energy [SOURCE: Page_77]

density (eD). [SOURCE: Page_77]

Model fitting [SOURCE: Page_77]

GARUNS uses the description of an animal in terms of its genetic scaling parameter and its [SOURCE: Page_77]

relative priorities to allow calculation of the amount of energy required to meet its performance [SOURCE: Page_77]

potentials. Given predetermined characteristics of diet, i.e. energy density and dry matter [SOURCE: Page_77]

content, this average requirement can be converted to an estimate of DMI. The starting point of [SOURCE: Page_77]

this approach is potential performance, and it can be termed as “potential output drives intake” [SOURCE: Page_77]

(Emmans and Kyriazakis, 2001), which is opposite to the approach described by, for instance, [SOURCE: Page_77]

Baldwin et al. (1987a; 1987b) where the starting point is a given feed intake. It is, therefore, [SOURCE: Page_77]

64 [SOURCE: Page_77]

Chapter 4 – Individual variability [SOURCE: Page_78]

reasonable to fit the model with observed BW and MY, and consider DMI as the consequence. [SOURCE: Page_78]

Moreover, since GARUNS assumes a general trajectory of dynamic partitioning according to the [SOURCE: Page_78]

relative priority to different life functions, we hypothesized no systematic difference between [SOURCE: Page_78]

predicted and observed animal performances. [SOURCE: Page_78]

The inputs of GARUNS are classified as: reproductive timing [age at first conception (PCI 0), [SOURCE: Page_78]

and parturition to conception interval (PCIc), where c is reproductive cycle number], and dietary [SOURCE: Page_78]

characteristics (eD and DM). PCIc, eD and DM were obtained directly from the database. No [SOURCE: Page_78]

records on body weight (BW), PCI$_0$, and pregnancy length were available until first calving in the [SOURCE: Page_78]

current database. It was, therefore, necessary to assume that all cows have the same age at first [SOURCE: Page_78]

conception and a standard pregnancy length, and thus age at first calving. In other words, we [SOURCE: Page_78]

adopted the shape of BW curve during the growing period of GARUNS, which has been [SOURCE: Page_78]

previously calibrated using the GARUNS’s database (Martin and Sauvant, 2010a). This is justified [SOURCE: Page_78]

because the model is designed to accommodate differences in growth performance between [SOURCE: Page_78]

individual cows only in terms of scaling and not in terms of the shape of the performance profile. [SOURCE: Page_78]

In this context, the scaling difference in BW of different animals is modulated by W M. In terms of [SOURCE: Page_78]

milk production, it was hypothesized by Martin and Sauvant (2010a) that peak milk yield of a [SOURCE: Page_78]

cow increases linearly by parity for all cows. However, it seemed reasonable to examine this [SOURCE: Page_78]

assumption in the present dataset. To address this issue a new parameter (ν ypi) was introduced to [SOURCE: Page_78]

modulate the priority for milk production independently in each parity, where i is parity. ν ypi is [SOURCE: Page_78]

relative deviation of priority for milk production in parity i from the lifetime ν y. It was assumed [SOURCE: Page_78]

that no deviation happens in first parity, i.e. νyp^1 is equal to 1. In this context, the priority for milk [SOURCE: Page_78]

production in parity i (νyi) is equal to νypi multiplied by νy. DMI is the function of total energy [SOURCE: Page_78]

requirement (ΣE) and a predetermined eD. [SOURCE: Page_78]

Model fitting to individual data [SOURCE: Page_78]

To derive individual genetic scaling parameters, the model was used to adjust these [SOURCE: Page_78]

parameter values for each cow so as to achieve best fit with the observed performance data [SOURCE: Page_78]

through sequential lactations of that cow. This calibration process was done individually, cow by [SOURCE: Page_78]

cow, and once the best fit was obtained the genetic scaling parameters were recorded for that [SOURCE: Page_78]

cow. It should be noted that the data used for estimating these genetic scaling parameters were [SOURCE: Page_78]

different from the one used to develop and parameterize GARUNS. The model calibration was [SOURCE: Page_78]

implemented and optimized with the Modelmaker software version 3.0 (Cherwell Scientific Ltd, [SOURCE: Page_78]

2000), using the Runge-Kutta 4 numerical integration procedure and a fixed integration step of 1 [SOURCE: Page_78]

65 [SOURCE: Page_78]

Chapter 4 – Individual variability [SOURCE: Page_79]

day. The numerical values of the genetic scaling parameters were estimated for each individual [SOURCE: Page_79]

cow data series (from first to last parity) using a least square procedure performed with the [SOURCE: Page_79]

simplex algorithm of Modelmaker to derive best fit parameters, with default parameter values [SOURCE: Page_79]

and 100 convergence steps. [SOURCE: Page_79]

Model evaluation [SOURCE: Page_79]

The ability of the GARUNS model to fit observed data was evaluated using the root mean [SOURCE: Page_79]

square error (RMSE), computed as follows: [SOURCE: Page_79]

1 N RMSE =             N i=1 (Oi - Pi ) 2 [SOURCE: Page_79]

where Oi and Pi are the ith observed and predicted values of BW, milk yield (MY), DMI, milk [SOURCE: Page_79]

fat concentration (MCF), milk protein concentration (MCP), and milk lactose concentration [SOURCE: Page_79]

(MCL), and N is the number of observations. RMSE was calculated for each day in milk (DIM) [SOURCE: Page_79]

across the N cows within each parity. The relative prediction error (RPE) was then computed as [SOURCE: Page_79]

100×RMSE/(mean of the observed data). Values of RPE less than 10%, between 10 – 20%, and [SOURCE: Page_79]

more than 20% indicate good, moderate, and poor prediction, respectively (Fuentes-Pila et al., [SOURCE: Page_79]

1996). [SOURCE: Page_79]

In order to examine the GARUNS assumption that the trajectory of dynamic partitioning of [SOURCE: Page_79]

relative priority between life functions is general for all individuals, irrespective of breeds, mixed [SOURCE: Page_79]

effect models were used. We hypothesized, if the assumption is adequate, that the slope of the [SOURCE: Page_79]

regression between the residuals of prediction, (i.e., Oi – Pi), versus day from calving should not [SOURCE: Page_79]

be significantly different from zero, nor should there be any breed effect on the slope. The general [SOURCE: Page_79]

form of the mixed model was: [SOURCE: Page_79]

Yijk = µ + αiDFCi + βjBreedj + λijDFC×Breedij + δiDFC2i + γk + εijk               [1] [SOURCE: Page_79]

where Yijk are the GARUNS model fitting residuals of BW, MY, milk fat (MCF), milk protein [SOURCE: Page_79]

(MCP), milk lactose (MCL), and DMI, µ is the overall intercept across animals, αi, βj, and λij, and δi [SOURCE: Page_79]

are regression coefficients of Y on the corresponding predictors of day from calving (DFC), breed, [SOURCE: Page_79]

interaction between DFC and breed, and squared DFC, respectively, γk is the random effect of kth [SOURCE: Page_79]

animal, εijk is the residual error which was assumed to be normally distributed with mean of 0 [SOURCE: Page_79]

and variance of σ2e, i is the number of days from calving, j is the number of breeds, and k is [SOURCE: Page_79]

number of animals. Given the high numbers of observations in these regressions, and therefore, [SOURCE: Page_79]

the high test power (Lin et al., 2013) at the level of the regression coefficients, significant effects of [SOURCE: Page_79]

66 [SOURCE: Page_79]

Chapter 4 – Individual variability [SOURCE: Page_80]

breed were evaluated between models with and without breed included. Thus, F-values were [SOURCE: Page_80]

computed as [(RSS$_2$-RSS$_1$)/(p^1-p^2)]/[RSS$_1$/(n-p^1)] to compare model [1] and the general model [SOURCE: Page_80]

having the following form: [SOURCE: Page_80]

Yijk = µ + αiDFCi + δiDFC2i + γk + εijk                              [2] [SOURCE: Page_80]

The differences between model [2] and model [1] were the removal of Breed and Breed×DFC [SOURCE: Page_80]

interaction effects. RSS$_1$ is residual sum of square of model [1], RSS$_2$ is residual sum of square of [SOURCE: Page_80]

model [2], p^1 = 7 is the number of parameters in model [1], p^2 = 3 is the number of parameters in [SOURCE: Page_80]

model [2], n is the number of animals (n = 81 or 65 or 27 for first or second or third parity, [SOURCE: Page_80]

respectively). Consequently, (p^1-p^2) was the numerator degree of freedom and (n-p^1) was the [SOURCE: Page_80]

denominator degree of freedom. F-values were computed individually for MY, BW, DMI, MCF, [SOURCE: Page_80]

MCP, and MCL. Subsequently, F-values were compared to critical F-values from the F-table [SOURCE: Page_80]

using corresponding nominator and denominator degree of freedom, and a significance level of [SOURCE: Page_80]

- Key_Entities: Agriculture, Irrigation

### 0.05. If the two models are not significantly different, it can be concluded that data for three

breeds can be fitted using simple models with general intercepts and slopes (i.e. model [2]). In [SOURCE: Page_80]

other words, any deviations between breeds must be small and the observed significant [SOURCE: Page_80]

differences could simply be the consequence of having a large number of observations. In order [SOURCE: Page_80]

to reduce the correlations between intercept and slope, data of DFC was centred on its mean. [SOURCE: Page_80]

The correlations between genetic scaling parameters and the similarities between breeds [SOURCE: Page_80]

were explored using principal components (PC) analysis. Mixed effect models, and principal [SOURCE: Page_80]

components analysis were performed using nlme and FactoMineR packages of the statistical [SOURCE: Page_80]

software R version 2.5.2 (R Development Core Team, 2014). [SOURCE: Page_80]

Results [SOURCE: Page_80]

General quality of model fitting [SOURCE: Page_80]

An example of model simulations of BW, MY, and milk composition plotted with observed [SOURCE: Page_80]

values for the first two parities is presented in Figure 4.2. Table 4.1 and Table 4.2 present the [SOURCE: Page_80]

means of observed values for BW, MY, DMI, MCF, MCP, and MCL together with RPE values. [SOURCE: Page_80]

The results are shown for three periods within parity and breed (week 1-15, week 16-35, and [SOURCE: Page_80]

week 36-45). There were very few observations after 45 weeks of lactation, thus they were [SOURCE: Page_80]

excluded from the calculations of RMSE and RPE. In general, the accuracy of predictions for BW, [SOURCE: Page_80]

MY, and DMI improved slightly in parity two and three compared to parity one, which was not [SOURCE: Page_80]

the case for milk composition. The accuracies for predicting performance of Danish Red, Danish [SOURCE: Page_80]

67 [SOURCE: Page_80]

Chapter 4 – Individual variability [SOURCE: Page_81]

Holstein, and Jersey were similar. GARUNS accurately predicted individual BW, MCF, MCP, and [SOURCE: Page_81]

MCL over three parities, which was demonstrated by the low values of relative prediction error [SOURCE: Page_81]

(RPE < 13%). MY and DMI were slightly over-predicted in early- and mid-lactation (RPE < 20%), [SOURCE: Page_81]

and under-predicted in the end of lactation (RPE > 20%). The late lactation period, however, [SOURCE: Page_81]

contributed on average only 7% to the total lactation yield of MY and 13% to the total DMI. We [SOURCE: Page_81]

also explored the change of prediction accuracy within parity by examining the relationship [SOURCE: Page_81]

between daily values of RMSE across all animals versus day in milk. It turns out that the [SOURCE: Page_81]

prediction accuracy tended to be lower during first four weeks and last 8 weeks. The relative [SOURCE: Page_81]

magnitudes of deviations were, moreover, smaller for BW, MCF, MCP, and MCL compared to [SOURCE: Page_81]

MY and DMI. [SOURCE: Page_81]

Figure 4.2. Model simulations (solid line) of body weight (BW, kg), milk yield (MY, kg/d), milk fat [SOURCE: Page_81]

concentration (MCF, kg/kg), milk protein concentration (MCP, kg/kg), and milk lactose concentration [SOURCE: Page_81]

(MCL, kg/kg) in first two lactations of a selected cow plotted with actual observations (symbol). For [SOURCE: Page_81]

clarity of presentation, MY is shown as MYx^15. [SOURCE: Page_81]

Chapter 4 – Individual variability [SOURCE: Page_84]

Evaluation of residuals for time-patterns [SOURCE: Page_84]

Plots of GARUNS fitting residuals (Observed – Predicted) relative to DFC are shown in [SOURCE: Page_84]

Figure 4.3. The aim of the regression analysis was to evaluate if the GARUNS assumption still [SOURCE: Page_84]

holds for different breeds. Thus, it was hypothesized that the slopes of regression lines between [SOURCE: Page_84]

individual residual versus day from calving should not be significantly different from zero. [SOURCE: Page_84]

Given the data were centred the significances of the intercepts are no longer important as they are [SOURCE: Page_84]

not primary indicators of a systematic time-trend of residuals. The results of multiple regression [SOURCE: Page_84]

analysis of residuals of fitting for BW, MY, DMI, and milk composition are shown in the [SOURCE: Page_84]

Appendix (Table A$_1$ and Table A$_2$). The significant linear and curvilinear (DFC and DFC$_2$) effects [SOURCE: Page_84]

indicate a systematic difference between the shape assumed by GARUNS and that of current [SOURCE: Page_84]

data. The curvilinear effect can be seen from Figure 4.2 or the values of RPE in the last stage of [SOURCE: Page_84]

lactation cycle (Table 4.1 and Table 4.2). There were linear slope differences between breeds [SOURCE: Page_84]

which could suggest that the key assumption of GARUNS model does not hold for different [SOURCE: Page_84]

breeds. We, however, suspected these significant differences might simply be the consequence of [SOURCE: Page_84]

having a large number of observations in the test (e.g. N = 20136 for MY). Indeed, the comparison [SOURCE: Page_84]

between the full model with inclusion of breed effect (model [1]) and the simple model without [SOURCE: Page_84]

inclusion of breed effect (model [2]) showed no significant improvement in the capability of [SOURCE: Page_84]

model fit by including breed. The F-values for fitting residuals of MY, BW, DMI, MCF, MCP, and [SOURCE: Page_84]

MCL in parity one varied from 0.10 to 1.07, which are considerably smaller than the value of F- [SOURCE: Page_84]

critical of 2.53 at the level of significance of 0.05. Additionally, the residual standard deviations of [SOURCE: Page_84]

model [1] and model [2] were very similar, i.e. MY (2.44 vs 2.50), BW (17.22 vs 17.28), DMI (3.10 [SOURCE: Page_84]

vs 3.11), MCF (0.045 vs 0.046), MCP (0.002 vs 0.002), and MCL (0.001 vs 0.001). These results [SOURCE: Page_84]

generally imply that there was evidence of shape differences but the differences were very small [SOURCE: Page_84]

and not important in the context of using the teleonomic model (GARUNS) for simulation [SOURCE: Page_84]

studies. [SOURCE: Page_84]
