Chapter 4 – Individual variability [SOURCE: Page_87]

Relationship between model parameters [SOURCE: Page_87]

The summary statistics for the genetic scaling parameters are shown in Table 4.3. There were [SOURCE: Page_87]

large variations in genetic parameters modulating body weight of the animals, i.e. W M, b^0 and νx, [SOURCE: Page_87]

which represented 23, 25 and 39% of their means, respectively. The relative variations of [SOURCE: Page_87]

parameters modulating milk yield and milk composition were less than 20%. There were no [SOURCE: Page_87]

significant differences between three breeds in the means of b^0 and νL (P > 0.05, statistics not [SOURCE: Page_87]

reported). The mean values of the remaining genetic parameters (WM, νx, νy, νF, and νp) were [SOURCE: Page_87]

similar between Danish Red and Danish Holstein (P > 0.05), but significantly different from those [SOURCE: Page_87]

of Jersey. [SOURCE: Page_87]

Figure 4.4. PCA loadings for principal component 1 and principal component 2 by variable (a), and [SOURCE: Page_87]

scores for individual cows (b). In plot b, symbols are breeds: () Danish Red, () Danish Holstein and [SOURCE: Page_87]

() Jersey. The variables included are genetic parameters for mature weight (W M), b^0 = rate of [SOURCE: Page_87]

regaining body reserves per day, νX = labile body mass mobilization index, νy = peak milk yield [SOURCE: Page_87]

potential, νF = milk fat secretion, νP = milk protein secretion, and νL = milk lactose secretion. [SOURCE: Page_87]

The correlations between genetic parameters were initially explored within each breed (data [SOURCE: Page_87]

not shown). Generally, there were few significant correlations except for the followings: W M was [SOURCE: Page_87]

negatively correlated with νy (r ≈ -0.50); νy was negatively correlated with νF, νp, and νL (r = -0.20 [SOURCE: Page_87]

to -0.61); positive correlations were observed between νF and νp (r = 0.53 to 0.83), and between νp [SOURCE: Page_87]

and νL (r = 0.35 to 0.57). The relationships between genetic scaling parameters were further [SOURCE: Page_87]

explored with PC analysis, and are shown in Figure 4.4 and Figure 4.5. The first two principal [SOURCE: Page_87]

components accounted for 56.1% of the total variation, whereas the inclusion of third and fourth [SOURCE: Page_87]

74 [SOURCE: Page_87]

Chapter 4 – Individual variability [SOURCE: Page_88]

principal components increased variation accounted up to 87.1%. The variables that are close [SOURCE: Page_88]

together are strongly correlated (e.g. νP and νF). [SOURCE: Page_88]

Figure 4.5. PCA loadings for principal component 3 and principal component 4 by variable (a), and [SOURCE: Page_88]

scores for individual cows (b). In plot b, symbols are breeds: () Danish Red, () Danish Holstein and [SOURCE: Page_88]

() Jersey. The variables included are genetic parameters for mature weight (W M), b^0 = rate of [SOURCE: Page_88]

regaining body reserves per day, νX = labile body mass mobilization index, νy = peak milk yield [SOURCE: Page_88]

potential, νF = milk fat secretion, νP = milk protein secretion, and νL = milk lactose secretion. [SOURCE: Page_88]

The loadings coefficients for each variable and each principal component are presented in [SOURCE: Page_88]

Table 4.4. Loadings coefficients (w) describe the weight or the position of variables in each [SOURCE: Page_88]

principal component. PC#1 distinguishes dairy cows with a high body weight (w = -0.74) and low [SOURCE: Page_88]

concentrations of milk protein and milk fat from those with a low body weight and high [SOURCE: Page_88]

concentrations of milk protein (w = 0.94) and milk fat (w = 0.92). This is mainly identifying the [SOURCE: Page_88]

difference between Jersey cows and the other breeds (Danish Holstein and Danish Red; Figure [SOURCE: Page_88]

4.4a). PC#2, independently from PC#1, indicates that high values of body weight (w = 0.56) and [SOURCE: Page_88]

milk lactose concentration (w = 0.57) were associated with a low value of milk yield (w = -0.86). [SOURCE: Page_88]

Danish Holstein and Danish Red show some separation on PC#2 (Figure 4.4b). PC#3 separates [SOURCE: Page_88]

animals with high ability of storage (w = 0.86) from animals with high ability of mobilization of [SOURCE: Page_88]

body reserves (w = -0.63). PC#3 and PC#4 probably indicate differences within breed. It is [SOURCE: Page_88]

difficult to distinguish between breeds from Figure 4.5b but it instead discriminates cows of high [SOURCE: Page_88]

capability of mobilization from cows of high capability of storage. This may be identifying cows [SOURCE: Page_88]

that have been selected for different purposes, i.e. milk production or milk-meat. [SOURCE: Page_88]

Chapter 4 – Individual variability [SOURCE: Page_90]

Discussion [SOURCE: Page_90]

The GARUNS model was generally able to fit individual curves of body weight, and milk [SOURCE: Page_90]

fat, milk protein, and milk lactose concentrations with a high degree of accuracy. Daily milk yield [SOURCE: Page_90]

and dry matter intake of individual cows were satisfactorily predicted in early- and mid-lactation [SOURCE: Page_90]

but significant under-predictions were found in late-lactation. Breeds and parities did not [SOURCE: Page_90]

influence the prediction accuracy. Further, the inclusion of a breed effect did not significantly [SOURCE: Page_90]

improve the regression models to describe time trends in the residuals of GARUNS fitting [SOURCE: Page_90]

(compared to the general regression models without inclusion of breed effect). Thus, the [SOURCE: Page_90]

GARUNS model can be used to simulate performance of individual cows using the assumption [SOURCE: Page_90]

that the shape of relative priorities is general, and the individual variability can be simply [SOURCE: Page_90]

captured using genetic scaling parameters. The general under-prediction of MY and DMI in late [SOURCE: Page_90]

lactation would be explained by the difference in feeding system between the current data (i.e. [SOURCE: Page_90]

Nielsen et al., 2003) and the one that Martin and Sauvant (2010) used for calibration (i.e. Schutz et [SOURCE: Page_90]

al., 1990). Cows in the study of Nielsen et al. (2003) were fed with a constant energy density diet [SOURCE: Page_90]

throughout lactation to allow cows to express their genetic potentials, whereas in Schutz et al. [SOURCE: Page_90]

(1990) cows were fed with a step-down reduction of energy density diet from early to late [SOURCE: Page_90]

lactation. This type of step feeding system is the most common one in current dairy practice [SOURCE: Page_90]

(Erasmus et al., 2013; Stewart et al., 2014). The reduced dietary energy in late lactation probably [SOURCE: Page_90]

results in a moderate lack of energy to allow cows to express their genetic potentials. Since the [SOURCE: Page_90]

maintenance has a higher priority than other needs (Jokela and Mutikainen, 1995; Heino and [SOURCE: Page_90]

Kaitala, 1999), and that in late lactation body reserves are recovered with high priority to prepare [SOURCE: Page_90]

for next reproductive cycle, a shortage of energy would result in an impaired milk yield [SOURCE: Page_90]

(Friggens, 2003). The high accuracy of predicting BW supports this argument. Given that [SOURCE: Page_90]

GARUNS is a systemic model with separate parameters controlling the dynamic of partitioning [SOURCE: Page_90]

priorities and the scaling of these according to different genetic potentials it has the flexibility to [SOURCE: Page_90]

be easily recalibrated to reflect the late lactation differences highlighted with the present dataset. [SOURCE: Page_90]

These results confirm the acceptability of using this teleonomic model for simulating lifetime [SOURCE: Page_90]

performance of individual cows. They also suggest the dataset used is a good one for estimating [SOURCE: Page_90]

genetic scaling parameters representing genetic potentials that are unaffected by any correlations [SOURCE: Page_90]

between lactation stage and environment. [SOURCE: Page_90]

It has commonly been recognized that animals in the same population differ in their ability [SOURCE: Page_90]

to perform, which comes as a result of variation in the nature of the animals or in the [SOURCE: Page_90]

77 [SOURCE: Page_90]

Chapter 4 – Individual variability [SOURCE: Page_91]

environment (Bryant et al., 2005). The data used in this study was obtained from a long-term [SOURCE: Page_91]

genetic evaluation experiment where three breeds of cow selected for different purposes were [SOURCE: Page_91]

kept in the same environment; thus making it appropriate for exploring the genetic variability [SOURCE: Page_91]

between individuals. In this context, given the GARUNS model fits well the observed data it can [SOURCE: Page_91]

be used to separate genetic variability in performance between individual cows from [SOURCE: Page_91]

environmental noise. Moreover, it can, by individually calibrating the model, derive genetic [SOURCE: Page_91]

scaling parameters representing genetic potentials of each cow. Characterizing individual [SOURCE: Page_91]

variation in this way is valuable as it is a prerequisite for simulation studies. In the context where [SOURCE: Page_91]

(nutritional) environment is constraint, energy intake might be insufficient for animals to attain [SOURCE: Page_91]

their genetic potentials thus some functions might will be given higher priority than others the [SOURCE: Page_91]

rules for these adjustments are given in the companion publication of this model (Martin and [SOURCE: Page_91]

Sauvant, 2010b). [SOURCE: Page_91]

To set up realistic population simulations it is important to acquire knowledge of variation [SOURCE: Page_91]

and possible correlations between the model parameters (Emmans and Fisher, 1986). The [SOURCE: Page_91]

summary statistics of genetic scaling parameters (Table 4.3) show no differences between Danish [SOURCE: Page_91]

Red and Danish Holstein in the means of each parameter. Conversely, the means of genetic [SOURCE: Page_91]

scaling parameters of Jersey were, with the exceptions of b^0 and νL, significantly different from [SOURCE: Page_91]

that of the other two breeds. There was a significantly smaller variation in ν L than in any of the [SOURCE: Page_91]

other parameters implying a low potential for genetic change in milk lactose concentration. This [SOURCE: Page_91]

result is consistent with that of other studies (Welper and Freeman, 1992; Bedö et al., 1995). [SOURCE: Page_91]

In the GARUNS model, with the exception of WM, each genetic scaling parameter modulates [SOURCE: Page_91]

a particular trait independently. Thus, the correlations between these parameters would largely [SOURCE: Page_91]

be induced by the biological relationships between the traits and not by the model structure as [SOURCE: Page_91]

such. Not unexpectedly, some of the genetic scaling parameters were correlated (Figure 4.4 and [SOURCE: Page_91]

Figure 4.5). The results of the principal component analysis also show that the model genetic [SOURCE: Page_91]

scaling parameters were affected by genotypes. The first two components accounted for up to [SOURCE: Page_91]

56.1% of the total variation of the data and mainly distinguish the genetic differences between [SOURCE: Page_91]

breeds (Figure 4.4). Generally, Danish Red and Danish Holstein breeds were represented by high [SOURCE: Page_91]

body weight and high milk production but low milk fat and milk protein concentrations. Jersey [SOURCE: Page_91]

cows were characterized by a small weight and high milk fat and milk protein concentrations. [SOURCE: Page_91]

These results were consistent with the summary statistics on the means of genetic scaling [SOURCE: Page_91]

parameters between the three breeds reported in Table 4.3. Further PCs brought additional [SOURCE: Page_91]

78 [SOURCE: Page_91]

Chapter 4 – Individual variability [SOURCE: Page_92]

information on genetic variation within breeds. Within breed, there were negative correlations [SOURCE: Page_92]

between the genetic scaling parameters for MY and milk composition (Figure 4.5). These results [SOURCE: Page_92]

agree with other studies that reported genetic correlations ranging from -0.12 to -0.34 for MY [SOURCE: Page_92]

versus MCF, -0.18 to -0.36 for MY versus MCP, and -0.08 for MY versus MCL (Welper and [SOURCE: Page_92]

Freeman, 1992; Pantelic´ et al., 2008; Vallas et al., 2010). The strongly positive correlation between [SOURCE: Page_92]

MCF and MCP agrees with the literature ranging from 0.51 to 0.56 (de Jager and Kennedy, 1987; [SOURCE: Page_92]

Welper and Freeman, 1992). The relationship between MCL and MCF and MCP varied from 0.16 [SOURCE: Page_92]

to 0.53, whereas this has been reported between 0.11 and 0.29 (Welper and Freeman, 1992; [SOURCE: Page_92]

Huhtanen, 2006). However, it should be noted that the relationships between the model [SOURCE: Page_92]

parameters and correlations between traits reported from the literature might not be the same [SOURCE: Page_92]

because they are not the direct measures of these traits but rather are representatives of those [SOURCE: Page_92]

traits. The negative correlation between WM and νy (r ≈ -0.50) does not agree with literature [SOURCE: Page_92]

correlation between BW and MY. Genetic correlations between BW and MY are often weak or [SOURCE: Page_92]

even negative (r = -0.01 to 0.025), indicating that selection for milk yield negligibly affects BW of [SOURCE: Page_92]

an animal (Morris, 1976; Brotherstone, 1994; Mason et al., 2009). However, Berry et al. (2003) [SOURCE: Page_92]

reported that when BW was adjusted for body condition score the correlation became positive [SOURCE: Page_92]

and ranged from 0.15 to 0.39. The negative correlation found in this study can be explained by the [SOURCE: Page_92]

fact that WM was incorporated in the equation of milk production simulation aiming to scale MY [SOURCE: Page_92]

to mature body size, i.e. genetic size-scaling rule (Taylor, 2010). Interestingly, the genetic scaling [SOURCE: Page_92]

parameters controlling body reserve *mobilization (νX) and accretion (b^0)] were not significantly [SOURCE: Page_92]

correlated within breed. PC#3 differentiated cows of high ability to mobilize body reserves from [SOURCE: Page_92]

those of high ability to storage (Figure 4.5) which might imply differences between cows in the [SOURCE: Page_92]

temporal pattern of nutrient partitioning that underpins lactation. [SOURCE: Page_92]

Another aspect of differences in the temporal pattern was found with respect to parity. [SOURCE: Page_92]

Rather than assuming a fixed ratio between parities in the genetic scaling parameter for milk [SOURCE: Page_92]

production, we allowed differences between animals in the ratio. As can be seen in Figure 4.6, the [SOURCE: Page_92]

priority for milk production in parity 2 tended to be impaired if it had been highly prioritized in [SOURCE: Page_92]

the previous parity. However, this needs to be confirmed by longer multi-lactation study. [SOURCE: Page_92]

Conclusions [SOURCE: Page_92]

The results of this study show the value of a dynamic model of lifetime performance as a tool [SOURCE: Page_92]

to derive estimates of genetic potentials of milk yield, milk composition, body reserve usage, and [SOURCE: Page_92]

growth for different genotypes of cow. The model was found, across a range of dairy breeds, to [SOURCE: Page_92]

79 [SOURCE: Page_92]

Chapter 4 – Individual variability [SOURCE: Page_93]

be sufficiently precise to be suitable for use in simulation studies of long-term efficiency. The [SOURCE: Page_93]

correlations between model genetic parameters agreed with the related literature values, and also [SOURCE: Page_93]

quantified some key components of variation in lactation performance. Thus, this model is a [SOURCE: Page_93]

suitable basis for simulating the effects of genetic selection strategy on lifetime efficiency of [SOURCE: Page_93]

individual cows, and thus address key questions about the impact of future selection (Friggens et [SOURCE: Page_93]

al., 2010; Dillon et al., 2006; Berry and Crowley, 2013). [SOURCE: Page_93]

Chapter 5 – Lifetime efficiency [SOURCE: Page_99]

Chapter 5 [SOURCE: Page_99]

Extending a lifetime nutrient partitioning model to [SOURCE: Page_99]

include performance consequences on probability of reproducing [SOURCE: Page_99]

and thereby allow prediction of lifetime efficiency of dairy cows [SOURCE: Page_99]

H. N. Phuong,†* P. Blavy†*, O. Martin†*, Ph. Schmidely†*, and N.C. Friggens,†* [SOURCE: Page_99]

† INRA, UMR791 Modélisation Systémique Appliquée aux Ruminants, F-75005 Paris, France [SOURCE: Page_99]

* AgroParisTech, UMR791 Modélisation Systémique Appliquée aux Ruminants, F-75005 Paris, [SOURCE: Page_99]

France [SOURCE: Page_99]

Animal (In preparation) [SOURCE: Page_99]

86 [SOURCE: Page_99]

Chapter 5 – Lifetime efficiency [SOURCE: Page_100]

Abstract [SOURCE: Page_100]

Reproductive success is a key component of lifetime efficiency, i.e. ratio of energy in milk [SOURCE: Page_100]

(MJ) to energy intake (MJ) over the lifespan, of cows. At the animal level breeding and feeding [SOURCE: Page_100]

managements might substantially impact on milk yield, body condition, and energy balance of [SOURCE: Page_100]

cows which are known as major contributors to reproductive failure in dairy cattle. This study [SOURCE: Page_100]

extended an existing lifetime performance model to incorporate the performance consequences as [SOURCE: Page_100]

a result of changing breeding and feeding strategies on probability of reproducing and thereby [SOURCE: Page_100]

allow prediction of cow lifetime efficiency. The model is dynamic and stochastic with an [SOURCE: Page_100]

individual cow being the unit of modelling and one day being the unit of time. To evaluate the [SOURCE: Page_100]

model, a French data including Holstein and Normande cows fed with high concentrate diet and [SOURCE: Page_100]

a Scottish data including Holstein cows selected for high (selected line) and average (control line) [SOURCE: Page_100]

genetic merit for fat plus protein, fed with high versus low concentrate diets were used. [SOURCE: Page_100]

Generally, the model consistently simulated productive and reproductive performance, lifespan, [SOURCE: Page_100]

and lifetime efficiency of various genotypes of cow across feeding systems. On the French data, [SOURCE: Page_100]

the model adequately simulated reproductive performance of Holstein cows but slight under- [SOURCE: Page_100]

predictions were found for Normande cows. On the Scottish data, concenption to first service [SOURCE: Page_100]

was comparably simulated while interval traits were under predicted which is mainly due to the [SOURCE: Page_100]

discrepancy in management issue. Genetic selection for greater milk production impaired [SOURCE: Page_100]

reproductive performance but not lifetime efficiency. Effect of high versus low concentrate [SOURCE: Page_100]

feedings on reproductive performance was well accommodated. Further works should include [SOURCE: Page_100]

economic indicators to allow more accurately simulate lifetime profitability in different scenarios. [SOURCE: Page_100]

Keywords: dairy cow, lifetime efficiency, reproduction, nutrient partitioning [SOURCE: Page_100]

87 [SOURCE: Page_100]

Chapter 5 – Lifetime efficiency [SOURCE: Page_101]

Introduction [SOURCE: Page_101]

It is now widely accepted that the single-trait selection for greater milk production in dairy [SOURCE: Page_101]

cattle has been associated with significant declines in animal health (Berry et al.., 2011) and [SOURCE: Page_101]

reproductive performance (Albarrán-Portillo and Pollott, 2013). These problems not only impair [SOURCE: Page_101]

lifetime production efficiency of dairy cows via reduced longevity by increasing involuntary [SOURCE: Page_101]

culling (Seegers et al.., 1998), they also increase veterinary and reproductive costs (Hogeveen et [SOURCE: Page_101]

al.., 2011), environmental losses (Garnsworthy, 2004), and affect the animal welfare (Oltenacu and [SOURCE: Page_101]

Algers, 2005). An increased milk yield, a negative energy balance, and a low body condition have [SOURCE: Page_101]

commonly been considered as the major biological contributors to the loss in longevity of dairy [SOURCE: Page_101]

cows [see reviews of Lucy (2001) and Friggens et al.. (2010)]. However, the individual effects of [SOURCE: Page_101]

milk yield, energy balance, and body condition on reproduction have been difficult to quantify [SOURCE: Page_101]

because they are often correlated. Experiments specifically designed to break these correlations [SOURCE: Page_101]

are unfortunately rare (Wright et al.., 1992), and a modeling approach to integrate all available [SOURCE: Page_101]

data seems necessary (Blanc et al.., 2001). Management strategies such as breeding (e.g. selecting [SOURCE: Page_101]

for high versus low genetic merit cows) and feeding (e.g. high versus low energetic diets) [SOURCE: Page_101]

programs can alter milk yield and body reserves, and consequently reproductive performance [SOURCE: Page_101]

(Cutullic et al.., 2009, 2011). In this context, if we wish to predict lifetime efficiency there is a need [SOURCE: Page_101]

of being able to predict responses of dairy cows in terms of both productive and reproductive [SOURCE: Page_101]

performance under different management strategies. Such a predictive capability is especially [SOURCE: Page_101]

important in the case of dairy production environments where a wide variation might be present [SOURCE: Page_101]

between genotypes of the animal and between the environments in which they are managed. [SOURCE: Page_101]

Individual-based herd models that describe each single animal can be used to simulate [SOURCE: Page_101]

animal responses to management practices (Brun-Lafleur et al.., 2013). Unfortunately, in most of [SOURCE: Page_101]

these models, the reproductive process is represented by assuming a fixed efficiency such as [SOURCE: Page_101]

fixed calving to ovulation or fixed parturition to conception intervals (Allore et al.., 1998; Blanc [SOURCE: Page_101]

and Agabriel, 2008; Martin and Sauvant, 2010). Though recent works of Inchaisri et al.. (2010) and [SOURCE: Page_101]

Brun-Lafleur et al.. (2013) have recognized the possible influences of milk yield and body [SOURCE: Page_101]

reserves on reproductive performance, the simulations of these traits are only based on genetic [SOURCE: Page_101]

potential and physiological stages, without a possibility of including an interaction with feeding [SOURCE: Page_101]

regimes. Additionally, these models are only developed for a single lactation and thus cannot be [SOURCE: Page_101]

used to predict the lifetime performance and lifetime efficiency of the animals. [SOURCE: Page_101]

88 [SOURCE: Page_101]

Chapter 5 – Lifetime efficiency [SOURCE: Page_102]

The objective of our study is therefore to extend a previously published lifetime nutrient [SOURCE: Page_102]

partitioning model of Martin and Sauvant (2010) for predicting lifetime efficiency of individual [SOURCE: Page_102]

dairy cows under various selected management strategies by incorporating the possible [SOURCE: Page_102]

influences of animal performance on reproductive performance. We hypothesize that a high milk [SOURCE: Page_102]

yield, a low energy balance, and a low body condition negatively affect reproductive [SOURCE: Page_102]

performance, and thereby productive lifespan which is expected to have consequences on [SOURCE: Page_102]

lifetime efficiency of dairy cows. [SOURCE: Page_102]

Figure 5.1. Schematic presentation of the model for predicting lifetime efficiency of individual cows. [SOURCE: Page_102]

Materials and Methods [SOURCE: Page_102]

The model used in this study is dynamic and stochastic with the individual cow being the [SOURCE: Page_102]

unit of modelling and one day being the unit of time. The lifetime of a cow is considered from [SOURCE: Page_102]

birth till culling. The schematic presentation of this model is presented in Figure 5.1. The core of [SOURCE: Page_102]

this extended model relies on the lifetime nutrient partitioning model of Martin and Sauvant [SOURCE: Page_102]

(2010), referred to here as GARUNS, which simulates lifetime performance of individual cows. [SOURCE: Page_102]

The inputs of GARUNS include genetic scaling parameters (to be specified in next section), [SOURCE: Page_102]

feeding information (i.e. dietary energy density), reproductive (i.e. conception and parturition), [SOURCE: Page_102]

and management (i.e. culling) events. The outputs of GARUNS are daily feed intake, milk yield [SOURCE: Page_102]

and composition, fetal growth, calf birth weight, body weight and composition, body condition [SOURCE: Page_102]

score, and energy balance throughout the lifespan of a cow. The reproduction sub-model [SOURCE: Page_102]

developed in the present study uses information of milk yield, body condition score, and energy [SOURCE: Page_102]

balance from GARUNS as its inputs to simulate reproductive responses of conception and [SOURCE: Page_102]

parturition, and culling decision which are in turn transferred to GARUNS. In the present study, [SOURCE: Page_102]

the lifespan of individual cows is mainly affected by reproductive performance and culling [SOURCE: Page_102]

89 [SOURCE: Page_102]

Chapter 5 – Lifetime efficiency [SOURCE: Page_103]

policy. The effect of diseases, for example, mastitis, ketosis or fatty liver on survivability of cows [SOURCE: Page_103]

is not currently incorporated. [SOURCE: Page_103]

Description of lifetime nutrient partitioning model (GARUNS) [SOURCE: Page_103]

The GARUNS model consists of a regulating sub-model providing a driving force to control [SOURCE: Page_103]

the function of an operating sub-model. An adapted scheme of GARUNS is presented in Figure [SOURCE: Page_103]


---
[CHUNK_METADATA]
- Parent_Topic: 5130044
- Summary: Technical section focusing on 5.2. the regulating sub-model describes the dynamic partitioning of a female mammal’s priority
- Key_Entities: Agriculture, Irrigation
- Confidence_Score: High
---
### 5.2. The regulating sub-model describes the dynamic partitioning of a female mammal’s priority

between life functions: growth (G), ageing (A), regaining of body reserves (R), and energy supply [SOURCE: Page_103]

of the unborn (U), newborn (N), and suckling (S) calf over a lifespan. This dynamic pattern of [SOURCE: Page_103]

priorities is assumed to be general for all individual cows. The sum of these relative priorities is [SOURCE: Page_103]

always equal to one. The operating sub-model uses the relative priorities to partition energy [SOURCE: Page_103]

intake between foetal growth, body weight and body composition, milk yield, and milk [SOURCE: Page_103]

composition over the lifespan and during repeated reproductive cycles. Genetic scaling [SOURCE: Page_103]

parameters are incorporated in the operating sub-model to scale individual performance [SOURCE: Page_103]

potentials of, for example, mature body size or milk production. It should be noted that these [SOURCE: Page_103]

genetic scaling parameters are not simply related to the widely reported breeding values [SOURCE: Page_103]

commonly used in the animal genetics literature. They act as multipliers on the different dynamic [SOURCE: Page_103]

priorities and thus provide the means to create different levels of genetic potential performance [SOURCE: Page_103]

for different cows. [SOURCE: Page_103]

In this context, the variation in genetic scaling parameters describes the variation in genetic [SOURCE: Page_103]

potential between animals. In the model, an animal is described in terms of its genotype for [SOURCE: Page_103]

growth, capacity to store and mobilize reserves, and milk production using the following genetic [SOURCE: Page_103]

scaling parameters: non-labile body mass at maturity (WM), rate of regaining body reserves per [SOURCE: Page_103]

day (b^0), labile body mass mobilization index (νX), peak milk yield potential (νy), milk fat [SOURCE: Page_103]

secretion (νF), milk protein secretion (νP), and milk lactose secretion (νL). The unit of WM is [SOURCE: Page_103]

kilogram, whereas the remaining parameters are fractions, and therefore, have no unit. Thus, to [SOURCE: Page_103]

describe each genotype a specific set of these genetic scaling parameters is needed. External [SOURCE: Page_103]

triggers of successful insemination (i.e. conception) drive the changes between non-reproductive [SOURCE: Page_103]

and reproductive states of the animal cueing in the dynamic priority trajectories described in the [SOURCE: Page_103]

regulating sub-model. [SOURCE: Page_103]

As shown in Figure 5.2, the combination between dynamic priority and genetic scaling [SOURCE: Page_103]

parameters makes it possible to quantify the total amount of energy required to fulfill all [SOURCE: Page_103]

functions (ΣE). The resulting dry matter intake (DMI) is quantified by multiplying ΣE with the [SOURCE: Page_103]

90 [SOURCE: Page_103]

Chapter 5 – Lifetime efficiency [SOURCE: Page_104]

predetermined dietary energy density (eD). The coefficients of energy partition to different life [SOURCE: Page_104]

functions (EG/ΣE, ER/ΣE, EMY/ΣE, where EG, ER, EMY are energy required for growth, reserves [SOURCE: Page_104]

storage, and milk production, respectively) are the result of the interactions between dynamic [SOURCE: Page_104]

partitioning priorities and genetic scaling parameters. [SOURCE: Page_104]

Figure 5.2. Schematic diagram of the GARUNS model [adapted with permission of Martin and [SOURCE: Page_104]

Sauvant (2010)], showing how energy requirements for different life functions (growth, body reserves, [SOURCE: Page_104]

and milk) are derived from genetic scaling parameters combined with lifetime profiles of relative [SOURCE: Page_104]

priorities for the different life functions. The priorities for foetal growth, ageing (in regulating sub- [SOURCE: Page_104]

model), and energy needed for foetal growth (in operating sub-model) are not presented as they are [SOURCE: Page_104]

assumed to be common trajectories for every individual. Total energy needed is the sum of the [SOURCE: Page_104]

different requirements, from which DM intake can be calculated assuming an appropriate diet energy [SOURCE: Page_104]

density (eD). [SOURCE: Page_104]

91 [SOURCE: Page_104]

92 Figure 5.3. Representation of the reproduction sub-model. This is simply a visualization of the model and thus the time scale is not necessarily correct. [SOURCE: Page_105]

Chapter 5 – Lifetime efficiency [SOURCE: Page_106]

Description of the reproduction sub-model [SOURCE: Page_106]

Because the main interest of this study is on lifetime efficiency of individual cows, for which [SOURCE: Page_106]

a key component is the lifespan of the animals, the reproductive information needed is only that [SOURCE: Page_106]

which creates variation in the lifespan. This can simply be seen as a result of whether or not, and [SOURCE: Page_106]

when, the cow successfully gets pregnant, and calves. Instead of modelling all the individual [SOURCE: Page_106]

steps of the reproductive process such as uterine evolution, resumption of estrus cycle, estrus [SOURCE: Page_106]

expression, conception, and embryonic survival, we assume a likelihood of conception of a viable [SOURCE: Page_106]

calf (LCONC) as the final product of the reproductive process, which is the accumulative result of [SOURCE: Page_106]

other steps involved towards having a newborn calf. In accordance with literature LCONC is [SOURCE: Page_106]

assumed to be affected by milk yield, energy balance, and body condition of the cow (Lucy, 2001; [SOURCE: Page_106]

Friggens et al., 2010). The influences of parity (primiparous versus multiparous), estrus number, [SOURCE: Page_106]

and disease on LCONC are not explicitly incorporated. The key reproductive events in our [SOURCE: Page_106]

reproduction sub-model consist of conception and parturition. Figure 5.3 shows that after [SOURCE: Page_106]

parturition, cows are assumed to have a period of postpartum anestrus of around 21.8 ± 3 days [SOURCE: Page_106]

(Darwash et al., 1997). Thereafter the cow is assumed to have an estrus of 3 days duration which [SOURCE: Page_106]

takes place every 21.8 ± 3 days from the previous estrus period (Darwash et al., 1997). The [SOURCE: Page_106]

breeding period is commenced after a voluntary waiting period, here set to 50 days (Löf et al., [SOURCE: Page_106]

2012). Prior to the end of the voluntary waiting period and out of the 3-day estrus windows, [SOURCE: Page_106]

LCONC is zero. During the breeding period, artificial insemination is assumed to take place at [SOURCE: Page_106]

every estrus with a LCONC greater than a threshold value (0.4) to reflect the idea that weak [SOURCE: Page_106]

estruses will be poorly expressed and then not detected by the inseminators. Insemination is [SOURCE: Page_106]

simulated using a random draw from a uniform distribution, U (0, 1). A cow is assumed pregnant [SOURCE: Page_106]

if the insemination value is smaller than the LCONC value. LCONC is calculated using LCONC [SOURCE: Page_106]

components determined by milk yield, energy balance, and body condition of the cows, i.e. [SOURCE: Page_106]

LCONCMY, LCONCEB, and LCONCBCS, respectively (Equation 1). We assume a maximal value of [SOURCE: Page_106]

LCONC of 0.6 ± 0.06 which is supported by a reported variation between 57 – 63% of the [SOURCE: Page_106]

conception rate of Holstein virgin heifers (Kuhn et al., 2006). This assumption is made in light of [SOURCE: Page_106]

our initial hypothesis that fertility is mainly affected by a high milk production, a negative energy [SOURCE: Page_106]

balance, and a low body condition, which do not exist in virgin heifers. Consequently, LCONC is [SOURCE: Page_106]

computed as follow: [SOURCE: Page_106]

## LCONC = 0.60 × (LCONCMY × LCONCEB × LCONCBCS)                            [1]

93 [SOURCE: Page_106]

Chapter 5 – Lifetime efficiency [SOURCE: Page_107]

The gestation length is assumed to follow a normal distribution, N (280, 5) days, as reported [SOURCE: Page_107]

by Hansen et al. (2004). Factors that influence gestation length such as breed, age, or birth weight [SOURCE: Page_107]

of the calf were not taken into account. [SOURCE: Page_107]

Effect of milk production on likelihood of conception [SOURCE: Page_107]

The underlying mechanism for the negative effect of milk yield (MY) on fertility of dairy [SOURCE: Page_107]

cows was investigated by Wiltbank et al. (2006). As high milk yield is supported by high dry [SOURCE: Page_107]

matter intake and consequently high blood flow in the liver, it increases the speed of clearance of [SOURCE: Page_107]

both estradiol and progesterone. The low concentration of plasma estradiol reduces the [SOURCE: Page_107]

stimulation of the centers that control estrus behavior, and thus the behavioral expression of [SOURCE: Page_107]

estrus, whereas a reduced circulating concentration of progesterone is associated with a low [SOURCE: Page_107]

pregnancy rate (McNeill et al., 2006). In this study, it is assumed that as soon as the cows are [SOURCE: Page_107]

producing milk it has a negative effect on LCONC, which increases in a sigmoid manner with [SOURCE: Page_107]

increasing milk yield. The likelihood of conception resulted from the effect of milk yield [SOURCE: Page_107]

(LCONCMY) is quantified in equation 2. [SOURCE: Page_107]

LCONCMY = 2 × (1 / 1 + exp (RatMY × MY))                           [2] [SOURCE: Page_107]

where MY is daily milk yield (kg/d), and RatMY is a constant parameter implying the extent [SOURCE: Page_107]

of the effect of milk yield on the likelihood of conception. The value of Rat MY (0.012) was [SOURCE: Page_107]

estimated from the study of Cutullic et al. (2011) in which the experiment was designed to [SOURCE: Page_107]

separate the effect of MY on reproductive performance from the effects of energy balance and [SOURCE: Page_107]

body condition. [SOURCE: Page_107]

Effect of energy balance on likelihood of conception [SOURCE: Page_107]

The effect of negative energy balance (NEB) is commonly attributed to the suppression of the [SOURCE: Page_107]

pulsatility of luteinizing hormone (LH) and reduces the ovarian responsiveness to LH [SOURCE: Page_107]

stimulation (Butler, 2000). During the period of negative energy balance, plasma glucose, insulin [SOURCE: Page_107]

and insulin-like growth factor-I (IGF-I) are reduced. Insulin is known to stimulate bovine [SOURCE: Page_107]

follicular cell (Spicer et al., 1993) whereas IGF-I is critical to ovarian follicular development [SOURCE: Page_107]

(Butler et al., 2006). Consequently, NEB shifts the course of postpartum ovarian activity and [SOURCE: Page_107]

strongly influences the resumption of ovarian cycles (Senatore et al., 2010). Other authors have [SOURCE: Page_107]

also reported an inferior oocyte quality in negative energy balance cows (Snijders et al., 2000; [SOURCE: Page_107]

Walters et al., 2002; Leroy et al., 2008). The effect of NEB on the likelihood of conception [SOURCE: Page_107]

(LCONCEB) is formalized in equation 3. [SOURCE: Page_107]

LCONCEB = 2 × (1 / 1 + exp (-RatEB × EB))                        [3] [SOURCE: Page_107]

94 [SOURCE: Page_107]

Chapter 5 – Lifetime efficiency [SOURCE: Page_108]

where EB is daily energy balance (MJ/d) and RatEB is a constant parameter. The value of RatEB [SOURCE: Page_108]

(0.027) was derived from the study of Wright (1992). This experiment on beef cattle was designed [SOURCE: Page_108]

to break the correlation between body fatness and body mobilization by applying two feeding [SOURCE: Page_108]

levels during lactation to cows that had previously been made thin or fat. Thus, it is possible to [SOURCE: Page_108]

separate the effect of body condition and negative energy balance on reproductive performance, [SOURCE: Page_108]

if we assume that there was no effect of MY. Energy balance is computed as follow: [SOURCE: Page_108]

EB = EIntake – (EGrowth + EMaintenance + EMY + EPregnancy)                  [4] [SOURCE: Page_108]

where EB is energy balance (MJ/d), EIntake is energy intake (MJ/d), EGrowth, EMaintenance, EMY, and [SOURCE: Page_108]

EPregnancy are energy requirements for growth, maintenance, milk production, and pregnancy [SOURCE: Page_108]

(MJ/d). Energy is measured as metabolizable energy. [SOURCE: Page_108]

Effect of body condition on likelihood of conception [SOURCE: Page_108]

In literature, some authors have observed the depressed reproductive performance in thin [SOURCE: Page_108]

compared to fat cows (Wright et al., 1992; Pryce et al., 2001; Veerkamp et al., 2001). Low body [SOURCE: Page_108]

condition is related to low level of leptin which is also linked to reproductive function (Spicer, [SOURCE: Page_108]

2001). The effect of body condition on the likelihood of conception (LCONCBCS) is formalized in [SOURCE: Page_108]

equation 5. To measure body fatness of cows, we use body condition score (BCS) on scale of 0 to 5 [SOURCE: Page_108]

(0 = emaciated and 5 = extremely fat). It is assumed that a level of BCS ≥ 3 has no effect on [SOURCE: Page_108]

## LCONCBCS.

LCONCBCS = 2 × (1 / 1+exp (-RatBCS × (BCS - 3))                           [5] [SOURCE: Page_108]

where RatBCS is a constant parameter. The value RatBCS (0.425) was obtained from the study of [SOURCE: Page_108]

Wright and co-workers (1992). [SOURCE: Page_108]

Culling policy [SOURCE: Page_108]

The reproductive culling rules assumed for the present study are as follows. Cows are culled [SOURCE: Page_108]

if they fail to get pregnant after 4 inseminations and the level of milk on that day is < 25 kg/d. If [SOURCE: Page_108]

the insemination number is > 4 but cows are still producing ≥ 25 kg/d of milk, a fifth insemination [SOURCE: Page_108]

is allowed, if this does not result in pregnancy they are culled. To take into account the fact that [SOURCE: Page_108]

some cows might not express estrus for a long time, we include another culling rule in which [SOURCE: Page_108]

cows are culled anyway if they are not pregnant after 160 days postpartum. For simplicity, at this [SOURCE: Page_108]

stage, the effects of other management factors such as age, or population structure on culling [SOURCE: Page_108]

decision were not taken into account. [SOURCE: Page_108]

95 [SOURCE: Page_108]

Chapter 5 – Lifetime efficiency [SOURCE: Page_109]

Model implementation and evaluation [SOURCE: Page_109]

The model was implemented with C++ programing language on the RECORD platform [SOURCE: Page_109]

(Bergez et al., 2013). To evaluate the model, two data sets were selected. In the first data set, of [SOURCE: Page_109]

Cutullic et al. (2011) and Delaby (personal communication, 2014), two breeds of cow (Holstein [SOURCE: Page_109]

Friesian selected for milk yield and dual-purpose Normande cows) fed with high concentrate [SOURCE: Page_109]

were used to evaluate the effect of genetic selection on reproductive performance and lifetime [SOURCE: Page_109]

efficiency. The second data set comes from the long-term experiment carried out at the Langhill [SOURCE: Page_109]

Dairy Cattle Research Center (Pryce et al., 1999; Coffey et al., 2004). In the Langhill data, the high [SOURCE: Page_109]

and low feeding systems were applied to two lines of Holstein Friesian cows: control line and the [SOURCE: Page_109]

line that had been selected for kilograms of fat plus protein yield. This allows testing the effect of [SOURCE: Page_109]

genotype and feeding interaction. [SOURCE: Page_109]

The GARUNS model was first adjusted to simulate populations of cows that match their [SOURCE: Page_109]

performance in the selected data sets. This was done using the genetic scaling parameters that [SOURCE: Page_109]

represent milk production level (νy), mature size, (WM), rate of regaining body reserves per day [SOURCE: Page_109]

(b^0), and labile body mass mobilization index (νX). The higher the values of νy and WM, for [SOURCE: Page_109]

example, the higher the capability of producing milk and the larger the cows. We assume that all [SOURCE: Page_109]

cows share the same capability of secreting milk fat (νF), milk protein (νP), and milk lactose (νL), [SOURCE: Page_109]

and thus milk composition. The within population variability in ν y, WM, b^0, and νX was simulated [SOURCE: Page_109]

using the standard deviations and correlations reported by Phuong et al. (2015). [SOURCE: Page_109]

Low feeding was regulated using a parameter of dietary energy density (eD) to reduce the [SOURCE: Page_109]

total energy intake of cows, and thus the performance (i.e. milk yield and body weight) to the [SOURCE: Page_109]

level reported in the real data, whereas high feeding implies no limitation of energy intake and [SOURCE: Page_109]

thus allows animals to achieve their genetic potential. Simulated results on conception to first [SOURCE: Page_109]

service (FSC), days from parturition to first service (DFS), days from parturition to conception or [SOURCE: Page_109]

day open (DOP), and calving interval (CI) were then compared with that of data sets. In order to [SOURCE: Page_109]

test the repeatability of the model, each scenario was run 20 times, with 200 cows each. [SOURCE: Page_109]

Calculation of lifetime efficiency [SOURCE: Page_109]

The lifetime efficiency of individual cows in each scenario was calculated using the [SOURCE: Page_109]

following equation: N E Milk LTE =           ×100                                    [6] 1 E Intake [SOURCE: Page_109]

96 [SOURCE: Page_109]

Chapter 5 – Lifetime efficiency [SOURCE: Page_110]

where LTE is lifetime efficiency (%), EMilk is total energy in milk (MJ), EIntake is total energy [SOURCE: Page_110]

intake (MJ), and N is time from birth to culling (day). [SOURCE: Page_110]

cow fed high concentrate diet in the experiment of Cutullic et al. (2011), whereas the low MY and [SOURCE: Page_110]

cows. No comparison in body weight curves could be made due to insufficient reported data, but [SOURCE: Page_110]

the simulated live weights one week postpartum of 678 and 700 for Holstein and Normande [SOURCE: Page_110]

cows, respectively, were comparable with the reported data. More generally, different [SOURCE: Page_110]

populations of cow that differ in their genetic production potential, for instance, high versus low [SOURCE: Page_110]

milk producing cow, could be simulated by scaling the above genetic scaling parameters up and [SOURCE: Page_110]

Effect of genotypes on reproductive performance and lifetime efficiency [SOURCE: Page_111]

The simulated reproductive performance, lifespan, and lifetime efficiency of the two [SOURCE: Page_111]

genotypes of cow in the example of Figure 5.4 are presented in Table 1. The model satisfactorily [SOURCE: Page_111]

predicted conception rate to first service of the Holstein cows (41 versus 38%), but considerably [SOURCE: Page_111]

under-predicted that of the Normande cows (48 versus 58%). In both genotypes, days to first [SOURCE: Page_111]

service, days open, and calving interval were comparably simulated. [SOURCE: Page_111]

High genetic merit for milk production cows (Holstein) have an inferior reproductive [SOURCE: Page_111]

performance than low genetic merit ones (Normande), i.e. lower conception rate, higher days to [SOURCE: Page_111]

first service, days open, calving interval, and shorter lifespan. Despite having worsen [SOURCE: Page_111]

reproductive efficiency and shorter productive longevity high milk producing cows still have [SOURCE: Page_111]

higher lifetime efficiency than the low milk producing cows. The proportion of cows that get [SOURCE: Page_111]

pregnant by days in milk is lower for simulated Holstein compared to Normande cows (Figure [SOURCE: Page_111]

5.5). Table 5.1 also shows a high consistency between the simulations with low values of standard [SOURCE: Page_111]

deviation. [SOURCE: Page_111]

Effect of genotypes and feeding levels on reproductive and lifetime efficiency [SOURCE: Page_112]

Table 5.2 presents the simulated reproductive traits in comparison to their corresponding [SOURCE: Page_112]

records in the Langhill data. The simulated conception rates at first service are comparable to that [SOURCE: Page_112]

of data. Days to first service are on average 10 days longer in simulated results compared to the [SOURCE: Page_112]

data records whereas the simulated days open and calving interval are shorter than that of [SOURCE: Page_112]

database. The patterns of reproductive performance, lifespan, and lifetime efficiency for control [SOURCE: Page_112]

and selected cows are similar to what observed in Table 5.1. Generally, cows selected for maximal [SOURCE: Page_112]

milk production have a lower reproductive performance, and thus a shorter lifespan. However, [SOURCE: Page_112]

lifetime efficiency of individual cows is mainly driven by milk production level rather than [SOURCE: Page_112]

reproductive performance and lifespan, which might explain why simulated LTE of the selected [SOURCE: Page_112]

cows is higher than that of the control animals. No clear effect of levels of concentrate feeding on [SOURCE: Page_112]

reproductive performance could be observed in the simulation. These results are confirmed by [SOURCE: Page_112]

the plots of the simulated proportion of cows getting pregnant of control and selected [SOURCE: Page_112]

populations in high versus low concentrate feedings presented in Figure 5.6. [SOURCE: Page_112]

Discussion [SOURCE: Page_114]

The main purpose of this study is to incorporate the consequences of performance levels on [SOURCE: Page_114]

reproductive success of individual cows into an existing lifetime performance model, and thereby [SOURCE: Page_114]

allow prediction of their lifetime efficiency. Because the major focus of this model is on lifetime [SOURCE: Page_114]

efficiency, for which a key component is productive lifespan, the key reproductive outcome [SOURCE: Page_114]

needed in the model is probability of pregnancy. Thus, instead of modelling all the individual [SOURCE: Page_114]

steps of the reproductive process as do many conventional reproduction models, we focused on [SOURCE: Page_114]

the final product of the reproductive process by assuming a likelihood of conception of a viable [SOURCE: Page_114]

calf, and making this likelihood sensitive to the performance levels, i.e. milk yield, body [SOURCE: Page_114]

condition, and energy balance. This allowed the model to evaluate at each time-step within the [SOURCE: Page_114]

relevant phases of lactation whether, and when the cow gets pregnant, or not. Based on these [SOURCE: Page_114]

reproductive outcomes the model was able to include variation in productive lifespan in the [SOURCE: Page_114]

simulation of lifetime performance and lifetime efficiency. This model development was [SOURCE: Page_114]

evaluated using two datasets with varying performance levels. To simulate different genotypes [SOURCE: Page_114]

of cow genetic scaling parameters are used to create differences in production levels such as milk [SOURCE: Page_114]

101 [SOURCE: Page_114]

Chapter 5 – Lifetime efficiency [SOURCE: Page_115]

yield, growth capacity, mature size, etc. (see Figure 1 and the description of the GARUNS model [SOURCE: Page_115]

section). [SOURCE: Page_115]

Comparison of simulation outputs on reproductive performance with selected data and [SOURCE: Page_115]

literature [SOURCE: Page_115]

Generally, the whole model showed a good capability of consistently simulating [SOURCE: Page_115]

reproductive performance, lifespan, lifetime performance, and lifetime efficiency of various [SOURCE: Page_115]

genotypes of cow across nutritional environments. Apart from the under-simulation of [SOURCE: Page_115]

conception rate to first service of the Normande cows the simulated outputs for other traits were [SOURCE: Page_115]

comparable with the records of Cutullic et al. (2011). The under-prediction of conception rate of [SOURCE: Page_115]

the Normande cows was probably due to the facts that the constant parametersin the present [SOURCE: Page_115]

reproduction sub-model, i.e. RatMY, RatEB, and RatBCS, were derived from the studies where [SOURCE: Page_115]

Holstein cows were used. Indeed, there have been reports of significant innate variation between [SOURCE: Page_115]

breeds for reproductive performance though fed with the same diet (Snijders et al., 2001; Dillon et [SOURCE: Page_115]

al., 2003; Cutullic et al., 2011). [SOURCE: Page_115]

In comparison to the Langhill data (Pryce et al., 1999), the model adequately predicted [SOURCE: Page_115]

conception rate to first service and days to first service but under-predicted days open and [SOURCE: Page_115]

calving interval. The under-predictions of DOP and CI might be caused by the difference in [SOURCE: Page_115]

management of the voluntary waiting period: in this model we set up a voluntary waiting period [SOURCE: Page_115]

of 50 days whereas it is on average 60 days in the Langhill data for both selected and control [SOURCE: Page_115]

cows (Smith; personal communication, 2014). In the United Kingdom, Wills (2012) stated that the [SOURCE: Page_115]

voluntary waiting period of dairy cows is between 60 – 65 days. In particular, the model was able [SOURCE: Page_115]

to reproduce the reproductive performance of cows fed high versus low concentrate, i.e. Pryce et [SOURCE: Page_115]

al. (1999) showed no considerable difference in the reproductive performance between cows fed [SOURCE: Page_115]

high versus low concentrate. Working on the same data at Langhill, but with a division of records [SOURCE: Page_115]

into heifers and cows over two lactations, McGowan et al. (1996), however, reported that low [SOURCE: Page_115]

concentrate feeding heifers had a better reproductive performance but an opposite pattern was [SOURCE: Page_115]

observed on cows. In the data of Pryce et al. (1999) 34% of records were from heifers. Further, the [SOURCE: Page_115]

effect of high versus low concentrate feeding (or dietary energy density) is not consistent in the [SOURCE: Page_115]

literature: there are studies reported a better reproduction of low concentrate compared to high [SOURCE: Page_115]

concentrate feeding cows such as Horan et al. (2005) and Walsh et al. (2008), there are also studies [SOURCE: Page_115]

in opposite side such as Jonsson et al. (1999), Pedernera et al. (2008), and Wall (2008). Cutullic et [SOURCE: Page_115]

al. (2011) showed a lower proportion of cows being inseminated over breeding time when fed [SOURCE: Page_115]

102 [SOURCE: Page_115]

Chapter 5 – Lifetime efficiency [SOURCE: Page_116]

with low energy density diet compared to cow fed control diet. This indicates a necessity of [SOURCE: Page_116]

considering carefully the difference between heifers and cows in their capability to reproduce, [SOURCE: Page_116]

especially in the way they respond to a change in (nutritional) environment. [SOURCE: Page_116]

In the literature, at the similar levels of milk production (≈ 38 kg at peak) and body weight (≈ [SOURCE: Page_116]

600 kg post calving), data from Ireland also show that Holstein Friesian cows fed high [SOURCE: Page_116]

concentrate diet had a first service conception rate that varied from 40 to 45%, an overall [SOURCE: Page_116]

conception rate of around 77%, a calving to first service interval of 73 to 78 days, a calving to [SOURCE: Page_116]

conception interval of 91 to 99 days, and number of services per calf of 2.1 to 2.7 (Snijders et al., [SOURCE: Page_116]

2001; Horan et al., 2005). These values are comparable to the present simulated values. The [SOURCE: Page_116]

simulated lifespans of the Holstein cows in either control or selected lines fed high concentrate [SOURCE: Page_116]

are comparable with reports from the literature, i.e. the simulated lifespan varies from 2137 to [SOURCE: Page_116]

2961 days whereas it is approximately 2200 days for British Holsteins (Pritchard et al., 2013), and [SOURCE: Page_116]

2128 days for Dutch Holsteins (CRV, 2014). In this model we assume that all cows start breeding [SOURCE: Page_116]

at 450 days and because the gestation length is on average 280 days, the productive life (time [SOURCE: Page_116]

from first calving to culling) can therefore be expected to be around 1819 days or 4.4 lactations [SOURCE: Page_116]

(i.e. lifespan/calving interval). This is higher than the reported mean productive lives of Austrian [SOURCE: Page_116]

Holstein cows of 3.3 lactations (Knaus, 2009), British Holstein cows of 1500 days (Pritchard et al., [SOURCE: Page_116]

2013), and that of American Holstein of 3 lactations on average (Hare et al., 2006). Such a longer [SOURCE: Page_116]

lifespan might be explained by the fact that in the present study the lifespan of individual cows is [SOURCE: Page_116]

mainly driven by reproductive performance and a simple culling policy. The effect of diseases, [SOURCE: Page_116]

for example, mastitis, ketosis or fatty liver on survivability of cows is not currently incorporated. [SOURCE: Page_116]

Effect of genotype and feeding system on reproductive performance and lifetime [SOURCE: Page_116]

efficiency of individual cows [SOURCE: Page_116]

The simulation outputs have showed that the genetic selection for greater milk production [SOURCE: Page_116]

decreases reproductive performance. High yielding cows took longer to be inseminated or to get [SOURCE: Page_116]

pregnant and thus have a longer calving interval than the low milk producing individuals. [SOURCE: Page_116]

Though the conception rate to first service was not different between the two genotypes, the [SOURCE: Page_116]

overall conception rate, or the proportion of cows getting pregnant (Figure 5.6), and the lifespan [SOURCE: Page_116]

(Table 5.2) were reduced in the high genetic merit, i.e. selected, compared to the low genetic [SOURCE: Page_116]

merit, i.e. control, cows. These results are consistent with literature comparisons of differences in [SOURCE: Page_116]

genetic merit for milk production (Snijders et al., 2001; Horan et al., 2005; Veerkamp et al., 2003; [SOURCE: Page_116]

Lopez et al., 2004; Yániz et al., 2008). Interestingly, in terms of efficiency, the present version of [SOURCE: Page_116]

103 [SOURCE: Page_116]

Chapter 5 – Lifetime efficiency [SOURCE: Page_117]

the model suggests that such decreases in reproductive performance and lifespan are probably [SOURCE: Page_117]

compensated by the high milk production level which explains the slightly higher lifetime [SOURCE: Page_117]

efficiency of the selected compared to the control cows (47 versus 41%, Table 5.2). In the present [SOURCE: Page_117]

study, we define LTE simply as the ratio of total energy captured in milk over total energy intake [SOURCE: Page_117]

and thus the improvement of LTE relies on the principle of diluting the maintenance requirement [SOURCE: Page_117]

by improving the level of milk production (VandeHaar and St-Pierre, 2006) or the reduction in [SOURCE: Page_117]

the number of unproductive days (Lormore and Galligan, 2001). At the animal level, the milk [SOURCE: Page_117]

production level has a more influence on LTE than the effect of productive life. Blank et al. (2012) [SOURCE: Page_117]

found a strongly positive correlation between lifetime efficiency (kg of milk/cow/day) and milk [SOURCE: Page_117]

yield (R$_2$ = 0.8) for cows of the same lifespan, but no significant correlation between productive [SOURCE: Page_117]

lifespan and lifetime efficiency could be found (R$_2$ not reported). Our result is also in agreement [SOURCE: Page_117]

with the spreadsheet model of Jones (2005) in which he computed the financial impacts of [SOURCE: Page_117]

reducing annual milk production to extend the productive life of dairy cows. The annual returns [SOURCE: Page_117]

were estimated using some specific assumptions on annual milk production, feed consumption, [SOURCE: Page_117]

milk prices, and feed prices. The simulated results showed that the net financial effect of [SOURCE: Page_117]

sacrificing milk production for a longer productive life for dairy cows was generally a loss. [SOURCE: Page_117]

However, neither the study of Jones (2005) nor the present study take into account the other [SOURCE: Page_117]

economic benefits of having a longer productive life such as more female calves available for [SOURCE: Page_117]

replacement or decreased costs of veterinary treatments and artificial insemination, and the [SOURCE: Page_117]

societal concerns on the animal welfare issues, which are especially important if we consider [SOURCE: Page_117]

production efficiency at the farm level. VandeHaar (2014), for example, indicated that the feed [SOURCE: Page_117]

consumed by heifers can account for 15 to 30% of the amount of feed of a cow during her lifetime. [SOURCE: Page_117]

The published estimates of the economic losses of clinical mastitis, for example, range from €61 to [SOURCE: Page_117]

€97 per cow on a farm (Hogeveen et al., 2011). At the farm level, the increased productive life and [SOURCE: Page_117]

consequently longevity will lead to fewer replacement heifers being required to achieve the same [SOURCE: Page_117]

level of output thus reducing the replacement costs; more offspring are produced, and more [SOURCE: Page_117]

intensive selection is possible (Bell et al., 2011). Garnsworthy (2004), using a modelling approach, [SOURCE: Page_117]

reported that improved longevity lowered environmental impact, for example, 27% of methane [SOURCE: Page_117]

produced on farms is expected to be produced by herd replacements and thus by lowering the [SOURCE: Page_117]

number of heifer replacements methane emission would also be mitigated. These points of [SOURCE: Page_117]

discussion indicate the need of further analyzing LTE not only at the individual but also at the [SOURCE: Page_117]

herd levels if we wish to fully evaluate the consequences of future management strategies. [SOURCE: Page_117]

104 [SOURCE: Page_117]

Chapter 5 – Lifetime efficiency [SOURCE: Page_118]

In terms of the effect of feeding system, the simulated outputs show that there were no clear [SOURCE: Page_118]

differences in the reproductive performance, lifespan, and lifetime efficiency between the low [SOURCE: Page_118]

and the high concentrate feeding groups. The plots (not shown) of simulated individual effect of [SOURCE: Page_118]

milk production, body condition score, and energy balance on likelihood of conception, i.e. [SOURCE: Page_118]

LCONCMY, LCONCBCS, and LCONCEB, respectively, of cows in high versus low concentrate [SOURCE: Page_118]

feeding groups indicate that the likelihood of conception in the low feeding group is reduced [SOURCE: Page_118]

through the low body condition score and negative energy balance but this is compensated by [SOURCE: Page_118]

the low milk production, whereas the major negative effect of high feeding on likelihood of [SOURCE: Page_118]

conception is relied on the high production of milk. These results are in agreement with the [SOURCE: Page_118]

conclusion of Cutullic et al. (2011), they concluded that low fed cows, on the one hand, may [SOURCE: Page_118]

suffer from a greater body condition loss and poorer body condition but on the other hand, they [SOURCE: Page_118]

may benefits from a lower milk yield level. [SOURCE: Page_118]

Conclusions [SOURCE: Page_118]

The whole model consistently simulated productive and reproductive performance, lifespan, [SOURCE: Page_118]

and lifetime efficiency of various genotypes of cow across feeding systems. The differences [SOURCE: Page_118]

between simulated and observed data for several traits were mainly due to the discrepancies in [SOURCE: Page_118]

assumed reproductive management. The simulated effect of genotypes on reproductive [SOURCE: Page_118]

performance was in agreement with the selected database. Genetic selection for greater milk [SOURCE: Page_118]

production impaired reproductive performance. Lifetime efficiency, defined simply in terms of [SOURCE: Page_118]

energy ratio, was slightly higher for high genetic merit cows compared to the lower genetic merit [SOURCE: Page_118]

individuals due to the compensation of milk quantity over the longer productive life. The effect [SOURCE: Page_118]

of a poorer nutritional environment on simulated reproductive performance was well modelled [SOURCE: Page_118]

with respect to the selected database. Further works need to be done for carefully [SOURCE: Page_118]

simulating/investigating the effect of a reduced feeding on reproductive performance of heifers [SOURCE: Page_118]

and cows. Economic indicators and other management factors should also be incorporated to [SOURCE: Page_118]

fully and more accurately simulate lifetime profitability in different scenarios. [SOURCE: Page_118]

### 6.1. Introduction

An increasing demand for sustainability of livestock farming systems is driving the need to [SOURCE: Page_124]

investigate novel selection and management strategies to reduce feed costs and to mitigate the [SOURCE: Page_124]

environmental impact of milk production, which can only be met through improved feed [SOURCE: Page_124]

efficiency (Berry and Crowley, 2013). The milk production efficiency of a dairy herd is the sum of [SOURCE: Page_124]

the individual female’s lifetime efficiency which depends upon the length of their productive [SOURCE: Page_124]

lives and the quantity of milk produced and feed intake during that period (Britt et al., 2003; [SOURCE: Page_124]

Sawa et al., 2013). A striking characteristic of the individual cow efficiency is its variability which [SOURCE: Page_124]

is in large part due to the animal differences in production potential and nutrient partitioning as [SOURCE: Page_124]

the consequence of selection and management (Bauman et al., 1985; Friggens and Newbold, [SOURCE: Page_124]

2007). In this context, understanding the biological basis for individual variability is central to [SOURCE: Page_124]

exploring the ability of the whole system to adapt to a changing environment. Because the on- [SOURCE: Page_124]

farm assessment of lifetime efficiency remains difficult as it requires a lot of information to be [SOURCE: Page_124]

recorded in a long period (Manafiazar et al., 2012), a modelling approach might offer an [SOURCE: Page_124]

alternative for investigating influences on lifetime efficiency of possible selection and [SOURCE: Page_124]

management strategies. Compared to other species, this is of special importance to the case of [SOURCE: Page_124]

dairy cows where wide variations can exist and where we have to meet the future challenges of [SOURCE: Page_124]

profitability and sustainability. This Ph.D. thesis was part of a Marie Curie Initial Training [SOURCE: Page_124]

Network, GreenhouseMilk, with the overall ambitions to better understand the role of dairy [SOURCE: Page_124]

genetics for nutrient partitioning and energy efficiency in the overall greenhouse gas output of [SOURCE: Page_124]

dairy systems, to provide tools to assist farmers select “efficient and environmentally friendly” [SOURCE: Page_124]

cows that suit their systems, and to particularly consider the role of genetics in the whole farming [SOURCE: Page_124]

system including feeding strategy and management policies such as housing period, fertilization [SOURCE: Page_124]

111 [SOURCE: Page_124]

Chapter 6 – Discussion [SOURCE: Page_125]

and manure management. As a contribution to such ambitions of the GreenhouseMilk project, to [SOURCE: Page_125]

reiterate, the work of the present thesis was aimed to ultimately develop a model for simulating [SOURCE: Page_125]

the consequences of different breeding and feeding strategies on the lifetime efficiency of [SOURCE: Page_125]

individual cows. To reach this general objective, three key research questions were identified and [SOURCE: Page_125]

were successively explored in Chapter 3, Chapter 4, and Chapter 5: [SOURCE: Page_125]

 Q$_1$. What does an improvement in energy efficiency relate to nitrogen efficiency? [SOURCE: Page_125]

 Q$_2$. How to represent genetic variability in production potentials of individual cows in a lifetime [SOURCE: Page_125]

nutrient partitioning model? [SOURCE: Page_125]

 Q$_3$. How to represent the effect of performance and nutritional environment on reproduction and [SOURCE: Page_125]

productive lifespan? [SOURCE: Page_125]

In Chapter 3 it was shown that energy and nitrogen efficiency, which are the two common [SOURCE: Page_125]

indicators of feed efficiency in the literature, were significantly and positively correlated. Energy [SOURCE: Page_125]

efficiency, nitrogen efficiency, and their relationship were affected by the common feed factors of [SOURCE: Page_125]

acid detergent fibre (ADF), digestible energy (DE), and proportion of concentrate (PCO). These [SOURCE: Page_125]

results indicated that, at the animal level fed the same diet, an improvement of lifetime energy [SOURCE: Page_125]

efficiency would also lead to an improvement of lifetime nitrogen efficiency. Consequently, they [SOURCE: Page_125]

supported our decision that we can initially build one model that can largely account for both [SOURCE: Page_125]

predicting energy efficiency, and considering nitrogen efficiency as an add-on. Thus, in Chapter 4 [SOURCE: Page_125]

an existing lifetime nutrient partitioning model was investigated for its capability of simulating [SOURCE: Page_125]

variability in genetic production potential of individual cows, using an external dataset. The [SOURCE: Page_125]

results showed that the model could satisfactorily simulate genetic variability in production [SOURCE: Page_125]

potential across a range of dairy breeds, and thus could be used for studying the effect of [SOURCE: Page_125]

selection and management strategies on the long-term efficiency. The starting distributions of the [SOURCE: Page_125]

model genetic scaling parameters which are prerequisites for a simulation study were also [SOURCE: Page_125]

explored. Chapter 5, with inclusions of findings from Chapter 4, successfully incorporated the [SOURCE: Page_125]

possible consequences of varying performance levels such as milk yield, body condition, and [SOURCE: Page_125]

energy balance on reproductive efficiency, and thus allowed simulating lifetime efficiency of [SOURCE: Page_125]

individual dairy cows under various selected scenarios. The whole model consistently simulated [SOURCE: Page_125]

productive and reproductive performance, lifespan, and lifetime efficiency of various genotypes [SOURCE: Page_125]

of cow across feeding systems. On a control feeding, the effect of genotypes on reproductive [SOURCE: Page_125]

performance is logical with the selected database. Genetic selection for a greater milk production [SOURCE: Page_125]

impairs reproductive performance. Genetic selection for a greater milk production impaired [SOURCE: Page_125]

112 [SOURCE: Page_125]

Chapter 6 – Discussion [SOURCE: Page_126]

reproductive performance. Lifetime efficiency, defined simply in terms of energy ratio, was [SOURCE: Page_126]

slightly, but not significantly, higher for selected cows compared to control cows due to the [SOURCE: Page_126]

compensation of milk quantity over the longer productive life. The effect of a poorer nutritional [SOURCE: Page_126]

environment on simulated reproductive performance was well accommodated with respect to [SOURCE: Page_126]

the selected database. [SOURCE: Page_126]

The intention of this chapter is to provide a general synthesis of the main points from the [SOURCE: Page_126]

previous chapters in the context of future dairy industry to meet the challenges of changing [SOURCE: Page_126]

environments while keeping profitability and sustainability. In addition to the achievements, the [SOURCE: Page_126]

possible shortcomings will also be addressed. In the first part the importance of considering both [SOURCE: Page_126]

energy and nitrogen efficiency in the context of modelling lifetime efficiency is discussed. The [SOURCE: Page_126]

second part addresses the challenges of being able to incorporating individual genetic variability [SOURCE: Page_126]

in production potential into a nutritional model as this is one of the common limitations of many [SOURCE: Page_126]

dairy production models. We then discuss about the possible trade-offs between short-term and [SOURCE: Page_126]

long-term efficiency under different breeding and feeding strategies, taking into account the [SOURCE: Page_126]

interaction between genotype by environment. The chapter is ended with general conclusions [SOURCE: Page_126]

from this thesis. [SOURCE: Page_126]
